AI Infra Q76 of 200
Why does PCIe bandwidth matter for AI servers, and how does PCIe Gen5 (128 GB/s x16) compare to previous generations?
A) PCIe bandwidth only affects gaming graphics — AI workloads run entirely inside GPU memory and never need PCIe communication after initial model loading
B) PCIe bandwidth connects GPUs to NICs and NVMe storage. When a GPU needs data from the network (distributed training gradient sync) or storage (checkpoint loading), PCIe is the data highway. PCIe Gen5 (128 GB/s x16) is 2× faster than Gen4 (64 GB/s) and 4× faster than Gen3 (32 GB/s). Insufficient PCIe bandwidth bottlenecks data flow to the GPU, leaving Tensor Cores idle — especially critical for multi-node training and high-speed storage
C) PCIe Gen5 provides identical performance to PCIe Gen3 for AI workloads — the higher numbers are theoretical and have no practical impact on training or inference throughput
D) PCIe is being replaced by NVLink for all GPU communication — modern AI servers don't use PCIe at all; they use NVLink fabrics for GPU-GPU, GPU-NIC, and GPU-storage connections
✅ Correct Answer: B

PCIe is the data highway connecting GPUs to the outside world. NVLink handles GPU-GPU communication (900 GB/s), but GPU-NIC and GPU-storage communication goes through PCIe. For distributed training across nodes: gradients from GPU must travel GPU → PCIe → NIC → network → remote node. If PCIe is Gen3 (32 GB/s), a 400Gb/s InfiniBand NIC is bottlenecked — the NIC can receive 50 GB/s but PCIe can only deliver 32 GB/s to the GPU. GPUDirect RDMA optimizes this path but still uses PCIe. For storage: checkpoint loading/saving at 7 GB/s NVMe speed needs PCIe Gen4 or better to avoid bottlenecking.

PCIe — The Data Highway
  PCIe GENERATION COMPARISON
  ┌────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Generation │ x16 Bandwidth│ Impact on AI                         │
  ├────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Gen3       │ ~32 GB/s     │ Bottlenecks 100Gb+ NICs and NVMe     │
  │ Gen4       │ ~64 GB/s     │ Adequate for 200Gb networking        │
  │ Gen5       │ ~128 GB/s    │ Required for 400Gb InfiniBand NDR    │
  └────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
  GPU ←PCIe→ NIC (network)    GPU ←PCIe→ NVMe (storage)
  Bottleneck example: 400Gb/s NIC (50GB/s) on PCIe Gen3 (32GB/s) 
  → 36% of network bandwidth WASTED
AI Infra Q77 of 200
What local storage configuration is optimal for an AI training server, and why does it matter?
A) A single large HDD (16TB+) — AI training data is large, and HDDs provide the best cost-per-terabyte for bulk storage of training datasets
B) No local storage needed — AI training servers should be diskless, booting from the network and accessing all data from a centralized NAS/SAN storage array
C) Standard SATA SSDs are sufficient — the storage speed doesn't matter because training data is loaded once into GPU memory and never accessed from disk again
D) Multiple high-speed NVMe SSDs (3.84-7.68TB each, 7 GB/s read) configured for high throughput. AI training needs fast storage for: checkpoint saving/loading (500GB checkpoint at 7 GB/s = ~70 sec vs 500+ sec on SATA), dataset caching (keep frequently used data locally), and container images. GPUDirect Storage enables direct NVMe→GPU transfers bypassing CPU
✅ Correct Answer: D

Storage is the most overlooked component in AI server design. Checkpoint I/O is the critical path — if a 500GB checkpoint takes 10 minutes to save (SATA SSD at 500 MB/s), that's 10 minutes of idle GPUs costing $100+/hour. NVMe at 7 GB/s saves it in ~70 seconds — 8× faster. GPUDirect Storage (GDS) enables a direct DMA path: NVMe → GPU memory, completely bypassing CPU and system memory. This is critical for training on datasets larger than GPU memory. DGX H100 includes 8× 3.84TB NVMe drives providing ~30TB of fast local storage.

Storage Performance — Why NVMe Matters
  CHECKPOINT SAVE TIME (500GB)
  ┌────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Storage Type   │ Speed        │ 500GB Checkpoint Time           │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ HDD            │ 200 MB/s     │ ~42 minutes ⛔                   │
  │ SATA SSD       │ 500 MB/s     │ ~17 minutes ⚠️                   │
  │ NVMe Gen3      │ 3,500 MB/s   │ ~2.4 minutes                    │
  │ NVMe Gen4      │ 7,000 MB/s   │ ~71 seconds ✅                  │
  └────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘
  8× GPUs idle at $100/hr = $1.67/min. 17 min checkpoint = $28 wasted.
  71 sec checkpoint = $2 wasted. NVMe pays for itself in days.
AI Infra Q78 of 200
What is GPU power delivery, and why do modern GPUs require specialized power connectors like 12VHPWR?
A) Modern GPUs draw 350-700W each. Standard PCIe slot provides only 75W, and traditional 8-pin connectors provide 150W each. The 12VHPWR (12+4 pin) connector delivers up to 600W through a single cable — reducing cable clutter for 8-GPU servers. SXM GPUs bypass connectors entirely — power is delivered through the mezzanine baseboard. Incorrect power delivery causes GPU throttling, instability, or system shutdown under load
B) Power connectors are standardized and interchangeable — any 8-pin cable can deliver unlimited power to any GPU regardless of its power rating or generation
C) GPUs are self-powered — they draw power directly from the PCIe slot and don't need external power connectors; the 12VHPWR connector is for monitoring only
D) Power delivery only matters for overclocking — at stock speeds, GPUs draw well under 100W, making power connectors optional for enterprise AI deployments
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU power delivery is one of the most critical and overlooked aspects of AI server design. An H100 SXM draws up to 700W. For an 8-GPU server, that's 5,600W just for GPUs — more than most household circuits. The power path: facility power (480V 3-phase) → PDU → server PSU (3,000W+ redundant) → power distribution board → GPU connectors. PCIe slot: 75W max. 8-pin PCIe: 150W. 12VHPWR (12+4): 600W through one cable. SXM: up to 700W through mezzanine connector. For PCIe H100 (350W), two 8-pin connectors work. For SXM H100 (700W), only the mezzanine baseboard can deliver this — you CANNOT power SXM GPUs through cables.

GPU Power Delivery
  POWER CONNECTOR CAPACITY
  ┌────────────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Connector Type     │ Max Power   │ Used For                      │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ PCIe Slot          │ 75W         │ All GPUs (base power)         │
  │ 8-pin PCIe         │ 150W        │ PCIe GPUs (L4, L40S, A100)   │
  │ 12VHPWR (12+4 pin) │ 600W        │ PCIe H100/H200                │
  │ SXM Mezzanine      │ 700W+       │ SXM H100/H200/B200            │
  └────────────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘
  8× H100 SXM Server: 8 × 700W = 5,600W GPUs + ~500W system = 6,100W total
  Requires: 3,000W+ redundant PSUs, 480V 3-phase input

2.2 Scaling GPU InfrastructureQ79-Q82

AI Infra Q79 of 200
What is NVLink and NVSwitch, and why are they essential for scaling beyond a single GPU?
A) NVLink is a software protocol for sharing GPU memory over Ethernet — NVSwitch is the management GUI for configuring NVLink connections
B) NVLink is a high-speed GPU interconnect (900 GB/s on H100) that directly connects GPU memory spaces — allowing GPUs to access each other's memory as if it were local. NVSwitch connects ALL GPUs in a server to each other simultaneously (all-to-all at full bandwidth), eliminating the need for GPU-to-GPU routing. Together they enable model parallelism — splitting models across GPUs with minimal communication overhead
C) NVLink is an alternative to CUDA — it's a competing GPU programming platform that some applications use instead of CUDA for better multi-GPU performance
D) NVLink is the physical power connector for SXM GPUs — NVSwitch is the power distribution board that routes electricity to each GPU in a DGX system
✅ Correct Answer: B

NVLink is what makes multi-GPU training practical. Without it, GPU-GPU communication goes through PCIe (64 GB/s shared across all GPUs). With NVLink 4.0, each GPU has a direct 900 GB/s link. NVSwitch takes this further — all 8 GPUs in a DGX H100 connect through NVSwitch, enabling any GPU to talk to any other GPU at full 900 GB/s SIMULTANEOUSLY. This all-to-all topology is critical for tensor parallelism where every GPU communicates with every other GPU during each forward/backward pass. Performance impact: training GPT-3 scale models with PCIe-only GPUs is 10-50× slower than NVLink-connected GPUs — effectively impossible for large models.

NVLink + NVSwitch — GPU Interconnect
  PCIe (WITHOUT NVLink)              NVSwitch (WITH NVLink)
  ┌────┬────┬────┬────┐             ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │GPU0│GPU1│GPU2│GPU3│             │        NVSwitch              │
  └──┬──┴──┬──┴──┬──┴──┬──┘         │    ┌───┬───┬───┬───┐        │
     └─── PCIe Switch ───┘          │    │G0 │G1 │G2 │G3 │        │
     64 GB/s SHARED                 │    └─┬─┴─┬─┴─┬─┴─┬─┘        │
     GPU0→GPU3: slow (shared bus)   │      └───┴───┴───┘          │
                                    │    ALL-TO-ALL 900 GB/s      │
                                    │    SIMULTANEOUS              │
                                    └──────────────────────────────┘
  NVLink Domain: Up to 256 GPUs connected via NVSwitch + InfiniBand
AI Infra Q80 of 200
What is the difference between scale-up (bigger server) and scale-out (more servers) for GPU infrastructure?
A) Scale-up and scale-out are identical strategies — they both mean adding more GPUs, and the only difference is whether those GPUs are in the same rack or different racks
B) Scale-up means adding more CPU cores to a server; scale-out means adding more GPUs — the terms refer to CPU and GPU scaling respectively
C) Scale-up: add more GPUs to a single server (e.g., 4→8 GPUs) connected via NVLink/NVSwitch. Best for model parallelism where GPUs need ultra-fast communication. Limited by physical server constraints (power, cooling, PCIe lanes). Scale-out: add more servers (e.g., 1→16 nodes) connected via InfiniBand/RoCE. Best for data parallelism where communication is less frequent. Modern large-scale training combines both: scale-up within nodes (NVLink) + scale-out across nodes (InfiniBand)
D) Scale-up is always better — putting all GPUs in one server with NVLink eliminates network latency, making scale-out architectures obsolete
✅ Correct Answer: C

Scale-up vs scale-out is the fundamental architecture decision for GPU clusters. Scale-up: within a single node, GPUs communicate via NVLink (900 GB/s, nanoseconds latency). Maximum practical scale: 8 GPUs (DGX) or 32 GPUs (DGX SuperPOD node group). Scale-out: across nodes, GPUs communicate via network (InfiniBand at 400 GB/s, microseconds latency). Can scale to thousands of GPUs. The hybrid approach uses scale-up for tensor parallelism (within NVLink domain) and scale-out for data/pipeline parallelism (across InfiniBand fabric). This is called "3D parallelism" and is how GPT-4 sized models are trained across 10,000+ GPUs.

Scale-Up vs Scale-Out
  SCALE-UP (within node)              SCALE-OUT (across nodes)
  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ DGX H100                 │       │ Node 0 ←──InfiniBand──→ Node 1│
  │ ┌───┬───┬───┬───┐        │       │ ┌───┐              ┌───┐     │
  │ │G0 │G1 │G2 │G3 │        │       │ │8GP│              │8GP│     │
  │ ├───┼───┼───┼───┤ NVLink │       │ └───┘              └───┘     │
  │ │G4 │G5 │G6 │G7 │ 900GB/s│       │ 400GB/s, μs latency          │
  │ └───┴───┴───┴───┘        │       │                               │
  │ 8 GPUs, 900 GB/s        │       │ Scale: 1000s of GPUs          │
  │ nanosecond latency       │       │ Best for: Data Parallelism    │
  └──────────────────────────┘       └──────────────────────────────┘
  HYBRID: Tensor Parallelism (NVLink within node) + Data Parallelism (IB across nodes)
AI Infra Q81 of 200
How do you size a GPU cluster — determining how many GPUs you need for a specific AI workload?
A) Always buy the maximum number of GPUs your budget allows — more GPUs always means better AI results, and unused capacity can be repurposed later
B) Use the rule of thumb: 1 GPU per 1 billion model parameters — a 70B model needs exactly 70 GPUs with no other considerations
C) GPU count doesn't matter for training — a single GPU will eventually finish any training job; the cluster size only affects convenience, not feasibility
D) Cluster sizing requires calculating: model memory footprint (can it fit on N GPUs with model parallelism?), target training time (how many GPUs to hit the deadline?), and scaling efficiency (what % of linear speedup does your parallelism strategy achieve?). Key formula: Training Time = (Single GPU time) / (N × Scaling Efficiency). Example: 30 days on 1 GPU, 90% efficiency → 4.2 days on 8 GPUs
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU cluster sizing is a mathematical exercise, not guesswork. Three constraints: Memory — model + optimizer + activations must fit across GPUs. For a 70B model with Adam optimizer: ~600GB minimum → at least 8× H100 (80GB each). Time — if the business needs results in 1 week and 1 GPU takes 56 days, you need at least 8 GPUs (assuming linear scaling). Efficiency — scaling is never perfectly linear. 8 GPUs might deliver 7.2× speedup (90% efficiency). 64 GPUs might deliver 45× (70% efficiency) due to communication overhead. The optimal cluster size balances all three constraints.

GPU Cluster Sizing Formula
  TRAINING TIME = SINGLE_GPU_TIME / (GPU_COUNT × SCALING_EFFICIENCY)
  
  EXAMPLE: 70B LLM training
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Single GPU time: 240 days (estimated)                        │
  │ Target: 30 days                                              │
  │ Minimum GPUs: 240/30 = 8 (assuming 100% efficiency)          │
  │ Actual GPUs (90% efficiency): 240/(30×0.9) = 8.9 → 16 GPUs  │
  │ Memory check: 600GB needed ÷ 80GB/GPU = 8 GPUs minimum ✅    │
  │ RECOMMENDATION: 16× H100 (2 DGX nodes)                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
AI Infra Q82 of 200
How is an AI cluster rack designed differently from a traditional enterprise server rack?
A) AI racks are power-dense (40-80kW vs 5-10kW for enterprise), require 480V 3-phase power, use liquid cooling, minimize cable lengths for InfiniBand (copper DAC for <3m, fiber for longer), and group GPUs into NVLink domains within the same rack/top-of-rack switch. Traditional enterprise racks spread servers across racks; AI clusters pack maximum compute into minimum space for low-latency GPU-GPU communication
B) AI racks are identical to enterprise racks — servers are servers; the only difference is that AI racks have "GPU" in the server name
C) AI racks are designed to maximize airflow using standard 120V power — GPUs are energy-efficient and don't require special power or cooling consideration
D) AI racks distribute GPUs across as many racks as possible — spreading GPUs out improves cooling efficiency and makes maintenance easier
✅ Correct Answer: A

AI racks are a different species from enterprise racks. Power: 40-80kW per rack (4-8 DGX H100 systems at 10kW each) vs 5-10kW for traditional. This requires 480V 3-phase power distribution. Cooling: liquid cooling mandatory above ~30kW/rack. Networking: AI racks use "rail-optimized" design — all GPU 0s connect to switch 0, GPU 1s to switch 1, etc. — minimizing latency for NCCL collectives. Copper Direct-Attach Copper (DAC) cables for intra-rack (passive, low latency, <3m), fiber for inter-rack. Density: keep GPUs that communicate most (NVLink domain) in the same rack to minimize inter-rack traffic.

AI Rack Design
  ENTERPRISE RACK (5-10kW)            AI RACK (40-80kW)
  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Mix of servers         │         │ 4× DGX H100 (8 GPUs each)   │
  │ 120V/208V power        │         │ 480V 3-phase power           │
  │ Air cooling            │         │ Liquid cooling (direct-chip) │
  │ 1-2 ToR switches       │         │ 8× InfiniBand leaf switches   │
  │ Any server anywhere    │         │ Rail-optimized GPU placement │
  └────────────────────────┘         └──────────────────────────────┘
  POWER: 1 AI rack = 8-16 enterprise racks

2.3 Power & Cooling for AI Data CentersQ83-Q86

AI Infra Q83 of 200
An 8× H100 SXM server draws approximately 6,100W under full load. How does this power requirement compare to traditional servers and what infrastructure changes does it demand?
A) 6,100W is a standard server power draw — most enterprise servers draw 5-8kW and can be plugged into standard 120V outlets without modification
B) 6,100W is 10-20× a typical server (300-600W). This requires: 480V 3-phase power (not 120V/208V standard), 60A+ circuits per server, high-capacity PDUs, and facility power infrastructure upgrades. A rack of 4 DGX systems draws 24-32kW — more than many small buildings' entire electrical service
C) Power requirements are artificially inflated by marketing — real-world AI workloads rarely exceed 30% GPU utilization, so actual power draw is typically 1,500-2,000W
D) GPU servers are more power-efficient than CPU servers — the 6,100W number is theoretical peak that's never reached in practice because GPUs automatically throttle to stay under 2,000W
✅ Correct Answer: B

AI server power is transformative for data center design. A single DGX H100 (10.2kW peak system power) draws more than an entire rack of traditional servers. This cascades through the entire power infrastructure: utility feed must be upgraded (medium-voltage 13.8kV → step-down transformer → 480V distribution), UPS systems must be sized for megawatt-scale loads (battery rooms, not rack-mount UPS), generator backup must handle the full AI load (diesel generators in the 2MW+ range), and power distribution uses busway systems (not individual rack PDUs). Air cooling maxes out at ~30kW/rack — liquid cooling becomes mandatory for AI density.

Power Requirements — AI vs Traditional
  POWER COMPARISON
  ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Equipment            │ Power Draw     │ Relative                 │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Standard 1U Server   │ 300-600W       │ 1× (baseline)            │
  │ 4-GPU Server (L40S)  │ 2,000-3,000W   │ 5-10×                    │
  │ 8-GPU Server (A100)  │ 4,000-6,500W   │ 10-20×                   │
  │ DGX H100             │ 10,200W (peak) │ 20-30×                   │
  │ Rack: 4× DGX         │ 32,000-40,000W │ 80-100× per rack         │
  └──────────────────────┴────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘
  H100: 700W SXM | A100: 400W SXM | L40S: 350W PCIe
AI Infra Q84 of 200
When does air cooling become insufficient for GPU servers, and what liquid cooling options exist?
A) Air cooling is always sufficient — modern fans can cool any GPU server regardless of power density; liquid cooling is unnecessary marketing hype
B) Liquid cooling is required for ALL GPU servers regardless of power — even a single L4 GPU needs direct-to-chip liquid cooling to function
C) Air cooling maxes out at ~30kW per rack. Above that, liquid cooling is required. Direct-to-chip: cold plates on GPU/CPU, removes 70-80% of heat via liquid, remainder via air. Immersion: entire server submerged in dielectric fluid — removes 100% of heat, enables 100kW+ per rack. Most AI deployments use direct-to-chip as the cost-effective middle ground
D) The only liquid cooling option is immersion — direct-to-chip is an experimental technology not yet available for production deployment
✅ Correct Answer: C

Cooling is the gating factor for AI data center density. Air cooling: fans move air across heatsinks. Limit: ~30kW/rack (beyond this, you can't move enough air without jet-engine noise levels). Direct-to-chip liquid: cold plates mounted on GPU/CPU die. Coolant (water or dielectric) circulates through cold plates, removing 70-80% of heat. Remaining 20-30% is removed by reduced-speed fans. Enables 50-80kW/rack. This is what most DGX deployments use. Immersion: server fully submerged in dielectric fluid. 100% heat removal via liquid. Enables 100kW+/rack — but requires specialized tanks, fluid management, and service procedures (can't hot-swap a GPU in a tank full of fluid).

Cooling Comparison
  AIR COOLING              DIRECT-TO-CHIP            IMMERSION
  ┌────────────────┐      ┌────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Fans + Heatsink │      │ Cold Plates     │       │ Server in Tank    │
  │ 100% heat → air │      │ 70-80% → liquid │       │ 100% → fluid     │
  │ Max ~30kW/rack  │      │ 20-30% → air    │       │ Max 100kW+/rack  │
  │ Lowest cost     │      │ Moderate cost   │       │ Highest cost     │
  │ Least complex   │      │ Retrofittable   │       │ New facility     │
  └────────────────┘      └────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘
  PUE: ~1.4              PUE: ~1.1                PUE: ~1.03
AI Infra Q85 of 200
What is PUE (Power Usage Effectiveness), and what PUE should you target for an AI data center?
A) PUE measures GPU performance per watt — higher PUE means more energy-efficient GPUs; the target is PUE > 2.0 for AI workloads
B) PUE is irrelevant for AI data centers — it only applies to traditional enterprise data centers running CPU workloads
C) PUE should be exactly 1.0 — any value above 1.0 indicates the data center is wasting energy and should be immediately shut down for remediation
D) PUE = Total Facility Power / IT Equipment Power. PUE of 1.0 means 100% of power goes to IT (perfect — no cooling/lighting/UPS losses). Traditional DC: 1.5-2.0 (50-100% overhead). AI DC with liquid cooling: 1.05-1.15. The goal is to minimize overhead — every watt spent on cooling is a watt NOT spent on GPUs
✅ Correct Answer: D

PUE measures data center efficiency. For an AI DC drawing 10MW: PUE 1.5 = 6.67MW to IT, 3.33MW overhead (cooling, UPS losses, lighting). PUE 1.1 = 9.09MW to IT, 0.91MW overhead. That 2.42MW difference could power ~40 more DGX H100 systems. Liquid cooling dramatically improves PUE because it's more efficient than air — liquid conducts heat 25× better than air, requiring less energy for the same heat removal. Hyperscale AI DCs (Meta, Microsoft, NVIDIA) target PUE 1.05-1.10 using direct-to-chip liquid cooling and free air cooling (using outside air when temperatures permit).

PUE — Measure of Data Center Efficiency
  PUE = TOTAL FACILITY POWER / IT EQUIPMENT POWER
  
  ┌──────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ PUE  │ Interpretation                                          │
  ├──────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1.0  │ Perfect — zero overhead (theoretical)                   │
  │ 1.1  │ Excellent — liquid cooling, free air (AI DC best)       │
  │ 1.4  │ Good — efficient air cooling (modern enterprise)        │
  │ 1.8  │ Average — traditional data center                       │
  │ 2.0+ │ Poor — old facility, inefficient cooling                │
  └──────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Example: 1MW IT load, PUE 1.6 → 1.6MW total (600kW overhead)
  Same IT load, PUE 1.1 → 1.1MW total (100kW overhead)
  Annual savings at $0.10/kWh: 500kW × 8760hrs × $0.10 = $438,000
AI Infra Q86 of 200
What is TDP (Thermal Design Power), and how should it influence GPU infrastructure planning?
A) TDP is the maximum sustained power a GPU is designed to dissipate as heat under realistic workloads. H100 SXM TDP = 700W — the cooling system must be able to remove 700W of heat per GPU continuously. TDP determines: cooling capacity (kW of heat removal needed), power supply sizing (PSUs must exceed total TDP + headroom), and facility power budget (rack TDP × rack count = facility kW requirement)
B) TDP is a marketing number with no engineering relevance — actual GPU power draw is typically 30-50% of TDP and cooling systems designed for TDP are grossly oversized
C) TDP only applies to CPUs — NVIDIA GPUs use a different metric called "Tensor Power Rating" (TPR) that replaces TDP entirely
D) TDP is the maximum theoretical power a GPU could draw if deliberately overclocked — it's 2-3× higher than normal operating power and should be ignored for infrastructure planning
✅ Correct Answer: A
TDP — Drives All Infrastructure Sizing
  GPU TDP: H100 SXM=700W | H100 PCIe=350W | A100 SXM=400W | L40S=350W | L4=72W
  8×H100 Server: 5,600W GPU + 500W sys = 6,100W → Cooling must remove 6.1kW
  TDP × GPU count = Cooling kW needed. Undersize → thermal throttling.

TDP is THE number that drives cooling and power infrastructure design. Under sustained AI training, GPUs run at or near TDP for days or weeks — this is not a theoretical peak, it's the steady-state operating point. Cooling design must handle: TDP × GPU count × simultaneous load factor (usually 1.0 for training — all GPUs at full load). For 8× H100 SXM: 8 × 700W = 5,600W GPU TDP + ~500W system = 6,100W total. Cooling system must remove 6.1kW of heat from this server continuously. For a rack of 4: 24.4kW cooling capacity needed. Undersizing cooling based on "typical" rather than TDP leads to thermal throttling — GPUs reduce clock speed to protect themselves, silently destroying training performance.

2.4 On-Premises vs Cloud AI InfrastructureQ87-Q90

AI Infra Q87 of 200
A startup needs GPU compute for AI model training. They have a $50K budget. Should they build on-premises or use cloud?
A) On-premises — buying a server with 4× A100 GPUs (~$80K) will pay for itself within 6 months compared to cloud GPU rental costs
B) Cloud — $50K doesn't cover even one DGX ($300K+). Cloud provides OPEX model: $50K buys ~500 hours of 8× H100 ($100/hr) or ~5,000 hours of 4× A100 ($10/hr). For a startup, cloud eliminates upfront capital, provides access to latest GPUs, enables elastic scaling, and avoids data center facility costs (power, cooling, networking, staffing)
C) Split the difference — buy one GPU for prototyping on-premises and use cloud for large training jobs; the hybrid approach always delivers the best economics
D) Neither — the startup should use free cloud tiers and CPU-only training; GPUs are unnecessary for AI and the $50K should be spent on data labeling
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cloud vs on-premises is fundamentally a CAPEX vs OPEX decision. On-premises: high upfront capital ($300K+ for DGX, plus data center infrastructure — power, cooling, networking, physical security, staffing — easily $500K+ total). Best when: sustained 24/7 utilization, data sovereignty requirements, predictable workloads, 3-5 year commitment. Cloud: zero upfront cost, pay-per-hour, elastic scaling. Best when: variable workloads, early-stage (experimentation), need latest GPUs without hardware refresh, limited capital budget. For a $50K startup: cloud is the only viable option. The break-even for on-premises typically occurs at 70-80% sustained utilization over 3 years.

Cloud vs On-Premises Economics
  BREAK-EVEN ANALYSIS (8× H100 equivalent)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Cloud: $100/hr × 24/7 = $72,000/month (100% utilization)        │
  │        $100/hr × 8hrs/day = $24,000/month (33% utilization)     │
  │                                                                  │
  │ On-Prem: $300K DGX + $200K infra = $500K upfront                │
  │          Amortized over 3 years = $13,900/month                  │
  │          + power/cooling/staff ~$5K/month = $18,900/month       │
  │                                                                  │
  │ BREAK-EVEN: ~27% utilization (6.5 hrs/day) over 3 years          │
  │ Below 27%: Cloud cheaper   |   Above 27%: On-Prem cheaper       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
AI Infra Q88 of 200
What is Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) for GPU infrastructure, and what costs are commonly overlooked?
A) TCO is simply the purchase price of the GPU server — ongoing costs like electricity and cooling are negligible compared to the hardware cost
B) TCO only matters for cloud deployments — on-premises infrastructure has fixed costs that don't vary with usage, making TCO analysis unnecessary
C) TCO includes: hardware purchase/lease, power (at $0.10/kWh, 10kW server = $8,760/year), cooling (liquid cooling infrastructure + ongoing costs), networking (InfiniBand switches at $50K+ each), data center space ($/kW/month), staffing (AI infrastructure engineers), software licenses (NVAIE at $4,500/GPU/year), and maintenance/support. Power and cooling often exceed hardware costs over a 3-year lifespan
D) TCO is NVIDIA's proprietary pricing model for DGX systems — it's not a general infrastructure concept
✅ Correct Answer: C
TCO — Hardware Is Only ~44%
  DGX H100 3-YEAR TCO: Hardware $300K | Power $27K | Cooling $45K | Net $50K | Software $108K | Staff $150K
  TOTAL ~$680K. Monthly equivalent: ~$18,900. Break-even vs cloud at ~27% utilization.

TCO analysis prevents "sticker shock" — the purchase price of a DGX H100 ($300K+) is only ~40% of the 3-year total cost. Power: 10.2kW × 8,760 hrs/year × $0.10/kWh = $8,935/year × 3 = $26,805. Cooling: liquid cooling infrastructure adds ~$15K-30K/year per rack. Networking: InfiniBand switches ($50K-100K each), cables, transceivers — easily $50K+ per node. Staffing: AI infrastructure engineers command $150K-250K salaries. Software: NVAIE at $4,500/GPU/year × 8 GPUs × 3 years = $108,000. Total 3-year TCO for one DGX: $300K (HW) + $27K (power) + $60K (cooling/net) + $108K (software) = ~$500K+. The hardware is often NOT the largest cost.

AI Infra Q89 of 200
What are the major cloud GPU options from AWS, Azure, and GCP, and how do you choose between them?
A) All cloud providers offer identical GPU instances — the choice doesn't matter; pick the cheapest per-hour rate regardless of GPU type or networking
B) GCP is the only cloud provider with NVIDIA GPUs — AWS and Azure use AMD and Intel GPUs which are incompatible with CUDA
C) Cloud GPU selection is purely about the GPU model — the surrounding infrastructure (networking, storage, CPU) is identical across all instance types
D) AWS: P5 (8× H100, EFA 3,200Gb/s), P4d (8× A100, EFA 400Gb/s), G5 (1-8× A10G), G6 (1-8× L4). Azure: ND H100 v5 (8× H100, InfiniBand), ND A100 v4 (8× A100). GCP: A3 (8× H100, GPUDirect-TCPX). Selection criteria: GPU model, GPU interconnect (NVLink vs PCIe), node networking (InfiniBand vs Ethernet, bandwidth), and regional availability (H100 availability is often constrained)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cloud GPU selection requires looking beyond the GPU model. AWS P5: 8× H100 with NVLink 4.0, 3,200Gb/s EFA networking — closest to on-prem DGX experience. AWS G6: 1-8× L4, Ethernet only — cost-effective for inference. Azure ND H100 v5: 8× H100, InfiniBand NDR networking — strong for multi-node training. GCP A3: 8× H100, GPUDirect-TCPX (Google's custom high-speed networking). Key differentiators: intra-node GPU interconnect (NVLink? PCIe only?), inter-node networking (InfiniBand? Ethernet? custom?), and storage integration (local NVMe capacity, object storage throughput). For distributed training: NVLink + InfiniBand is essential — this rules out many cheaper instances.

AI Infra Q90 of 200
What is NVIDIA DGX Cloud, and how does it differ from traditional cloud GPU instances?
A) DGX Cloud is NVIDIA's managed AI infrastructure — NVIDIA provisions and manages DGX clusters inside cloud provider data centers (currently Oracle Cloud, soon AWS/Azure/GCP). Unlike renting raw GPU instances (where you manage drivers, CUDA, frameworks), DGX Cloud provides a fully managed AI platform with pre-configured software stack, Base Command orchestration, and NVIDIA support — closer to "AI Platform as a Service" than "GPU as a Service"
B) DGX Cloud is a public cloud provider — NVIDIA built its own data centers to compete directly with AWS, Azure, and GCP in the general cloud computing market
C) DGX Cloud is just a rebranded AWS P5 instance — NVIDIA resells AWS GPU capacity under the DGX Cloud brand with no functional difference
D) DGX Cloud is a consumer service for gaming — it streams PC games to users' devices using GeForce GPUs in the cloud
✅ Correct Answer: A

DGX Cloud is NVIDIA's "we'll run AI infrastructure for you" offering. Key differentiators from raw cloud GPU: NVIDIA manages the full stack (drivers, CUDA, cuDNN, frameworks, orchestration), pre-configured for AI (no driver installation, no CUDA version management), Base Command integration (job scheduling, cluster management, monitoring), and NVIDIA support (AI infrastructure experts, not general cloud support). It runs ON cloud provider infrastructure (Oracle Cloud initially, expanding to AWS/Azure/GCP) — NVIDIA rents the data center space and runs their own DGX servers there. Pricing includes everything (compute + software + support), not just raw GPU hours.

2.5 Cluster Components & Architecture for AIQ91-Q100

AI Infra Q91 of 200
What are the key node types in an AI cluster, and why can't you just have GPU nodes?
A) Only GPU nodes are needed — all cluster functions (scheduling, storage, login) can run on GPU nodes without dedicated infrastructure
B) AI clusters require multiple node types: GPU compute nodes (training/inference — H100/A100), CPU-only login/head nodes (job submission, file management), storage nodes (high-performance parallel filesystems like Lustre/WEKA/VAST), and management nodes (Slurm controller, monitoring, identity). GPU nodes are too expensive to waste on infrastructure tasks that don't need GPU acceleration
C) AI clusters only need GPU nodes and storage nodes — login and management functions are handled by cloud services; no on-premises infrastructure nodes are required
D) AI clusters are identical to HPC clusters — the node types and configurations are interchangeable with no AI-specific requirements
✅ Correct Answer: B

AI clusters are multi-tiered. GPU compute nodes are the expensive, specialized resource — they should do nothing but AI computation. Login/head nodes: where users SSH in, submit jobs, manage files — CPU-only, modest specs (2-4 cores, 32GB RAM). Running these on GPU nodes wastes $100/hr resources. Storage nodes: AI workloads need high-throughput parallel filesystems (Lustre, GPFS, WEKA, VAST) capable of saturating GPU bandwidth — separate storage servers with high-speed networking. Management nodes: Slurm controller, monitoring stack (Prometheus/Grafana/DCGM), identity management (LDAP/AD) — critical but lightweight. The golden rule: GPU nodes are for GPUs; everything else goes on cheaper infrastructure.

AI Cluster Node Types
  AI CLUSTER ARCHITECTURE
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GPU COMPUTE (expensive — $100+/hr)                               │
  │ ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────────┐   │
  │ │ DGX #1   │ DGX #2   │ DGX #3   │ ...                      │   │
  │ └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────────┘   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LOGIN (CPU)        STORAGE (CPU+NVMe)     MANAGEMENT (CPU)      │
  │ ┌────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐  │
  │ │ SSH, Slurm │     │ Lustre/WEKA/VAST │   │ Slurm Controller │  │
  │ │ file mgmt  │     │ Parallel FS      │   │ Prometheus/Graf  │  │
  │ └────────────┘     └──────────────────┘   │ LDAP, DNS, DHCP  │  │
  │                                           └──────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
AI Infra Q92 of 200
What is the "three-fabric" design in AI clusters, and why are separate networks necessary?
A) A single Ethernet network handles everything — modern 400GbE switches provide sufficient bandwidth for compute, storage, and management traffic simultaneously
B) The three fabrics are redundant copies of the same network for high availability — they carry identical traffic for fault tolerance
C) AI clusters use three separate network fabrics: Compute fabric (InfiniBand/RoCE at 400Gb/s — GPU-to-GPU communication, NCCL collectives, lowest latency), Storage fabric (InfiniBand or high-speed Ethernet — GPU to parallel filesystem, GPUDirect Storage), and Management fabric (1GbE Ethernet — SSH, monitoring, provisioning, out-of-band management). Separation prevents storage traffic from interfering with latency-sensitive GPU communication
D) The three fabrics refer to three different InfiniBand speed tiers: NDR (400Gb/s), HDR (200Gb/s), and EDR (100Gb/s) for different priority traffic
✅ Correct Answer: C

Network fabric separation is critical for AI cluster performance. Compute fabric: the most critical — carries NCCL AllReduce traffic during distributed training. Latency directly affects training throughput. InfiniBand with sharp computing (in-network reduction) is typically used. Must be lossless — any packet drop causes NCCL to stall. Storage fabric: GPUDirect Storage traffic — high bandwidth but can tolerate slightly higher latency. Often uses the same InfiniBand technology but separate fabric to avoid competing with compute traffic. Management fabric: low-speed Ethernet (1-25GbE) for SSH, Slurm commands, monitoring, and BMC/IPMI. Doesn't need high bandwidth — keeping it separate prevents accidental large data transfers from impacting cluster operations.

Three-Fabric AI Cluster Design
  COMPUTE FABRIC          STORAGE FABRIC          MANAGEMENT FABRIC
  ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐
  │ InfiniBand NDR   │   │ InfiniBand/RoCE  │   │ 1-25 GbE         │
  │ 400 Gb/s         │   │ 200-400 Gb/s     │   │ SSH, Slurm, BIMC │
  │ GPU-GPU (NCCL)   │   │ GPU-Storage (GDS)│   │ Monitoring       │
  │ LATENCY-CRITICAL │   │ BANDWIDTH-HEAVY  │   │ LOW-BANDWIDTH    │
  │ LOSS MUST BE 0%  │   │ Loss tolerance   │   │ Loss tolerant    │
  └──────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘
  SEPARATION: Storage traffic NEVER competes with NCCL for bandwidth
AI Infra Q93 of 200
What is Slurm, and why is it the dominant job scheduler for AI/HPC clusters?
A) Slurm is a container orchestrator like Kubernetes — it manages Docker containers across GPU nodes with auto-scaling and service discovery
B) Slurm is a GPU driver management tool — it handles CUDA driver installation, updates, and version management across hundreds of GPU nodes
C) Slurm is a monitoring dashboard — it displays GPU utilization metrics in real-time using DCGM data with Grafana visualization
D) Slurm (Simple Linux Utility for Resource Management) is an open-source job scheduler and workload manager — it allocates GPU nodes to users/jobs, manages job queues with priorities and fair-share scheduling, enforces time limits, and handles resource isolation. It's dominant because it scales to 100,000+ nodes, supports GPU scheduling (--gpus, --gres), and is the standard in HPC/AI centers worldwide
✅ Correct Answer: D

Slurm is the operating system of AI clusters — it decides who gets GPUs and when. Key concepts: Partitions (groups of nodes, e.g., "dgx" partition for DGX nodes, "inference" for L40S nodes), Accounts (billing/priority groups — research lab A vs lab B), QOS (Quality of Service — job priority, max runtime, max GPUs per user), and GPU Generic Resource Scheduling (--gres=gpu:H100:8 — request 8 H100 GPUs). Slurm's GPU support is critical: it tracks which GPUs are allocated to which jobs, prevents GPU oversubscription, and handles GPU affinity (binding jobs to specific GPUs for optimal NVLink topology).

Slurm Architecture
  USERS: sbatch job.sh, srun --pty, squeue, scancel
          ↓
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ slurmctld (controller) — scheduling logic, job queue, DB     │
  │         ↓                                                     │
  │ slurmd (daemon on each node) — executes jobs, reports status │
  │         ↓                                                     │
  │ slurmdbd (database) — job accounting, fair-share tracking   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  GPU JOB: #SBATCH --gres=gpu:H100:8 --time=48:00:00 --partition=dgx
AI Infra Q94 of 200
Why does AI networking need InfiniBand or specialized Ethernet (Spectrum-X) rather than standard enterprise Ethernet?
A) AI training involves frequent, synchronized communication bursts (NCCL AllReduce) where ALL GPUs must exchange data simultaneously. Standard Ethernet uses "best-effort" delivery with packet loss handled by TCP retransmission — in AI, one dropped packet stalls ALL GPUs (the "straggler problem"). InfiniBand provides: lossless fabric (credit-based flow control — zero packet loss), RDMA (Remote Direct Memory Access — GPU memory → NIC → wire → NIC → GPU memory bypassing CPU), and in-network computing (SHARP — the switch performs the reduction operation, halving data movement). Spectrum-X is NVIDIA's Ethernet solution that adds these capabilities to Ethernet
B) AI networking can use standard enterprise Ethernet — the performance difference between 400GbE enterprise switches and InfiniBand is negligible for AI workloads
C) InfiniBand is only needed for storage — GPU-to-GPU communication during training uses PCIe within the server and doesn't require special networking
D) Wi-Fi 7 provides sufficient bandwidth and latency for AI cluster networking — wired connections are legacy technology being phased out
✅ Correct Answer: A
AI Networking — Why Standard Ethernet Fails
  STANDARD ETHERNET: Best-effort, packet loss → TCP retry → ALL GPUs STALL
  AI NETWORKING (IB/Spectrum-X): Lossless, RDMA GPU→GPU, SHARP in-switch reduction
  0.001% packet loss = 50% NCCL throughput collapse. AI needs ZERO loss.

AI networking is fundamentally different from enterprise networking. In distributed training, GPUs run in lockstep — every GPU must complete its forward+backward pass, then AllReduce gradients, then all GPUs proceed together. If one GPU's network packet is delayed or dropped, ALL GPUs wait. Standard Ethernet drops packets under congestion and relies on TCP retransmission — in AI, this creates cascading stalls. InfiniBand uses credit-based flow control — a sender cannot transmit unless the receiver has buffer space — guaranteeing zero packet loss. RDMA enables direct memory access between GPU HBM across nodes without CPU involvement. SHARP (in-network computing): the InfiniBand switch itself sums gradients during AllReduce, cutting data movement in half. Spectrum-X brings these capabilities to Ethernet for organizations committed to Ethernet infrastructure.

AI Infra Q95 of 200
What is GPUDirect RDMA, and why does it matter for multi-node AI training?
A) GPUDirect RDMA compresses gradient data before network transmission — it's a data compression technology that reduces bandwidth requirements by 50%
B) GPUDirect RDMA is a GPU memory expansion technology — it allows GPUs to use system RAM as extended memory when HBM is full
C) GPUDirect RDMA enables direct data transfer between GPU memory on one node and GPU memory on another node — bypassing CPU and system memory on both ends. The data path is: GPU HBM → PCIe → NIC → InfiniBand → NIC → PCIe → GPU HBM. Without it, the path would be: GPU HBM → CPU RAM → NIC → InfiniBand → NIC → CPU RAM → GPU HBM — doubling latency and consuming CPU resources. For NCCL AllReduce, GPUDirect RDMA is essential for high-performance multi-node training
D) GPUDirect RDMA is a GPU virtualization technology — it allows multiple VMs to share a single GPU through remote direct memory access
✅ Correct Answer: C
GPUDirect RDMA vs Traditional Data Path
  WITHOUT GPUDirect RDMA          WITH GPUDirect RDMA
  GPU→CPU RAM→NIC→NET→NIC→CPU→GPU  GPU→NIC→NET→NIC→GPU
  6 memory copies, CPU loaded     2 copies, zero CPU, 3× lower latency

GPUDirect RDMA eliminates the CPU from the data path during multi-node GPU communication. In NCCL AllReduce across nodes: 8 GPUs per node × N nodes. During gradient synchronization, each GPU's gradients must reach other GPUs. Without GPUDirect RDMA: GPU → CPU RAM (PCIe) → NIC buffer (PCIe) → network. The CPU bounce adds latency and consumes memory bandwidth. With GPUDirect RDMA: GPU → NIC (direct PCIe peer-to-peer) → network. This cuts latency by 30-50% and frees CPU resources. For large-scale training (100+ nodes), this difference compounds — GPUDirect RDMA can mean the difference between 85% and 95% scaling efficiency.

AI Infra Q96 of 200
What is a DPU (Data Processing Unit), and what role does the NVIDIA BlueField DPU play in AI infrastructure?
A) A DPU is a smaller, cheaper GPU for inference — it's NVIDIA's entry-level AI accelerator for workloads that don't need full Tensor Core performance
B) A DPU is a smart NIC that offloads infrastructure tasks from the CPU — networking (OVS, routing, firewall), storage (NVMe-oF, compression, encryption), and security (TLS, IPsec, root of trust). The BlueField-3 DPU frees CPU cores for AI workloads by handling data center infrastructure, and enables software-defined, hardware-accelerated networking and storage for GPU clusters
C) A DPU is a display processing unit — it handles video output and GUI rendering for AI workstations, similar to a graphics card's display controller
D) A DPU is a deprecated term for a GPU — NVIDIA renamed their GPU line to DPU in 2023 to emphasize data processing capabilities over graphics
✅ Correct Answer: B
DPU — The Third Compute Engine
  AI SERVER: CPU + GPU + DPU
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ CPU: AI orchestration, data prep, job scheduling            │
  │ GPU: AI training/inference (Tensor Cores, HBM)              │
  │ DPU: Networking, Storage, Security offload (BlueField-3)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  DPU frees 20-30% CPU for AI workloads by offloading infrastructure

The DPU is the third compute engine in modern AI servers, alongside CPU and GPU. In a GPU server, the CPU should be focused on AI workload orchestration — not running the network stack, storage drivers, or security software. The BlueField DPU takes over: networking (OVS offload, RDMA, network virtualization), storage (NVMe over Fabrics offload, compression at line rate, encryption at 400Gb/s), and security (hardware root of trust, zero-trust networking, real-time telemetry). For AI clusters: the DPU handles east-west traffic between GPUs at line rate while the CPU focuses on training orchestration. This is critical for multi-tenant cloud GPU deployments where infrastructure overhead can consume 20-30% of CPU cores.

What is NVIDIA Base Command, and how does it simplify AI cluster management?
A) Base Command is a Linux shell replacement — it's an AI-optimized command-line interface that replaces bash for GPU cluster administration
B) Base Command is a GPU driver management tool — it automates CUDA driver installation and updates across heterogeneous GPU clusters
C) Base Command is a firmware-level interface for DGX systems — it's the BIOS/UEFI replacement that manages low-level hardware configuration
D) Base Command is NVIDIA's AI cluster management and orchestration platform. It provides: centralized job scheduling (submit, monitor, manage across clusters), dataset and model versioning, resource monitoring and utilization tracking, user/team/project management, and integration with DGX Cloud. It's the management layer above Slurm/Kubernetes that provides a unified interface for AI teams to manage their workloads without dealing with cluster infrastructure directly

Base Command — AI Cluster Management
  Base Command Layer (above Slurm/K8s)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Web UI + CLI: Jobs | Datasets | Models | Users | Monitoring │
  │         ↓                                                     │
  │ Slurm (training) / Kubernetes (inference)                    │
  │         ↓                                                     │
  │ GPU Cluster (DGX/HGX)                                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Base Command addresses the "clusters are hard to use" problem. AI researchers want to submit training jobs, not manage Slurm configurations. Base Command provides a web UI and CLI that abstracts the underlying cluster scheduler. Features: project workspaces (isolated environments for different teams), dataset management (version, share, and mount datasets), job templates (standardized training configurations), and utilization dashboards (who's using GPUs, for what, how efficiently). It integrates with Slurm and Kubernetes underneath but provides a simpler interface on top. For enterprises with multiple AI teams, Base Command provides governance (who can use how many GPUs) and cost tracking (chargeback per project/team).

AI Infra Q98 of 200
What is the difference between Slurm and Kubernetes for AI workload orchestration, and when would you use each?
A) Slurm is designed for batch HPC/AI jobs — optimized for scheduling thousands of short-to-long-running jobs across bare-metal GPU nodes with minimal overhead. Kubernetes is designed for containerized microservices — optimized for long-running services with auto-scaling, rolling updates, and service discovery. For AI: Slurm excels at ML training (batch jobs, MPI/NCCL integration, tight GPU allocation). Kubernetes excels at ML inference (containerized serving, auto-scaling, load balancing). Many organizations use both: Slurm for training, Kubernetes for inference
B) Kubernetes has completely replaced Slurm — K8s with GPU Operator is the only orchestrator needed for modern AI infrastructure; Slurm is legacy technology
C) Slurm and Kubernetes are identical — they're both container orchestrators with the same scheduling model, GPU support, and scaling characteristics
D) Neither Slurm nor Kubernetes should be used for AI — AI workloads should run directly on bare-metal GPUs without orchestration overhead
✅ Correct Answer: A
Slurm vs Kubernetes for AI
  SLURM (Batch)                    KUBERNETES (Service)
  ┌──────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ sbatch train.sh          │    │ kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml│
  │ Job → runs → completes   │    │ Pod → runs continuously      │
  │ Best for: Training        │    │ Best for: Inference serving  │
  │ MPI/NCCL native           │    │ GPU Operator for GPU mgmt   │
  └──────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────────┘

Slurm and Kubernetes solve different problems in AI infrastructure. Slurm: batch-oriented, designed for HPC. Jobs are scheduled, run to completion, and release resources. GPU allocation is explicit and exclusive (--gres=gpu:H100:8). Integrated with MPI/NCCL for distributed training. Excellent for: model training, hyperparameter sweeps, data preprocessing pipelines. Kubernetes: service-oriented, designed for continuous workloads. Pods can auto-scale, self-heal, and load-balance. GPU allocation via device plugin. Integrated with service mesh, ingress, and monitoring. Excellent for: inference serving (Triton, NIM), MLOps pipelines, JupyterHub. The "Slurm for training, K8s for inference" pattern is common because training is batch and inference is service-oriented.

AI Infra Q99 of 200
What is InfiniBand, and why does it dominate AI cluster networking over Ethernet?
A) InfiniBand is a storage protocol — it connects GPU servers to NVMe storage arrays but is not used for GPU-to-GPU communication
B) InfiniBand is a high-performance networking technology purpose-built for HPC/AI. Advantages over Ethernet: RDMA as a native transport (not bolted on like RoCE), credit-based flow control (lossless by design — critical for NCCL), in-network computing (SHARP — switches perform reductions), and deterministic latency (~1μs port-to-port vs 5-50μs for Ethernet). InfiniBand NDR delivers 400Gb/s per port with HPC-optimized congestion control. Ethernet with RoCE can approach InfiniBand performance, but InfiniBand's purpose-built nature gives it the edge for the largest AI clusters
C) InfiniBand and Ethernet are identical in performance — the choice between them is purely based on vendor preference and has no technical implications
D) InfiniBand has been replaced by Wi-Fi 7 in modern AI data centers — wireless networking provides lower latency and higher bandwidth than wired InfiniBand
✅ Correct Answer: B
InfiniBand vs Ethernet for AI
  INFINIBAND NDR                    SPECTRUM-X ETHERNET
  ┌──────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ 400 Gb/s per port        │     │ 400/800 Gb/s per port       │
  │ RDMA native              │     │ RoCE (RDMA over Ethernet)   │
  │ Credit-based (lossless) │     │ Adaptive routing + DPU      │
  │ SHARP in-network compute │     │ Ethernet ecosystem           │
  │ ~1μs latency             │     │ ~3-5μs latency              │
  └──────────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────────────┘

InfiniBand's dominance in AI comes from being purpose-built. RDMA is native — not an extension like RoCE (RDMA over Converged Ethernet). Credit-based flow control guarantees zero packet loss at the link level — critical because NCCL performance collapses with even 0.001% packet loss. SHARP (Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol): the InfiniBand switch hardware performs the reduction operation (summing gradients) as data passes through — effectively halving the data that must traverse the network. For a 1,024-GPU AllReduce, SHARP reduces network traffic by 2×. Congestion control: InfiniBand uses adaptive routing — dynamically spreading traffic across multiple paths to avoid hotspots. Ethernet is catching up (Spectrum-X, Ultra Ethernet Consortium), but InfiniBand remains the gold standard for exascale AI clusters.

AI Infra Q100 of 200
What is NVIDIA Spectrum-X, and why was it developed as an Ethernet alternative to InfiniBand?
A) Spectrum-X is a wireless networking technology — it uses the 60GHz spectrum band to provide cable-free GPU interconnect for flexible data center layouts
B) Spectrum-X is a rebranded InfiniBand product — NVIDIA renamed their InfiniBand line to Spectrum-X for marketing purposes with no technical changes
C) Spectrum-X is NVIDIA's Ethernet platform for AI — combining Spectrum-4 switches (51.2 Tb/s, 64× 800GbE ports), BlueField-3 DPUs, and specialized software (adaptive routing, congestion control, performance isolation). It brings InfiniBand-like capabilities (lossless fabric, adaptive routing, low latency) to Ethernet — enabling organizations committed to Ethernet infrastructure to build high-performance AI clusters without adopting InfiniBand
D) Spectrum-X is a software-only solution — it runs on any standard Ethernet switch without requiring NVIDIA hardware, making it a vendor-neutral AI networking platform
✅ Correct Answer: C
Spectrum-X — AI Ethernet Platform
  SPECTRUM-X = Spectrum-4 Switch + BlueField-3 DPU + Software
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Spectrum-4: 51.2 Tb/s, 64× 800GbE, adaptive routing         │
  │ BlueField-3: RoCE acceleration, congestion control          │
  │ Software: Performance isolation, lossless fabric management  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Ethernet for AI — InfiniBand capabilities, Ethernet ecosystem

Spectrum-X is NVIDIA's answer to "we want AI networking but we're an Ethernet shop." Many large enterprises have standardized on Ethernet for decades — their networking teams, tools, and processes are built around Ethernet. Spectrum-X lets them build AI clusters without adopting InfiniBand. Key technologies: Spectrum-4 switch (51.2 Tb/s, adaptive routing — dynamically balances traffic across all available paths), BlueField-3 DPU (hardware-accelerated RoCE, congestion control at the edge), and performance isolation (multiple AI jobs sharing the network without interfering — each job gets guaranteed bandwidth). The tradeoff vs InfiniBand: slightly higher latency (~3-5μs vs ~1μs), but Ethernet ecosystem compatibility. For most enterprises, Spectrum-X provides "good enough" AI networking without the InfiniBand learning curve.

2.6 Facility Requirements for AI Data CentersQ101-Q106

AI Infra Q101 of 200
An AI data center is being planned for a DGX SuperPOD deployment. What floor loading capacity is required, and why is this different from traditional data centers?
A) Standard data center floor loading (500-800 kg/m²) is sufficient — DGX racks use lightweight materials and weigh no more than traditional server racks
B) AI racks are extremely heavy — a rack of 4 DGX H100 systems can weigh 1,500-2,000+ kg (3,300-4,400+ lbs). Standard raised floors (rated 500-800 kg/m²) cannot support this. AI data centers need reinforced slab floors (1,500-2,500 kg/m² rating) or ground-level deployment. This fundamentally changes facility design and location options
C) Floor loading is irrelevant for AI — DGX systems are always wall-mounted to save floor space, eliminating weight concerns entirely
D) AI racks are lighter than traditional racks because GPUs have replaced heavy spinning hard drives — typical AI rack weight is under 500 kg
✅ Correct Answer: B

AI racks present unprecedented weight challenges. A DGX H100 weighs ~58 kg (128 lbs). A rack with 4 DGX systems: 4 × 58kg + rack/cables/PDU/networking = ~280-350 kg for just the equipment. Plus the rack cabinet itself. The concentrated weight — all in one rack footprint (~0.6 m²) — creates point loads far exceeding standard raised floor ratings. Solutions: ground-floor deployment (concrete slab), reinforced raised floors with supplemental supports, or weight-distributing base plates. This is a gating factor — you can't just "add GPUs to an existing data center" without structural engineering assessment.

Floor Loading — AI vs Traditional
  RACK WEIGHT COMPARISON
  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Rack Type            │ Weight (loaded)  │ Floor Rating Needed      │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Enterprise (CPU)     │ 400-800 kg       │ 500-800 kg/m²            │
  │ AI (4× DGX H100)     │ 1,500-2,000 kg   │ 1,500-2,500 kg/m²       │
  │ AI (8× DGX B200)     │ 2,000-2,500 kg   │ 2,000-3,000 kg/m²       │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘
  Standard raised floor: FAILS at ~800 kg/m² → AI needs slab or reinforced
AI Infra Q102 of 200
What power infrastructure is needed from the utility to the rack for a 1MW AI data center?
A) Standard 120V/208V service with standard panel boards — 1MW is a typical commercial building load requiring no special infrastructure
B) AI data centers run on battery power — they're completely off-grid using renewable energy with no utility connection required
C) Utility provides medium voltage (13.8kV or 34.5kV) → on-site substation steps down to 480V 3-phase → switchgear distributes to PDUs → PDUs step down to 415V/240V for racks → rack PDU feeds individual servers. Each DGX H100 requires 60A 3-phase circuits. Backup: UPS (battery) for graceful shutdown + diesel generators for extended outages
D) Power infrastructure is identical to an office building — standard 120V outlets and extension cords are sufficient for GPU servers
✅ Correct Answer: C

AI data center power is an industrial-scale challenge. The path: Utility transmission lines (115kV+) → on-site substation (13.8kV or 34.5kV) → step-down transformers (480V 3-phase) → main switchgear → Power Distribution Units (PDUs) → remote power panels → rack PDUs → server PSUs. Each DGX H100 needs 4× C19/C20 power connections (not standard NEMA 5-15 outlets). For a 1MW facility: that's ~100 DGX systems. UPS must cover the full load for 5-15 minutes (until generators start) — that's a room-sized battery bank, not a rack-mount UPS. Generator must handle 1MW+ with step-load capability.

AI Data Center Power Path
  UTILITY 115kV → SUBSTATION 13.8kV → TRANSFORMER 480V → SWITCHGEAR
  → PDU → RACK PDU (415V 3-phase) → Server PSU → GPU (700W each)
  UPS: Battery room, 1MW for 10min | Generator: 1.5MW diesel, step-load
AI Infra Q103 of 200
What fire suppression system is appropriate for an AI data center, and why can't standard water sprinklers be used?
A) Standard water sprinklers — they're code-compliant, cost-effective, and GPU servers are waterproof so water damage isn't a concern
B) No fire suppression needed — GPU servers use solid-state components that cannot catch fire; fire risk is eliminated by design
C) CO₂ flooding systems — they're the cheapest gas-based option and are safe for occupied spaces during discharge
D) Clean agent systems (FM-200, Novec 1230, inert gas) — they extinguish fires without damaging electronics, leave no residue, and are safe for occupied spaces. Water would destroy $300K+ GPU servers even if the fire is small. Pre-action dry-pipe systems may be used as backup but clean agent is primary for the data hall
✅ Correct Answer: D

Fire protection for AI data centers must balance safety with asset protection. Water: effective at extinguishing fires but destroys electronics on contact. A sprinkler activating over a DGX rack causes $1M+ in damage even if the fire was small. Clean agents (FM-200, Novec 1230): flood the room with gas that chemically interrupts combustion without harming electronics. They evaporate cleanly, leave no residue, and are safe for occupied spaces at design concentrations. The system typically uses VESDA (Very Early Smoke Detection Apparatus) — laser-based smoke detection that alerts at the incipient stage, before visible smoke or flame. Pre-action sprinklers (water held back by electric valve, only releases if BOTH smoke detector AND heat detector trigger) provide code-compliant backup.

Fire Suppression for AI Data Centers
  DETECTION: VESDA (laser smoke, stage-1 alert) → Heat detectors → Flame detectors
  SUPPRESSION: FM-200/Novec 1230 (clean agent, primary) → Pre-action sprinkler (backup)
  ⚠️ Water = $1M+ damage per rack | Clean agent = $0 equipment damage
AI Infra Q104 of 200
What physical security measures are appropriate for an AI data center housing millions of dollars in GPU infrastructure?
A) Multi-layer security: perimeter (fencing, bollards, cameras), building (badge access, biometrics, mantraps), data hall (separate badge zones, cameras, two-person rule), and rack level (locked cabinets, intrusion detection). GPU value density ($300K+ per 4U) makes them high-theft targets — security must match the asset value. Also: supply chain security (GPUs verified at delivery), secure disposal (GPU zeroization), and visitor escort policies
B) A single lock on the data center door is sufficient — GPUs are too heavy to steal and have no black-market value
C) Physical security is outsourced entirely to cloud providers — on-premises AI data centers don't need security because all valuable assets are virtual
D) Cybersecurity is the only security that matters — firewalls and encryption replace the need for physical security measures
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU security is a physical concern. At $30K-40K per H100 and 8 per DGX, a single rack holds $1M+ in GPUs — comparable to a rack of gold bars. Multi-layer security follows the "defense in depth" principle. Perimeter: anti-ram bollards, security fencing, 24/7 camera coverage with AI-based anomaly detection. Building entry: badge + biometric (fingerprint/iris), mantrap (one person at a time, weight-based tailgating detection). Data hall: separate access control, cameras on every rack aisle, two-person rule for GPU access. Rack: locking front/rear doors, intrusion detection switches, GPU serial number tracking. Supply chain: GPU shipments verified at receiving (serial numbers checked against manifest), tamper-evident packaging inspection.

AI Data Center Security — Defense in Depth
  LAYER 1 (Perimeter) → LAYER 2 (Building) → LAYER 3 (Data Hall) → LAYER 4 (Rack)
  Fencing, bollards    Badge+biometric      Separate access      Locked cabinet
  AI cameras            Mantrap              Aisle cameras        Intrusion sensor
  24/7 guards           Visitor escort       2-person rule        Serial tracking
AI Infra Q105 of 200
What location factors should be considered when siting an AI data center?
A) Only real estate cost matters — the cheapest available land anywhere in the world is the optimal AI data center location
B) Power cost ($/kWh is the #1 factor — at 10MW, $0.01/kWh difference = $876K/year), network latency to users/researchers, natural disaster risk (earthquakes, floods, hurricanes), climate (free air cooling potential), tax incentives (many jurisdictions offer data center tax breaks), and talent availability (AI infrastructure engineers). Power cost alone can make or break the economics
C) Proximity to NVIDIA headquarters in Santa Clara, California — all AI data centers must be within 100 miles for hardware support eligibility
D) Aesthetic factors — AI data centers should be located in scenic areas to attract top AI talent with beautiful views from the server rooms
