AI data center siting is dominated by power economics. Power cost comparison: Quincy, WA ($0.03/kWh — hydroelectric) vs Virginia ($0.06/kWh) vs California ($0.15/kWh). For a 10MW AI DC: Quincy = $2.6M/year, Virginia = $5.3M, California = $13.1M. The $10.5M/year difference between Quincy and California buys a lot of GPUs. Network latency: training is latency-tolerant but researchers need interactive access. Natural disasters: avoid flood zones and seismic areas (Oregon/WA for seismic, Florida/Gulf for hurricanes). Climate: cool climates reduce cooling energy (Nordic countries popular — free air cooling year-round). Tax incentives: many states/countries offer sales tax exemptions on data center equipment, property tax abatements, and power cost reductions.

AI Data Center Location Factors
  POWER COST (10MW, 24/7): $0.03/kWh→$2.6M/yr | $0.06→$5.3M | $0.15→$13.1M
  NETWORK: <10ms to users (training OK with higher latency)
  CLIMATE: Cold=free cooling | DISASTER: Avoid seismic/flood/hurricane zones
  TAX: Data center incentives can reduce TCO 20-30%
AI Infra Q106 of 200
What is the purpose of UPS and generator backup in an AI data center?
A) UPS allows training to continue uninterrupted during extended power outages — AI jobs seamlessly migrate to battery power for days
B) UPS and generators are unnecessary — AI training checkpoints mean any power loss is irrelevant; you just resume from the last checkpoint when power returns
C) UPS provides immediate, gap-free power during the 10-15 seconds between utility failure and generator startup — preventing GPU server crashes and checkpoint corruption. Generators provide long-term backup for hours/days. The UPS also conditions power (filtering spikes/sags/noise). For AI DCs, the UPS typically targets 5-15 minutes runtime — enough for graceful shutdown if generators fail
D) UPS stands for "Uninterrupted Processing System" — it's a redundant GPU cluster that takes over training when the primary cluster loses power
✅ Correct Answer: C

UPS and generator form a two-stage power protection system. UPS (Uninterruptible Power Supply): online double-conversion — AC→DC→AC continuously. Provides zero transfer time (the inverter is always powering the load). Battery banks sized for 5-15 minutes at full load. Also provides power conditioning — filtering voltage sags, surges, harmonics, and frequency variations that would crash sensitive GPU servers. Generator: diesel or natural gas, sized for full data center load plus growth margin. Auto-start on utility loss, reaches full output in 10-15 seconds. The UPS bridges the gap. For AI: a power glitch of even 50ms can crash GPU servers — the UPS prevents this. Generator fuel storage: typically 24-72 hours with refueling contracts.

Power Protection Chain
  UTILITY → UPS (battery, 0ms switch) → PDU → Server
                ↓ (utility fails)
  GENERATOR (starts in 10s) → UPS → PDU → Server (seamless)
  UPS: 5-15min battery | Generator: 24-72hrs fuel | Power conditioning: always

2.7 Networking for AI WorkloadsQ107-Q114

AI Infra Q107 of 200
Why is east-west traffic dominant in AI clusters, and how does this differ from traditional data center networking?
A) AI clusters have the same traffic patterns as enterprise — the network design is identical regardless of workload type
B) East-west traffic in AI refers to GPU data being sent to China and India — it's a geopolitical routing optimization
C) AI clusters are north-south dominant because most traffic goes to cloud storage and the internet for inference serving
D) In AI training, GPUs constantly communicate with each other (NCCL AllReduce, gradient sync) — this is east-west traffic within and between nodes. Traditional DCs are north-south dominant (client→server requests). AI clusters generate Tbps of east-west traffic that never leaves the cluster — requiring non-blocking fabric designs where any GPU can talk to any GPU at full bandwidth
✅ Correct Answer: D

AI traffic patterns fundamentally differ from traditional data centers. Enterprise DC: 80% north-south (user→web server→database). AI training DC: 90%+ east-west (GPU↔GPU NCCL communication). During AllReduce, every GPU communicates with every other GPU simultaneously — generating massive internal bandwidth demands. This requires non-blocking fabric: any GPU port can communicate at full line rate to any other GPU port without congestion. Traditional oversubscribed designs (3:1 or higher) that work fine for web traffic fail catastrophically for AI — NCCL performance collapses with congestion. This is why AI clusters use fat-tree or dragonfly topologies with 1:1 oversubscription.

Traffic Patterns — Enterprise vs AI
  ENTERPRISE DC (North-South)           AI DC (East-West)
  Users → Web → DB                      GPU0↔GPU1↔GPU2↔...↔GPU1023
  80% client-server                    90%+ GPU-GPU (NCCL AllReduce)
  Oversubscription: 3:1 OK             Oversubscription: MUST be 1:1
AI Infra Q108 of 200
What is RDMA (Remote Direct Memory Access), and why is it essential for AI networking?
A) RDMA allows one computer's GPU to directly read/write another computer's GPU memory over the network — bypassing the CPU and operating system on both ends. This is essential because NCCL AllReduce requires every GPU to exchange gradients with every other GPU; without RDMA, data would go GPU→CPU RAM→NIC→network→NIC→CPU RAM→GPU, doubling latency and consuming CPU resources. InfiniBand natively supports RDMA; Ethernet supports it via RoCE (RDMA over Converged Ethernet)
B) RDMA is a GPU memory expansion technology — it allows GPUs to use network-attached RAM as extended HBM when local memory is full
C) RDMA is a remote desktop protocol — it allows administrators to access GPU servers remotely for management and troubleshooting
D) RDMA stands for "Redundant Data Mirroring Array" — it's a RAID-like technology for mirroring training data across multiple GPU servers
✅ Correct Answer: A

RDMA is the technology that makes distributed training practical. Without RDMA: GPU computes gradient → copies to CPU RAM (PCIe) → CPU copies to NIC buffer → NIC sends → remote NIC receives → CPU copies to RAM → CPU copies to GPU. That's 6 memory copies and 2 CPU interrupts per data transfer. With RDMA (GPUDirect): GPU DMA engine → NIC → wire → NIC → GPU DMA engine. Two copies, zero CPU involvement, ~3× lower latency. For NCCL AllReduce on 1,024 GPUs, this difference is transformative — CPU-mediated transfers would consume 20-30% of CPU cores just moving data, while adding microseconds of latency per transfer.

RDMA vs Traditional Network Transfer
  WITHOUT RDMA: GPU→CPU→RAM→NIC→NET→NIC→RAM→CPU→GPU (6 copies, 2 CPUs)
  WITH RDMA:    GPU→NIC→NET→NIC→GPU (2 copies, 0 CPUs)
  Latency: ~10μs vs ~3μs | CPU: loaded vs idle | Bandwidth: 85% vs 98% of line rate
AI Infra Q109 of 200
What is RoCE (RDMA over Converged Ethernet), and how does it compare to native InfiniBand RDMA?
A) RoCE is a completely different protocol from InfiniBand — it cannot be used for GPU communication and is only for storage traffic
B) RoCE brings RDMA capabilities to Ethernet — wrapping InfiniBand transport packets inside Ethernet frames. RoCE v2 uses UDP/IP encapsulation for routability. Compared to native InfiniBand: RoCE is slightly higher latency (3-5μs vs ~1μs), requires PFC (Priority Flow Control) for losslessness, and depends on DCB (Data Center Bridging) configuration. The tradeoff: InfiniBand performance on Ethernet infrastructure — critical for organizations committed to Ethernet
C) RoCE outperforms native InfiniBand — Ethernet's larger MTU and broader ecosystem make RoCE the superior choice for all AI workloads
D) RoCE is a wireless technology — it provides RDMA over Wi-Fi for cable-free GPU cluster networking
✅ Correct Answer: B

RoCE is the bridge between Ethernet and RDMA. It encapsulates InfiniBand transport (reliable connected transport) inside Ethernet frames — RoCE v2 adds UDP/IP headers for routing across subnets. Key dependencies: PFC (Priority Flow Control) — creates lossless Ethernet classes by pausing transmitters when buffers fill. Without PFC, packet loss destroys RDMA performance. ECN (Explicit Congestion Notification) — marks packets experiencing congestion before drops occur. DCB (Data Center Bridging) — ensures consistent PFC/ETS configuration across the fabric. The challenge: PFC misconfiguration is the #1 cause of RoCE performance issues. Spectrum-X addresses this with adaptive routing and intelligent congestion control that reduces PFC dependency.

RoCE vs Native InfiniBand
  ROCE v2                              NATIVE INFINIBAND
  RDMA over UDP/IP Ethernet            RDMA native transport
  Latency: 3-5μs                      Latency: ~1μs
  Lossless via PFC (complex)          Lossless by design (credit-based)
  NIC: ConnectX-7                     NIC: ConnectX-7 (same HW)
AI Infra Q110 of 200
What network topology is optimal for GPU clusters, and what is "rail-optimized" design?
A) Simple star topology — one central switch connecting all GPUs; it's the simplest and most cost-effective design
B) Daisy-chain — GPUs connected in a single line; data passes through each GPU sequentially like a bucket brigade
C) Fat-tree (non-blocking) for general GPU clusters — provides full bisection bandwidth. Rail-optimized: each GPU in a server connects to a different leaf switch — GPU 0s all connect to switch 0, GPU 1s to switch 1, etc. This ensures NCCL ring/AllReduce operations traverse the minimum number of switch hops, and GPU-to-GPU communication within the same "rail" stays within a single switch
D) Wireless mesh topology — Wi-Fi 7 mesh between GPU servers eliminates the need for physical cables
✅ Correct Answer: C

Network topology directly determines training performance. Fat-tree: a non-blocking Clos topology where spine switches connect to leaf switches, and leaves connect to servers. Provides full bisection bandwidth — any server can communicate at full line rate to any other server. Oversubscription is 1:1 (non-blocking). Rail-optimized design (NVIDIA's recommended topology for DGX): each DGX has 8 GPUs, each with its own NIC. GPU 0's NIC connects to leaf switch 0, GPU 1's NIC to leaf switch 1, etc. This means all GPU 0s in the cluster are on the same rail/switch — intra-rail communication stays within one switch (minimum latency). NCCL ring algorithms naturally map to this topology. For 1,024 GPUs: 8 rails × 128 GPUs per rail.

Rail-Optimized Topology
  DGX #1: G0→Switch0  G1→Switch1  ...  G7→Switch7
  DGX #2: G0→Switch0  G1→Switch1  ...  G7→Switch7
  All GPU 0s on SAME switch → intra-rail comm at 400Gb/s, 1 switch hop
  NCCL Ring naturally maps to rail topology
AI Infra Q111 of 200
What is tail latency, and why is it particularly damaging for distributed AI training?
A) Tail latency is the delay between a user submitting a training job and the job actually starting — it's a scheduling problem, not a networking problem
B) Tail latency only affects inference, not training — training runs are batch processes where occasional delays don't matter
C) Tail latency is a GPU memory issue — it refers to the last portion of HBM being slower than the first portion due to thermal effects
D) Tail latency is the worst-case (p99, p99.9) network latency — while average latency might be 3μs, 0.1% of packets may experience 100μs+. In synchronous AllReduce, EVERY GPU waits for the SLOWEST participant — one GPU experiencing tail latency stalls the entire 1,024-GPU training step. This is the "straggler problem" — tail latency directly reduces training throughput, not just average latency
✅ Correct Answer: D

Tail latency is the hidden performance killer in AI clusters. Average latency looks fine (3μs), but the p99.9 latency reveals the truth (100μs+). In synchronous AllReduce, the entire training step is gated by the slowest GPU. If 1,023 GPUs finish in 3μs but GPU #514 takes 150μs due to congestion, ALL 1,024 GPUs wait 150μs. At 1M iterations × 150μs per iteration = 150 seconds of pure waiting — per training run. Solutions: adaptive routing (dynamically avoid congested paths), deep-buffered switches (absorb micro-bursts), and QoS (prioritize NCCL traffic). InfiniBand's cut-through switching and credit-based flow control inherently minimize tail latency better than Ethernet.

Tail Latency — The Straggler Problem
  AVERAGE LATENCY: 3μs (looks great!)
  P99.9 LATENCY: 150μs (the hidden killer)
  1 slow GPU → ALL 1024 GPUs wait → 150μs × 1M iterations = 150 seconds wasted
  SOLUTIONS: Adaptive routing, deep buffers, QoS, cut-through switching
AI Infra Q112 of 200
What is NVIDIA Spectrum-X, and how does it specifically address AI networking challenges on Ethernet?
A) Spectrum-X combines Spectrum-4 switches (51.2 Tb/s, adaptive routing), BlueField-3 DPUs (congestion control, RoCE acceleration), and software (performance isolation, fabric management) to deliver lossless, low-latency Ethernet for AI. Adaptive routing spreads traffic across all available paths, preventing hot-spots and reducing tail latency. Performance isolation guarantees bandwidth per tenant, preventing noisy-neighbor problems in multi-tenant AI clouds
B) Spectrum-X is a spectrum analyzer tool — it monitors wireless frequency usage in data centers to avoid Wi-Fi interference with InfiniBand signals
C) Spectrum-X is a software-defined radio platform — it converts Ethernet signals to InfiniBand protocol for hybrid networking environments
D) Spectrum-X is NVIDIA's branding for standard enterprise Ethernet — it adds no new capabilities beyond what any 400GbE switch provides
✅ Correct Answer: A

Spectrum-X is NVIDIA's answer to "AI networking on Ethernet." Traditional Ethernet fails at AI because: ECMP (Equal-Cost Multi-Path) static hashing creates hot-spots, packet loss triggers TCP retransmission stalls, and lack of performance isolation means one job's traffic can starve another. Spectrum-X fixes these: Adaptive routing (packet-level, not flow-level — each packet can take a different path based on real-time congestion), dynamic congestion control (BlueField-3 DPU monitors and adjusts transmission rates), and performance isolation (guaranteed bandwidth per job — solves the multi-tenancy problem for AI cloud providers). Together, these deliver InfiniBand-like performance on Ethernet infrastructure.

Spectrum-X = Spectrum-4 + BlueField-3 + Software
  SPECTRUM-4: 51.2Tb/s switch, packet-level adaptive routing, deep buffers
  BLUEFIELD-3 DPU: Congestion control, RoCE acceleration, telemetry
  SOFTWARE: Performance isolation, fabric validation, automated management
  RESULT: InfiniBand-class AI networking on Ethernet infrastructure
AI Infra Q113 of 200
What is GPUDirect Storage, and how does it differ from GPUDirect RDMA?
A) GPUDirect Storage and GPUDirect RDMA are the same technology — the terms are interchangeable
B) GPUDirect RDMA enables GPU↔GPU communication across the network (GPU→NIC→wire→NIC→GPU). GPUDirect Storage enables GPU↔NVMe storage communication (NVMe→PCIe→GPU) — bypassing CPU and system memory for data loading. GDS is critical for training on datasets larger than GPU memory, enabling direct streaming from NVMe to GPU at full storage bandwidth
C) GPUDirect Storage is a cloud backup service — it automatically backs up GPU memory contents to cloud object storage during training
D) GPUDirect Storage enables GPU↔CPU communication — it's an optimized path for CPU-GPU data transfers that replaces standard PCIe DMA
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPUDirect Storage (GDS) solves the data loading bottleneck. Traditional data path: NVMe → CPU RAM (via PCIe + DMA) → CPU copies to pinned memory → GPU (via PCIe DMA). That's two PCIe traversals and a CPU memory copy. GDS path: NVMe → GPU (direct DMA over PCIe bus, bypassing CPU and system RAM entirely). This is critical when dataset size exceeds GPU memory — data streams directly from NVMe to GPU as needed. For applications like graph neural networks or large-scale recommendation systems where datasets are terabytes, GDS eliminates the CPU bottleneck and reduces data loading latency by 2-3×.

GPUDirect RDMA vs GPUDirect Storage
  GPUDirect RDMA: GPU₁→NIC→Network→NIC→GPU₂ (GPU-GPU across nodes)
  GPUDirect Storage: NVMe→GPU (direct DMA, bypasses CPU/RAM)
  Both: zero CPU involvement, direct PCIe peer-to-peer transfers
AI Infra Q114 of 200
What is adaptive routing, and why is it critical for AI network performance?
A) Adaptive routing means the network administrator manually reconfigures routes when they notice congestion — it's a manual optimization process
B) Adaptive routing is a DNS-based technique that routes users to the geographically nearest GPU cluster for inference serving
C) Adaptive routing pre-computes all possible paths at network configuration time and never changes them — it's "adaptive" because it adapts to topology changes, not traffic
D) Adaptive routing dynamically selects the best path for each packet based on real-time congestion — unlike static ECMP which hashes flows to fixed paths. In AI workloads with elephant flows (large gradient transfers), static hashing creates persistent hot-spots. Adaptive routing spreads traffic across all available paths at packet granularity, eliminating hot-spots and reducing tail latency by up to 10×
✅ Correct Answer: D

Adaptive routing is the solution to ECMP's fundamental flaw. ECMP (Equal-Cost Multi-Path): hashes the 5-tuple (src IP, dst IP, src port, dst port, protocol) to select one fixed path. All packets of a flow follow the same path. Problem: NCCL creates long-lived "elephant flows" (gigabytes of gradient data). If two elephant flows hash to the same path, that path is permanently congested while other paths sit idle. Adaptive routing (used by Spectrum-4 and InfiniBand): each packet independently selects the least-congested path at that instant. Result: all paths are utilized, hot-spots are eliminated, and tail latency drops dramatically. Spectrum-4 does this at 51.2 Tb/s — processing 8 billion packets per second with per-packet path selection.

ECMP vs Adaptive Routing
  ECMP (STATIC): Flow→Hash→Fixed path → Hot-spots when elephant flows collide
  ADAPTIVE (DYNAMIC): Each packet→Least congested path → All paths utilized
  Result: 10× lower tail latency, full fabric utilization

2.8 DPU (Data Processing Unit) — BlueFieldQ115-Q116

AI Infra Q115 of 200
What is a DPU, and how does the BlueField-3 DPU accelerate AI infrastructure beyond what a standard NIC provides?
A) A DPU is a smart NIC with its own CPU cores, memory, and accelerators — it offloads infrastructure tasks (networking OVS, storage NVMe-oF, security TLS/IPsec, RDMA, and virtualization) from the host CPU. BlueField-3 has 16 ARM cores, 16GB DDR5, and dual 200Gb/s ports. It handles the data center "overhead" so the host CPU can focus entirely on AI workloads. Unlike a standard NIC which just moves packets, a DPU processes, filters, encrypts, and routes them in hardware
B) A DPU is a smaller, slower GPU for non-AI workloads — it's a budget graphics processor for display output only
C) A DPU is a Data Processing Utility — it's a software daemon that runs on the host CPU to manage data preprocessing for AI training
D) A DPU is identical to a standard NIC but with NVIDIA branding — the hardware is the same; "DPU" is a marketing term
✅ Correct Answer: A

The DPU is the third compute engine in the modern data center. A standard NIC (like ConnectX-7) accelerates network I/O but leaves protocol processing to the host CPU. A DPU (BlueField-3) has its own compute: 16 ARM Cortex-A78 cores, 16GB DDR5, crypto accelerators, and programmable data path engines. It runs its own OS (DPU OS, based on Linux) and can independently handle: OVS (Open vSwitch) offload for virtual networking, NVMe-oF (NVMe over Fabrics) for remote storage access, TLS/IPsec encryption at line rate, root of trust and secure boot, and real-time telemetry. For AI: this frees 20-30% of host CPU cores that would otherwise be consumed by infrastructure — those cores can now do AI data preprocessing or orchestration instead.

NIC vs DPU — What's the Difference
  STANDARD NIC (ConnectX-7)            DPU (BlueField-3)
  Moves packets fast                   Processes packets intelligently
  Host CPU runs protocols              DPU CPU runs protocols
  No compute onboard                   16 ARM cores, 16GB RAM
  Crypto offload (basic)               Full TLS/IPsec/encryption at line rate
  InfiniBand/RoCE capable              Same + virtualization, storage, security
AI Infra Q116 of 200
What is NVIDIA DOCA, and how does it relate to the BlueField DPU like CUDA relates to the GPU?
A) DOCA is a database optimized for DPU storage — it's a key-value store that runs on BlueField for distributed caching
B) DOCA is a container runtime — it replaces Docker on DPU-powered servers for better security isolation
C) DOCA (Data Center Infrastructure on a Chip Architecture) is the software development platform for BlueField DPUs — just as CUDA enables GPU programming, DOCA enables DPU programming. It includes: drivers, libraries (networking, storage, security), SDK, runtime, and reference applications. Developers write DOCA programs to offload infrastructure workloads to the DPU
D) DOCA is an alternative to CUDA — it's a competing GPU programming model that some developers prefer over CUDA
✅ Correct Answer: C

DOCA is to DPU what CUDA is to GPU — the software ecosystem that makes the hardware programmable and useful. DOCA includes: DOCA Flow (programmable packet processing pipeline), DOCA Telemetry (real-time network and system metrics), DOCA Firefly (precise time synchronization for distributed systems), and DOCA Driver (low-level hardware interface). Without DOCA, BlueField is just a NIC with ARM cores — with DOCA, it's a programmable data center infrastructure platform. NVIDIA's strategy: CUDA for AI compute, DOCA for infrastructure compute. Together they enable fully accelerated data centers.

CUDA:GPU :: DOCA:DPU
  CUDA → GPU (AI compute acceleration)
  DOCA → DPU (Infrastructure acceleration)
  Together: Fully accelerated AI data center

3.1 AI Data Center Management & MonitoringQ117-Q120

AI Ops Q117 of 200
What is DCGM (Data Center GPU Manager), and what essential metrics does it provide?
A) DCGM is a GPU benchmarking tool — it runs performance tests to measure peak TFLOPS but doesn't provide ongoing monitoring
B) DCGM is NVIDIA's GPU monitoring and diagnostics platform. It provides: GPU utilization (SM, memory, encoder/decoder), temperature, power draw, clock speeds (SM and memory), ECC error counts (single and double bit), PCIe/NVLink throughput, XID errors (GPU hardware/software faults), and process tracking. It exports to Prometheus via DCGM-Exporter for Grafana dashboards and integrates with NVIDIA Nsight for profiling
C) DCGM is a GPU driver — it's the low-level software that enables the operating system to communicate with NVIDIA GPUs
D) DCGM is a cloud management platform — it's a web-based console for provisioning GPU instances across AWS, Azure, and GCP
✅ Correct Answer: B

DCGM is the foundation of GPU monitoring. Unlike nvidia-smi which provides a point-in-time snapshot, DCGM continuously collects metrics and provides health monitoring. Key metrics: GPU utilization (SM active %, memory bandwidth utilization), temperature (current, throttling threshold), power (current draw vs TDP), ECC errors (correctable single-bit vs uncorrectable double-bit — double-bit indicates failing GPU), XID errors (NVIDIA error codes — specific codes indicate specific hardware/software failures), and NVLink errors (replay counts, CRC errors). DCGM health checks: automated diagnostics that can proactively detect failing GPUs before they impact training jobs. DCGM-Exporter bridges to the Prometheus/Grafana monitoring ecosystem.

DCGM Monitoring Stack
  DCGM → DCGM-Exporter → Prometheus → Grafana
  Metrics: GPU util, temp, power, ECC, XID, NVLink, PCIe, clocks, processes
  Health checks: Automated GPU diagnostics, proactive failure detection
AI Ops Q118 of 200
What is nvidia-smi, and what critical information can you get from it in one command?
A) nvidia-smi is a GPU driver installer — it downloads and installs the latest NVIDIA drivers for your GPU model
B) nvidia-smi is a benchmark tool — it runs AI training workloads and reports performance metrics like images/second
C) nvidia-smi only shows GPU temperature — it's a simple thermal monitoring tool with no other functionality
D) nvidia-smi (System Management Interface) is the command-line Swiss Army knife for GPU management. One command shows: GPU model, utilization, memory usage (used/total), temperature, power draw/limit, processes using the GPU, driver/CUDA version, PCIe link speed, ECC status, and compute mode. Flags: -l (continuous monitoring), -q (detailed query), --format=csv (scripting), dmon (device monitoring), pmon (process monitoring)

nvidia-smi is the first tool every AI infrastructure engineer reaches for. It provides an instant snapshot of GPU health and activity. Key flags: `nvidia-smi` (default table view — all GPUs, utilization, memory, temp, power), `nvidia-smi -l 1` (refresh every second — watch GPU behavior in real-time), `nvidia-smi -q -d UTILIZATION,MEMORY` (detailed query on specific metrics), `nvidia-smi dmon` (device monitoring — compact view of SM utilization, memory utilization, temperature, power), `nvidia-smi pmon` (process monitoring — which user/process is using which GPU), and `nvidia-smi --format=csv` (machine-readable output for scripting).

AI Ops Q119 of 200
What does a well-designed GPU monitoring Grafana dashboard include?
A) GPU utilization (SM, memory bandwidth), temperature (per GPU, thermal throttling alerts), power (current vs TDP, total cluster power), ECC errors (trending — increasing errors predict GPU failure), NVLink throughput and errors, per-user GPU allocation, job-level GPU metrics (which job uses which GPUs), and cluster-level aggregate utilization (overall GPU efficiency). Alert thresholds: temp >85°C, ECC double-bit errors >0, GPU utilization <10% for >1hr (idle GPU waste)
B) A single number showing average GPU utilization across the cluster — detailed per-GPU metrics are unnecessary noise
C) Only power consumption and cost — GPU utilization and temperature are secondary concerns for cost-focused operations
D) Grafana cannot monitor GPUs — GPU monitoring requires proprietary NVIDIA tools; Grafana is only for CPU and network monitoring
✅ Correct Answer: A

A good GPU dashboard answers: "Are my GPUs being used efficiently?" at every level — cluster, node, GPU, and job. Cluster level: total GPU hours utilized vs idle, aggregate power consumption, active jobs. Node level: per-node GPU utilization, temperature hot-spots, NVLink health. GPU level: individual GPU SM utilization, memory bandwidth utilization, ECC error count — this is where you catch failing GPUs before they corrupt training runs. Job level: which job/user is using which GPUs, job duration, GPU efficiency (actual TFLOPS vs theoretical). Alerts: idle GPUs (wasting $100/hr), thermal throttling (performance silently degrading), ECC errors (GPU failing).

GPU Dashboard — Key Metrics by Level
  CLUSTER: Total GPU-hrs, utilization%, power MW, jobs running
  NODE: Per-node util, temp hot-spots, NVLink health
  GPU: SM%, Mem BW%, ECC errors, XID faults, temp, power
  JOB: Which job→which GPU, GPU efficiency (actual/theoretical TFLOPS)

AI Ops Q120 of 200
What are XID errors in NVIDIA GPUs, and how should you respond to them?
A) XID errors are user application errors — they indicate bugs in the training code and should be fixed by the AI researcher
B) XID errors are cosmetic warnings — they don't affect GPU performance and can be safely ignored in production
C) XID errors are NVIDIA GPU hardware/software fault codes. Common codes: XID 48 (double-bit ECC error → failing GPU memory, replace GPU), XID 79 (GPU has fallen off the bus → PCIe link failure), XID 13 (graphics engine exception), XID 31 (GPU memory page fault). XID errors indicate real problems — ranging from driver issues to imminent hardware failure. Response depends on the code: some require GPU replacement, others require driver updates or system reboots
D) XID errors are network errors — they indicate InfiniBand link failures between GPU servers, not GPU hardware issues
✅ Correct Answer: C

XID errors are your GPUs crying for help. Each code has a specific meaning: XID 48 (double-bit ECC error) = uncorrectable memory error → GPU memory is failing, replace the GPU. XID 79 (GPU fallen off bus) = PCIe link lost → check PCIe slot, riser, or GPU. XID 31 (memory page fault) = GPU tried to access invalid memory → could be application bug or failing HBM. XID 13 (graphics engine exception) = GPU compute engine fault. Monitoring XID errors in DCGM/Prometheus and alerting on ANY XID > 0 (except benign codes) is critical — a GPU generating XID errors will corrupt training runs with silent data corruption. Proactive GPU replacement based on XID trends prevents catastrophic training failures.

Critical XID Error Codes
  XID 48: Double-bit ECC → Replace GPU (failing HBM)
  XID 79: GPU off bus → Check PCIe slot/riser/GPU seating
  XID 31: Memory page fault → App bug or failing HBM
  XID 13: Graphics exception → Compute engine fault
  RESPONSE: Any XID → investigate immediately. XID 48/79 → schedule GPU replacement.

3.2 GPU Monitoring Deep DiveQ121-Q125

AI Ops Q121 of 200
nvidia-smi shows "GPU-Util: 95%" but your training throughput is low. What does GPU utilization actually measure, and what might be the real bottleneck?
A) GPU-Util measures overall GPU efficiency — 95% means the GPU is performing optimally and the bottleneck must be elsewhere (CPU, network, or storage)
B) GPU-Util measures SM (Streaming Multiprocessor) activity — but not WHAT the SMs are doing. A GPU at 95% SM utilization could be: compute-bound (actually doing math — good), memory-bound (SMs waiting for HBM — bad), or kernel-launch-bound (SMs idle between tiny kernels — bad). Use DCGM's more detailed metrics (SM active %, memory bandwidth utilization %, Tensor Core utilization %) to identify the REAL bottleneck
C) GPU-Util at 95% always means the GPU is fully utilized — any throughput issues are purely due to model architecture and cannot be improved by infrastructure changes
D) GPU-Util measures memory usage percentage — 95% means 95% of HBM is allocated, which indicates the model is too large for the GPU
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU-Util is the most misleading metric in GPU monitoring. It measures whether the SM had ANY active warps in the last sampling period — NOT whether the SM was doing useful work. A GPU at 95% SM util could be: memory-stalled (SMs executing but waiting 200+ cycles for HBM on every instruction), bubble-bound (pipeline parallelism bubbles where GPUs wait for data from previous pipeline stages), or doing tiny inefficient kernels (SM active for 10μs, idle for 100μs — but sampled during the active window). DCGM provides more useful metrics: `DCGM_FI_PROF_SM_ACTIVE` (SMs actually executing), `DCGM_FI_PROF_DRAM_ACTIVE` (HBM bandwidth utilization), and `DCGM_FI_PROF_PCIE_TX_BYTES` (PCIe throughput).

GPU-Util — What It Really Means
  GPU-Util 95% COULD MEAN:
  ✅ Compute-bound: Tensor Cores crunching (good)
  ⚠️ Memory-bound: SMs waiting for HBM (bottleneck = bandwidth)
  ⚠️ Kernel-launch-bound: SMs idle between small kernels (fix: kernel fusion)
  ⚠️ Pipeline bubble: GPU waiting for previous stage (fix: micro-batching)
  USE DCGM detailed metrics to identify the REAL bottleneck
AI Ops Q122 of 200
Your GPU is running at 82°C. The H100 throttling threshold is 85°C. Should you be concerned?
A) No — 82°C is well within safe limits; GPUs are designed to operate at up to 100°C indefinitely
B) Yes — immediately shut down the server; 82°C indicates imminent GPU failure
C) Temperature doesn't matter for GPUs — they have infinite thermal tolerance due to liquid cooling
D) 82°C is approaching the 85°C throttling threshold but is still operational. Monitor the TREND — if temperature is stable at 82°C, cooling is adequate (barely). If temperature is climbing toward 85°C, investigate: airflow obstruction (dust, cable management), cooling system issues (pump failure, low coolant, air in liquid cooling loop), or increased ambient temperature (data center AC failure). At 85°C, the GPU WILL throttle — silently reducing clock speeds and training throughput
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU temperature management is about preventing thermal throttling — the silent performance killer. H100 thermal specifications: maximum operating temperature 85°C (GPU silicon), at which point the GPU begins reducing clock speeds to protect itself. This throttling is GRADUAL — you lose 5-10% clock speed at 85°C, 20-30% at 90°C. For a training job that expects 100 TFLOPS, throttled performance might be 70 TFLOPS — you're paying full GPU price for 70% performance. Good practice: maintain GPU temperature below 80°C under sustained load. Margin to 85°C provides headroom for ambient temperature variations and cooling system degradation. Monitor GPU clock speeds alongside temperature — if clocks drop while temperature is high, throttling is active.

GPU Temperature Management
  H100: Max 85°C → throttling begins (clock speed reduction)
  GOOD: <80°C under sustained load (margin for ambient changes)
  WARNING: 80-85°C (monitor trend, check cooling)
  CRITICAL: >85°C (throttling active, performance degraded silently)
AI Ops Q123 of 200
How do ECC (Error Correcting Code) errors indicate GPU health, and what's the difference between single-bit and double-bit errors?
A) Single-bit ECC errors (SBE) are corrected automatically by the GPU — one flipped bit is detected and fixed. Occasional SBEs are normal (cosmic rays, electrical noise). A sudden increase in SBE rate indicates degrading HBM. Double-bit errors (DBE) CANNOT be corrected — they indicate a serious hardware fault requiring GPU replacement. DBE count >0 is an immediate alert: that GPU is producing incorrect computations (silent data corruption)
B) Both single and double-bit errors are harmless — ECC automatically corrects all errors regardless of bit count with no performance impact
C) Single-bit errors are fatal — any ECC error requires immediate GPU replacement regardless of type or frequency
D) ECC errors only affect gaming graphics — AI training uses FP16/BF16 which is immune to memory errors
✅ Correct Answer: A

ECC errors are the canary in the GPU coal mine. HBM uses ECC to protect against memory errors — each memory word has extra bits that allow error detection and correction. SBE (correctable): one bit flips, ECC detects and fixes it transparently. Normal rate: 0-10 per GPU per day. TRENDING increase → replace GPU (degrading memory cell). DBE (uncorrectable): two or more bits flipped in the same word — ECC detects but CANNOT fix. This means the GPU produced incorrect computation results. In AI training, this translates to corrupted gradients and silently wrong model weights. DBE count >0 = STOP using this GPU, replace immediately. The training run using this GPU may need to be restarted from the last checkpoint before the error.

ECC Errors — GPU Health Indicator
  SINGLE-BIT (SBE): Corrected automatically. 0-10/GPU/day = normal. Increasing → replace.
  DOUBLE-BIT (DBE): Cannot correct. >0 = GPU failure. Silent data corruption in training.
  MONITOR: DCGM ECC error counters. Alert on DBE>0 or SBE rate increase.
AI Ops Q124 of 200
What is GPU virtualization, and what are the three main approaches: GPU passthrough, vGPU, and MIG?
A) GPU virtualization is a myth — GPUs cannot be shared; each user must have exclusive access to an entire physical GPU
B) All three approaches are identical — they all provide the same level of isolation, performance, and flexibility
C) Passthrough: entire GPU assigned to one VM (full performance, no sharing). vGPU (Virtual GPU): GPU time-sliced across multiple VMs using NVIDIA's GRID/vGPU software — good for VDI and light AI. MIG (Multi-Instance GPU): HARDWARE partitioning of A100/H100 into isolated GPU instances — each with dedicated SM, memory, and cache. MIG provides hardware-level isolation (no noisy neighbor), while vGPU provides software-level time-slicing
D) Passthrough is the newest technology — it replaces MIG and vGPU as the recommended approach for all GPU sharing scenarios
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU virtualization enables multiple users/workloads to share GPU resources. Passthrough (PCIe passthrough): the hypervisor assigns an entire physical GPU to a single VM. Full native performance, no sharing overhead. Used for dedicated AI training VMs. vGPU (NVIDIA Virtual GPU): the GPU driver creates virtual GPUs that time-slice the physical GPU. Multiple VMs share one GPU through scheduling. Good for VDI, JupyterHub, and light inference where consistent performance isn't critical. Drawback: software-level isolation — a noisy neighbor can impact others. MIG (Multi-Instance GPU): HARDWARE partitioning. An A100 can be split into up to 7 GPU instances (H100: 7). Each MIG instance has DEDICATED SM slices, memory, L2 cache, and memory bandwidth. Hardware-enforced isolation — no noisy neighbor. Best for multi-tenant inference and small training jobs.

GPU Virtualization Options
  PASSTHROUGH: 1 GPU → 1 VM, full perf, exclusive
  vGPU: 1 GPU → N VMs, software time-slicing, good for VDI
  MIG: 1 GPU → up to 7 instances, HARDWARE isolation, best for multi-tenant AI
AI Ops Q125 of 200
What are MIG profiles on the H100, and how do you choose the right one?
A) MIG profiles are cosmetic labels — all profiles provide identical GPU resources; the naming is for marketing purposes
B) H100 MIG profiles define how GPU resources are partitioned: 1g.20gb (1/7 compute, 20GB HBM), 2g.20gb, 3g.40gb, 4g.40gb, 7g.80gb (full GPU). The "g" is compute slices (out of 7), GB is dedicated HBM. Choose based on workload: 1g.20gb for small inference, 3g.40gb for medium models, 7g.80gb for full training. Mixing profiles is possible: e.g., 2× 3g.40gb + 1× 1g.20gb on a single H100
C) MIG profiles only differ in display resolution — they're for configuring monitor outputs, not GPU compute resources
D) MIG is a cloud-only feature — it's only available on NVIDIA's DGX Cloud platform, not on physical GPUs
✅ Correct Answer: B

MIG profiles are the recipe for GPU partitioning. An H100 has 7 compute slices (SMs partitioned 1:7, 2:7, 3:7, 4:7, or 7:7) and 80GB HBM (partitioned accordingly). Available profiles: 1g.20gb (smallest), 2g.20gb, 3g.40gb, 4g.40gb, 7g.80gb (full GPU). Profile selection: 1g.20gb for small BERT inference (~440MB model), 2g.20gb for medium models, 3g.40gb for Llama-7B inference, 7g.80gb for training or large model inference. Mixed configurations: 2× 3g.40gb + 1× 1g.20gb (total: 7 slices, 100GB — but HBM is 80GB so the last profile gets less). MIG requires A100 or H100 (not available on L40S, L4, or consumer GPUs).

H100 MIG Profiles
  1g.20gb: 1/7 compute + 20GB → Small inference (BERT)
  2g.20gb: 2/7 compute + 20GB → Medium inference
  3g.40gb: 3/7 compute + 40GB → Llama-7B inference
  4g.40gb: 4/7 compute + 40GB → Balanced
  7g.80gb: Full GPU → Training, large model inference
  MIX: 2×3g.40gb + 1×1g.20gb on one H100 (total must not exceed 7 slices)

3.3 AI Cluster Orchestration & Job SchedulingQ126-Q127

AI Ops Q126 of 200
How does Slurm schedule GPU jobs, and what does `#SBATCH --gres=gpu:H100:8` mean?
A) Slurm cannot schedule GPUs — it's a CPU-only scheduler; GPU management requires Kubernetes
B) The --gres flag is optional metadata — Slurm ignores it and assigns GPUs randomly to any job
C) --gres=gpu:H100:8 means the job requests 8 H100 GPUs from any nodes — Slurm will assign any available H100 GPUs regardless of which node they're on, even splitting across nodes
D) --gres=gpu:H100:8 requests exactly 8 H100 GPUs as a Generic Resource. Slurm's GRS (Generic Resource Scheduling) tracks GPU types and counts per node. It will find a node (or nodes) with 8 available H100 GPUs, allocate them exclusively to this job, and set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES so the job only sees its assigned GPUs. When the job finishes, GPUs are released
✅ Correct Answer: D

Slurm GPU scheduling uses Generic Resource Scheduling (GRES). Configuration: each node defines its GPU resources in slurm.conf (`GresTypes=gpu`, `NodeName=dgx[01-10] Gres=gpu:H100:8`). Job submission: `#SBATCH --gres=gpu:H100:8 --nodes=1` (request 8 H100s on 1 node). Slurm tracks: which GPUs are allocated, to which job, for how long. Environment: Slurm sets CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES and GPU_DEVICE_ORDINAL so the job sees only its assigned GPUs (GPU 0-7 in the container map to physical GPUs 24-31 on the node, for example). Exclusivity: GPUs allocated to one job cannot be used by another. Release: GPUs freed when job completes or reaches time limit.

Slurm GPU Scheduling
  #SBATCH --gres=gpu:H100:8 --nodes=1 --time=48:00:00 --partition=dgx
  Slurm finds node with 8 free H100s → allocates → sets CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES
  Job runs with exclusive GPU access → completes → GPUs released

Without it, each of these components must be manually installed and maintained on every node — GPU Operator makes GPU K8s clusters manageable at scale
B) GPU Operator is a GPU hardware management tool — it physically installs and removes GPUs from server PCIe slots using robotic arms
C) GPU Operator is an AI model deployment tool — it converts PyTorch models to TensorRT and deploys them as Kubernetes services
D) GPU Operator is a cloud billing tool — it tracks GPU usage per Kubernetes namespace for chargeback and cost allocation

GPU Operator is the bridge between Kubernetes and NVIDIA GPUs. The operator pattern: a Kubernetes controller that watches for desired state and reconciles actual state. GPU Operator manages: NVIDIA driver (installs correct driver version per GPU model), container runtime (nvidia-container-toolkit for GPU access from containers), K8s device plugin (advertises GPU resources to the scheduler), DCGM exporter (monitoring metrics), and MIG manager (configures MIG partitions). Benefits: automated driver lifecycle (upgrade drivers across 100 nodes with one command), consistent configuration (every node gets identical GPU software stack), and day-2 operations (monitoring, health checks, auto-remediation). Part of NVIDIA AI Enterprise.

GPU Operator — Automated GPU Lifecycle on K8s
  GPU OPERATOR manages: Driver | Container Runtime | Device Plugin | DCGM | MIG

3.4 Virtualizing Accelerated InfrastructureQ128-Q135

AI Ops Q128 of 200
When should you use MIG vs vGPU vs GPU passthrough for AI workloads?
A) Always use passthrough — sharing GPUs is never appropriate for production AI workloads
B) MIG for multi-tenant AI inference (hardware isolation, predictable performance, dedicated memory per instance). vGPU for VDI and light workloads where hardware isolation isn't critical (time-slicing, software-level sharing). Passthrough for dedicated training jobs needing full GPU performance (no sharing overhead). Kubernetes: MIG via GPU Operator for inference, passthrough for training. Cloud: use MIG profiles to pack multiple inference models onto one GPU efficiently
C) vGPU is always superior to MIG because software is more flexible than hardware — hardware partitioning is an outdated approach
D) All three provide identical performance and isolation — choice is purely a matter of licensing cost
✅ Correct Answer: B

The GPU virtualization decision matrix: MIG (hardware partitioning) → best for production multi-tenant AI. Hardware-enforced isolation means one tenant's workload can't impact another's. Predictable performance (dedicated SM slices and memory bandwidth). A100: up to 7 instances. H100: up to 7 instances. vGPU (software time-slicing) → best for VDI, JupyterHub, light development. GPUs are time-shared — a noisy neighbor CAN impact others. Lower cost than MIG (no hardware requirement beyond GPU). Passthrough → best for training, large model inference, or any workload needing the full GPU. Zero virtualization overhead, full performance. PCIe passthrough or GPU Operator with full GPU allocation.

GPU Virtualization Decision Matrix
  WORKLOAD              BEST CHOICE    WHY
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Multi-tenant infer.   MIG            HW isolation, predictable perf
  Training (any size)   Passthrough    Full GPU, zero overhead
  VDI / JupyterHub      vGPU           Software sharing, cost-effective
  Large model infer.    Passthrough    Needs full GPU memory/bandwidth
  Small model infer.    MIG            Pack multiple models on 1 GPU
AI Ops Q129 of 200
How does GPU time-slicing work in Kubernetes for sharing GPUs among pods?
A) Kubernetes cannot share GPUs — each pod must claim an entire physical GPU exclusively
B) GPU time-slicing uses MIG hardware to split GPUs — it requires A100 or H100 GPUs
C) GPU time-slicing uses the NVIDIA device plugin's time-slicing configuration to create multiple "virtual" GPUs from one physical GPU — pods get time-sliced access. Unlike MIG (hardware isolation), time-slicing shares the GPU at the CUDA level with software scheduling — no memory or fault isolation between pods. Good for development and burst workloads; not recommended for production where predictable performance matters
D) Time-slicing is a GPU overclocking technique — it runs the GPU at higher clock speeds for short bursts to improve training throughput
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU time-slicing is the simplest GPU sharing mechanism in Kubernetes. The NVIDIA device plugin can be configured with time-slicing parameters: `time-slicing: { replicas: 4 }` makes 1 physical GPU appear as 4 virtual GPUs. Pods request one of these virtual GPUs and the CUDA time-slicing scheduler multiplexes them. Key limitation: NO memory isolation — if Pod A allocates 30GB, Pod B can only use the remaining 50GB (on 80GB GPU). NO fault isolation — if Pod A's CUDA kernel crashes, it may impact Pod B. NO performance isolation — a compute-intensive pod impacts others. MIG solves all three with hardware partitioning. Time-slicing is appropriate for development, CI/CD, and non-production workloads.

Time-Slicing vs MIG on Kubernetes
  TIME-SLICING: 1 GPU → N "virtual" GPUs, software sharing, no isolation
  MIG: 1 GPU → up to 7 isolated instances, hardware partition, full isolation
  K8s: GPU Operator manages both. Time-slicing for dev, MIG for production.
AI Ops Q130 of 200
What is the NVIDIA GPU Operator's MIG manager, and how does it automate MIG configuration?
A) MIG manager is a physical device — a PCIe card that plugs into servers to enable MIG on non-A100/H100 GPUs
B) MIG manager requires manual configuration — an administrator must SSH into each node and run nvidia-smi mig commands to configure profiles
C) MIG manager only works with vGPU — it cannot manage hardware MIG partitions
D) MIG manager is a component of GPU Operator that automates MIG lifecycle: it applies MIG configuration from a ConfigMap (`mig-config`), creates GPU instances and compute instances on each node, labels nodes with available MIG profiles, and handles reconfiguration when nodes join/leave. Admins define desired MIG layout once; MIG manager ensures every node matches
✅ Correct Answer: D

MIG manager makes GPU partitioning operationally feasible at scale. Without it: admin must manually run `nvidia-smi mig -cgi` on every node, verify configuration, and redo on node reboot. With MIG manager: define desired configuration in a ConfigMap, label nodes, apply — GPU Operator reconciles. ConfigMap example: `{ "version": 1, "mig-configs": { "all-1g.20gb": { "devices": "all", "mig-enabled": true, "mig-devices": { "1g.20gb": 7 } } } }`. This creates 7 MIG instances of 1g.20gb on every GPU. MIG manager handles: GPU reset before reconfiguration, MIG enable/disable, profile application, and node labeling for scheduler awareness.

MIG Manager — Automated GPU Partitioning
  ADMIN: Define MIG config in ConfigMap → Apply
  GPU OPERATOR (MIG Manager): Reconcile → Reset GPU → Apply MIG profiles → Label node
  K8s SCHEDULER: See MIG resources → Schedule pods to matching MIG instances
AI Ops Q131 of 200
What is GPU resource management best practice — when should you use MIG, vGPU, passthrough, or time-slicing?
A) Production inference (multi-tenant): MIG (hardware isolation). Production training: passthrough (full GPU). Development/CI: time-slicing (easiest, low cost). VDI/remote workstations: vGPU (GPU sharing for non-AI). Cloud/K8s GPU-as-a-Service: MIG for inference tiers, passthrough for training tiers. The key principle: production workloads need predictable performance → MIG or passthrough. Non-production can tolerate variability → time-slicing or vGPU
B) Always use passthrough for all workloads — simplicity is more important than GPU utilization efficiency
C) Always use time-slicing for all workloads — maximum GPU sharing maximizes ROI regardless of performance predictability
D) GPU resource management is automatic — the GPU driver automatically selects the best sharing mode based on workload characteristics
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU resource management is about matching the sharing technology to the workload's performance requirements. The spectrum: Passthrough (best performance, worst sharing) → MIG (good performance, good sharing, hardware isolation) → vGPU (moderate performance, good sharing, software isolation) → Time-slicing (variable performance, best sharing, no isolation). For a GPU cloud service: offer "dedicated GPU" tier (passthrough, premium pricing) and "shared GPU" tier (MIG, standard pricing). For an enterprise AI platform: training cluster (passthrough), inference cluster (MIG), development cluster (time-slicing). The right choice depends on the workload's sensitivity to: performance predictability, memory isolation, fault isolation, and cost.

GPU Sharing — Performance vs Isolation Spectrum
  PASSTHROUGH → MIG → vGPU → TIME-SLICING
  Best perf, no sharing → HW isolated → SW shared → No isolation
  Training, large infer → Multi-tenant infer → VDI → Dev/CI
AI Ops Q132 of 200
What is GPU allocation in Slurm vs Kubernetes — how do they differ in approach?
A) Slurm and Kubernetes use identical GPU allocation mechanisms — both use the same device plugin and scheduling model
B) Slurm: GPUs are allocated as Generic Resources (GRES) — exclusive, job-scoped allocation with topology awareness (NVLink affinity). K8s: GPUs are allocated via device plugin as extended resources (`nvidia.com/gpu`) — pod-scoped, supports MIG and time-slicing. Slurm is batch-oriented (jobs request GPUs, run, release). K8s is service-oriented (pods claim GPUs, can run indefinitely, support scaling). Slurm excels at HPC-style scheduling with queue priorities and fair-share; K8s excels at container orchestration with auto-scaling and self-healing
C) Slurm cannot allocate GPUs — it's a CPU-only scheduler; Kubernetes is the only orchestrator that supports GPU allocation
D) Slurm and Kubernetes both allocate GPUs but neither supports MIG or GPU sharing — exclusive GPU access is the only mode on both platforms
✅ Correct Answer: B

Slurm and K8s represent different GPU allocation philosophies. Slurm: GRES-based, job-scoped, exclusive allocation. CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES set by Slurm. GPU affinity: Slurm can optimize for NVLink topology (keep GPUs on same NVSwitch together). K8s: device-plugin based, pod-scoped. Supports MIG-aware scheduling (pods request `nvidia.com/mig-1g.20gb`). Supports GPU sharing via time-slicing. Slurm's strength: sophisticated queue management (priority, fair-share, preemption, backfill). K8s's strength: service-level management (auto-restart, scaling, rolling updates, service discovery). Many organizations use both: Slurm for training jobs, K8s for inference services.

Slurm vs K8s GPU Allocation
  SLURM: sbatch --gres=gpu:H100:8 → Job claims 8 GPUs → runs → releases
  K8S: container requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1 → Pod claims GPU → runs continuously
  SLURM: Batch, exclusive, topology-aware | K8S: Service, shareable, auto-scaling

B) Run:ai replaces Slurm and Kubernetes entirely — it's a complete replacement orchestrator with its own scheduler
C) Run:ai is a GPU orchestration platform that sits on top of Kubernetes — it provides: GPU fractioning (dynamic MIG and time-slicing), fair-share scheduling across teams, GPU quota management, workload prioritization, and GPU utilization analytics. Dashboards show: GPU utilization by team/project, queue wait times, and cost allocation. Acquired by NVIDIA in 2024.

Run:ai — GPU Orchestration Layer
  Run:ai ABOVE K8s/Slurm: GPU pooling, fair-share, quotas, fractional sharing

AI Ops Q134 of 200
How do you monitor job-level GPU utilization — tracking which job uses which GPU and how efficiently?
A) Job-level GPU monitoring is impossible — you can only see aggregate per-GPU metrics
B) Use nvidia-smi pmon once and write down the results — periodic manual checks are sufficient for production
C) Cloud providers handle job-level monitoring automatically — on-premises clusters cannot track GPU-to-job mapping
D) DCGM monitors which processes/PIDs use each GPU. Slurm logs map job IDs to GPU allocations. Combine: DCGM metrics (GPU util, memory, power per PID) + Slurm accounting (sacct — job ID, user, GPU count, runtime). This data enables: chargeback (bill team A for their GPU usage), right-sizing (job only needs 4 GPUs, not 8), and idle detection (GPU allocated but 0% utilization → job hung).

Job-Level GPU Monitoring
  DCGM (per-process) + Slurm (job→GPU mapping) → Prometheus → Grafana
  Metrics: GPU util%, memory efficiency, GPU-hrs, actual vs theoretical TFLOPS

What is capacity planning for GPU infrastructure, and how do you forecast future needs?

D) Capacity planning is purely about counting GPUs — utilization trends and workload analysis are unnecessary

Key data sources: DCGM/Prometheus utilization trends (peak utilization should be <85% to leave headroom), Slurm queue metrics (sacct — average queue wait time; increasing wait = capacity shortage), GPU-hour consumption by team/project (who's growing fastest?), model size trajectory (are teams adopting larger models that need more GPUs?), and known pipeline (new model training scheduled for Q3, new team joining in Q4). The demand signal: when average GPU utilization exceeds 75% or queue wait times exceed 4 hours, it's time to order more GPUs.

GPU Capacity Planning
  DATA: DCGM trends, Slurm queue wait, GPU-hr/team, model size growth
  FORECAST: Linear regression + step-function (new projects)

3.5 AI Operations Best PracticesQ136-Q150

AI Ops Q136 of 200
How should you handle a GPU that shows increasing ECC single-bit errors over several weeks?
A) Ignore it — single-bit errors are automatically corrected and have no impact on training accuracy or GPU longevity
B) Track the trend and proactively schedule GPU replacement. An increasing SBE rate (e.g., 5/day → 50/day → 500/day) indicates degrading HBM that will eventually produce uncorrectable double-bit errors. Proactive replacement during a maintenance window prevents unexpected training failures and silent data corruption. DCGM health checks can automate this detection
C) Immediately shut down the entire server — any ECC error increase indicates imminent catastrophic GPU failure
D) Increase GPU clock speeds to compensate — higher clocks can overcome memory errors through overvoltage
✅ Correct Answer: B

ECC error trending is one of the most valuable GPU health indicators. A stable SBE rate (5-20/day) is normal — cosmic rays and electrical noise cause occasional bit flips. An increasing trend signals physical HBM degradation: a memory cell is weakening and will eventually fail. The progression: occasional SBE → frequent SBE → DBE (double-bit, uncorrectable). At DBE, the GPU produces wrong computation results — corrupting training runs. Proactive replacement at the increasing-SBE stage prevents: unexpected training failures (job crashes mid-run), silent data corruption (wrong gradients → wrong model), and emergency maintenance (replacing GPU at 2AM vs planned window).

ECC Error Lifecycle — When to Replace
  STABLE (5-20/day): Normal operation — monitor
  INCREASING (20→100/day): Schedule replacement within 2 weeks
  RAPID (>200/day): Replace within 48 hours — DBE imminent
  DBE DETECTED (>0): GPU already producing wrong results — stop using immediately
AI Ops Q137 of 200
How do you handle GPU driver and CUDA toolkit updates across a cluster of 100+ GPU nodes?
A) SSH into each node manually and run the installer — systematic automation is unnecessary for driver updates
B) Never update GPU drivers — the original factory-installed driver version should be maintained indefinitely for stability
C) Use standard OS package managers (apt/yum) — NVIDIA drivers integrate seamlessly with OS package management
D) Use GPU Operator (K8s) or configuration management (Ansible, Slurm with automated scripts) to manage driver/CUDA lifecycle: drain node (cordon, drain running jobs), update driver/CUDA, reboot, validate (nvidia-smi, DCGM health check), uncordon. Staged rollout: 10% of nodes → validate 24hrs → 50% → validate → 100%. Always test on a canary node first
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU driver management at scale requires automation and caution. A bad driver update can render 100 GPUs unusable simultaneously. Process: canary deployment (1 node, test all workloads for 24hrs), staged rollout (10% → validate → 50% → validate → 100%), drain before update (move jobs off the node — Slurm: `scontrol update NodeName=dgx01 State=DRAIN` or K8s: `kubectl drain`), update (driver installer or GPU Operator), reboot (GPU driver updates require reboot), validate (nvidia-smi, DCGM health check, run a test training job), uncordon. Rollback plan: have previous driver version available — if issues detected, roll back within the 10% phase. GPU Operator handles this for K8s.

GPU Driver Update Process
  CANARY (1 node, 24hr) → 10% (test) → 50% (validate) → 100% (complete)
  Each step: Drain → Update → Reboot → Validate → Uncordon
  ROLLBACK PLAN: Keep previous driver version available
AI Ops Q138 of 200
What is NVIDIA NSight Systems, and how does it help optimize AI workload performance?
A) NSight Systems is a system-wide performance profiling tool — it captures: GPU kernels (which kernels run when, duration, occupancy), CPU-GPU synchronization (where the CPU waits for GPU and vice versa), memory operations (HBM and PCIe transfers), NVLink activity, and NCCL communication patterns. It visualizes the entire application timeline, revealing bottlenecks: CPU-bound (GPU idle waiting for CPU data loading), GPU-bound (kernels too slow), or communication-bound (NCCL overhead dominating)
B) NSight Systems is a GPU temperature monitoring tool — it creates thermal maps of GPU die hotspots during training
C) NSight Systems is an AI model architecture search tool — it automatically designs optimal neural network architectures
D) NSight Systems is a cloud cost optimization tool — it analyzes GPU cloud spending and recommends cheaper instance types
✅ Correct Answer: A

NSight Systems is the profiler for answering "why is my training slow?" It captures a timeline of everything happening on the GPU system: CUDA kernels (green blocks — GPU computing), memory copies (red blocks — HBM↔CPU transfers), CPU threads (blue — data loading, Python), and NCCL communication (purple — gradient sync). The timeline reveals: GPU starvation (long gaps between GPU kernels = CPU is the bottleneck), small kernel overhead (many tiny kernels with launch overhead between them = kernel fusion needed), NCCL dominance (more time in communication than computation = network bottleneck), and idle time (GPU idle for extended periods = scheduling or data loading issue).

NSight Systems Timeline Analysis
  GREEN (GPU kernels): Good — GPU is computing
  RED (memory copies): Overhead — minimize CPU↔GPU transfers
  BLUE (CPU): If between greens → CPU bottleneck (data loading, python)
  PURPLE (NCCL): If dominant → network bottleneck in distributed training
  GAPS (white space): GPU idle → investigate why
AI Ops Q139 of 200
What is centralized logging for GPU clusters, and why is it essential for troubleshooting?
A) Centralized logging copies GPU driver logs to a USB drive on each server — manual collection is sufficient
B) GPU clusters don't need logging — nvidia-smi provides real-time data and historical logs are irrelevant
C) Centralized logging aggregates logs from all GPU nodes into platforms like ELK (Elasticsearch, Logstash, Kibana) or Loki/Grafana. It captures: GPU XID errors (which GPU, when, what code), driver messages, CUDA errors, Slurm job logs, NCCL debug output, and system logs. Essential because GPU failures are rare but catastrophic — when training crashes at 2AM, you need to search across 100 nodes instantly to find which GPU caused it and why
D) Centralized logging is handled by nvidia-smi automatically — no additional tools are needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU cluster logging answers "what happened and when?" When a 1,024-GPU training job crashes at 3AM with "NCCL timeout," you need to identify: which GPU(s) caused the timeout, which node(s), what was happening before the crash (XID errors, ECC errors, thermal events). Without centralized logging: SSH into 128 nodes, grep individual log files — takes hours. With centralized logging (ELK/Loki): search `XID OR NCCL timeout` across all nodes in seconds, correlate events by timestamp, identify root cause in minutes. Key log sources: dmesg (kernel/NVIDIA driver), DCGM (GPU health), Slurm (job accounting), NCCL debug output (communication failures), and syslog (system events).

GPU Cluster Centralized Logging
  NODE LOGS → Logstash/Promtail → Elasticsearch/Loki → Kibana/Grafana
  SEARCH: "XID 48" across all 128 nodes in seconds
  CORRELATE: GPU failure event at 03:14:22 → NCCL timeout at 03:14:23 → Job crash
AI Ops Q140 of 200
What is cluster provisioning for AI, and how do you automate installing OS + NVIDIA drivers + CUDA on 100+ nodes?
A) Manual USB installation per node — 100 USBs for 100 nodes, installed one at a time by a technician
B) Use infrastructure-as-code and provisioning tools: MAAS/Metal for bare-metal provisioning (PXE boot, OS install), Ansible/Puppet for configuration management (NVIDIA drivers, CUDA, cuDNN, Docker, Slurm), and GPU Operator for K8s GPU stack. Golden image approach: create a validated image with all GPU software pre-installed and tested, then clone to all nodes. Provisioning time: <1 hour for 100 nodes with PXE boot + golden image
C) Cloud-only — on-premises GPU clusters cannot be automated; each node requires manual setup
D) GPU clusters self-provision — modern GPUs include firmware that automatically downloads and installs all required software
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU cluster provisioning is the foundation — get this wrong and nothing else works reliably. The stack: bare-metal provisioning (PXE boot, MAAS/Metal/Canonical MAAS → install OS via network boot), configuration management (Ansible playbooks: NVIDIA driver blacklist nouveau, install CUDA 12.x, cuDNN 8.x, Docker, Slurm/NCCL), validation (run DCGM diagnostics, NCCL bandwidth test, training smoke test), and GPU Operator for K8s environments (automates the GPU software stack within Kubernetes). Golden image: build one node perfectly, capture image, deploy to all nodes — ensures identical configuration (no "works on node 5 but not node 23" issues).

GPU Cluster Provisioning Stack
  PXE BOOT → OS install → Ansible (GPU drivers, CUDA, cuDNN, Docker, Slurm)
  → Validation (DCGM diag, NCCL test, smoke training) → Ready for jobs
  Golden image: Build once, deploy everywhere. <1hr for 100 nodes.
AI Ops Q141 of 200
What is HA (High Availability) design for AI clusters, and which components need redundancy?
A) AI clusters don't need HA — if a GPU node fails, training resumes from checkpoint on another node
B) HA only applies to storage — GPU compute nodes are inherently unreliable and can't be made redundant
C) HA means buying duplicate GPUs — for every H100, buy a spare H100 sitting idle as a hot standby
D) HA for AI clusters focuses on infrastructure services, not GPU compute (which is inherently ephemeral): redundant Slurm controller (active/passive), redundant management network switches, redundant storage (RAID, replication), redundant power (dual PSUs, dual PDUs, UPS + generator), and redundant networking (dual InfiniBand fabrics). GPU compute nodes are NOT redundant — jobs checkpoint and restart on other nodes
✅ Correct Answer: D

AI cluster HA is about keeping the cluster operational, not keeping individual GPU jobs running. GPU training jobs are inherently batch — they expect to checkpoint and potentially restart. HA protects: Slurm controller (if controller dies, no new jobs can be submitted — active/passive failover via shared storage or database replication), storage (data loss is catastrophic — RAID + replication + backups), networking (leaf/spine switches in pairs — if one switch fails, bandwidth degrades but cluster remains operational), and power (dual PSUs per server, dual PDUs, UPS + generator). GPU compute nodes: no redundancy needed — a failed GPU is cordoned off and replaced; jobs that were using it checkpoint and restart.

AI Cluster HA Design
  REDUNDANT: Slurm controller, storage, networking, power
  NOT REDUNDANT: GPU compute nodes (batch jobs checkpoint + restart)
  PRINCIPLE: Keep the cluster running; let individual jobs recover
AI Ops Q142 of 200
What is the role of Prometheus and Grafana in GPU cluster monitoring?
A) DCGM-Exporter exposes GPU metrics in Prometheus format → Prometheus scrapes and stores time-series data → Grafana visualizes and alerts. This is the standard open-source monitoring stack for GPU clusters. Prometheus handles metric collection, storage, and alerting rules (e.g., GPU temperature >85°C for 5 minutes → page on-call). Grafana provides dashboards (cluster overview, per-node detail, job-level GPU usage)
B) Prometheus and Grafana are NVIDIA tools — they're proprietary NVIDIA software bundled with DGX systems
C) Prometheus replaces DCGM — it directly reads GPU telemetry without needing DCGM as an intermediary
D) Prometheus is a job scheduler — it allocates GPU resources to training jobs with priority-based queuing
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus + Grafana is the de facto standard for GPU cluster monitoring. The pipeline: DCGM collects GPU metrics → DCGM-Exporter converts to Prometheus format (exposes HTTP endpoint /metrics) → Prometheus scrapes every 15-60 seconds → Grafana queries Prometheus (PromQL) and renders dashboards. Alertmanager: Prometheus evaluates alerting rules (e.g., `DCGM_FI_DEV_ECC_DBE_COUNT > 0`) → Alertmanager routes to PagerDuty/Slack/email. Key dashboards: Cluster Overview (total GPU utilization, active jobs, power MW), Node Deep Dive (per-GPU metrics, thermal map, ECC trends), Job View (which job uses which GPUs, GPU efficiency), and Cost (GPU-hours consumed per team/project).

Prometheus + Grafana for GPU Monitoring
  DCGM → DCGM-Exporter → Prometheus (metrics DB + alerting) → Grafana (dashboards)
  Alertmanager → PagerDuty/Slack on: GPU temp>85°C, ECC DBE>0, GPU idle>1hr
AI Ops Q143 of 200
What GPU metrics indicate a "healthy" vs "degraded" vs "failing" GPU?
A) All GPUs showing any utilization are healthy — metrics cannot distinguish between healthy and failing GPUs
B) Only GPU temperature matters — if temperature is below 85°C, the GPU is healthy regardless of other metrics
C) Healthy: stable temperature <80°C under load, ECC SBE <20/day stable, no XID errors, NVLink CRC errors <100/hr, clock speeds at expected values. Degraded: increasing ECC SBE trend, occasional XID 13/31, NVLink retries increasing, clock speed fluctuating. Failing: DBE >0, XID 48/79, NVLink down, temperature >85°C with throttling, GPU falling off PCIe bus
D) GPU health is binary — it either works or doesn't; there's no "degraded" state
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU health is a spectrum, and catching degradation early prevents catastrophic failures. The key is trending: a single XID 13 might be a fluke, but 5 per day is a pattern. ECC SBE at 50/day and climbing is worse than 200/day stable (which might be normal for that GPU model and workload). The most reliable failing indicators: DBE (immediate — GPU is producing wrong results), XID 48 (GPU memory hardware failure), XID 79 (GPU has fallen off the PCIe bus — complete disconnection), and thermal throttling while temperature is below threshold (indicates cooling system issue, not GPU failure). DCGM health checks automate this assessment.

GPU Health Spectrum
  HEALTHY: Stable temp, low ECC, no XID, full clock speeds
  DEGRADED: Trending ECC increase, occasional XID, clock fluctuations
  FAILING: DBE>0, XID 48/79, NVLink down, thermal throttle, PCIe disconnect
  ACTION: Healthy=monitor, Degraded=schedule replacement, Failing=replace NOW
AI Ops Q144 of 200
How do you handle GPU clock speeds that fluctuate during training?
A) Fluctuating clock speeds are always a sign of GPU failure — replace the GPU immediately
B) GPU clock speeds dynamically adjust based on thermal headroom, power limits, and workload. SM clock and memory clock are managed independently. Fluctuations are normal if within expected range. Check: thermal throttling (temp >85°C → clock drops), power throttling (reaching TDP limit → clock drops), and application clocks vs base clocks (boost clocks are opportunistic). Use `nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK` to see current vs max clocks and throttling reasons
C) GPU clock speeds should always be fixed at maximum — any fluctuation indicates a driver bug requiring reinstallation
D) Clock speeds don't affect AI performance — GPUs deliver the same TFLOPS regardless of clock frequency
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU clock management is dynamic and adaptive. H100 SM clock range: base ~1.1 GHz, boost up to ~1.98 GHz. Memory clock: typically fixed at rated speed (HBM3). Clocks fluctuate for three reasons: thermal (GPU protecting itself — reduce clock to reduce heat), power (hitting the 700W TDP limit — reduce clock to stay within power budget), and workload (GPU boosts when compute-bound, relaxes when idle). To diagnose: `nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK` shows current clocks, max clocks, and throttling reasons. If clocks are consistently below base clock, investigate cooling (temperature too high) or power delivery (PSU insufficient). Application clocks can be set with `nvidia-smi -ac` for consistent performance in benchmarking.

GPU Clock Management
  nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK → Shows current/max clocks + throttling reasons
  THERMAL: Temp>85°C → clock drops | POWER: TDP reached → clock drops
  NORMAL: Boost clocks are opportunistic | FIX: Better cooling or power delivery
AI Ops Q145 of 200
How do you track which user or process is using each GPU?
A) GPU process tracking is impossible — GPUs don't expose process-level information
B) Ask users to manually report their GPU usage via email at the end of each day
C) Use the GPU serial number to match with purchase records — each GPU is assigned to one user permanently
D) `nvidia-smi pmon` shows which processes are using which GPUs in real-time (PID, process name, GPU memory). DCGM tracks per-process GPU utilization and memory usage over time. Slurm records job-to-GPU mapping (sacct -j JOBID --format=AllocTRES). GPU Operator labels K8s pods with GPU allocation. Combine for full audit trail: which user/job/pod used which GPU, when, and how efficiently
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU process tracking is essential for accountability and troubleshooting. When GPU utilization spikes at 3AM: who caused it? `nvidia-smi pmon` shows current PIDs and GPU memory usage. DCGM provides historical per-process metrics. Slurm: `sacct -j JOBID --format=User,Account,AllocTRES,Elapsed` shows which user/job used which GPUs. K8s: GPU Operator annotates pods with GPU allocation. Combine: Slurm/K8s tells you WHO had the GPU; DCGM tells you HOW they used it. Use case: "GPU utilization was 0% for 4 hours but the GPU was allocated to job 54321" → job was hung → alert user or kill job.

GPU Process Tracking
  nvidia-smi pmon: Current PID, GPU memory per process
  DCGM: Historical per-process GPU utilization and memory
  Slurm: sacct → User, JobID, GPUs allocated, runtime
  K8s: GPU Operator → pod↔GPU mapping
AI Ops Q146 of 200
What is the difference between interactive jobs (srun --pty) and batch jobs (sbatch) in Slurm for AI workloads?
A) Interactive (srun --pty bash): allocates GPUs immediately and gives you a shell — used for debugging, Jupyter notebooks, and exploratory work. The GPU is allocated until you exit. Batch (sbatch script.sh): submits a job script to the queue — runs when resources are available. Interactive ties up GPUs even when idle; batch maximizes cluster utilization by queueing. Best practice: interactive for development (<2hrs), batch for production training
B) Interactive and batch are identical — they both submit to the queue and run when resources become available
C) Interactive jobs don't use GPUs — they're CPU-only for code development; GPU access requires batch submission
D) Slurm doesn't support interactive GPU jobs — only Kubernetes supports interactive GPU sessions
✅ Correct Answer: A

Interactive vs batch is about workflow, not capability. Interactive (`srun --gres=gpu:H100:1 --pty bash`): allocates 1 H100 immediately (or queues briefly if unavailable), opens a shell. You can run Python, test code, launch Jupyter. The GPU stays allocated until you `exit` — even if you're reading documentation for 30 minutes. This is GPU waste. Batch (`sbatch train.sh`): script runs when scheduled, executes, releases GPUs. Best utilization because GPUs are only held during actual computation. Many clusters enforce time limits on interactive jobs (max 2-4 hours) and lower priority than batch jobs to discourage GPU squatting.

Interactive vs Batch GPU Jobs
  INTERACTIVE (srun --pty): GPU allocated NOW, shell access, for debugging
  BATCH (sbatch): Queued, runs when scheduled, best GPU utilization
  TIME LIMITS: Interactive <2-4hrs (lower priority) | Batch up to days (higher priority)
