What is Slurm job accounting, and what critical information does it provide?
A) Job accounting is manual timesheets — users report their GPU usage to administrators weekly
B) Job accounting only tracks job duration — GPU allocation details are not recorded
C) Slurm job accounting (via slurmdbd) records every job: user, account, job ID, submit time, start time, end time, exit code, GPUs allocated, CPU/memory requested, and node list. `sacct` queries this database. Essential for: chargeback (bill departments for GPU usage), utilization analysis (which teams use the most?), capacity planning (trending GPU demand), and troubleshooting (was the job running when the GPU failed?)
D) Job accounting is handled by Prometheus — Slurm has no built-in job tracking capability
✅ Correct Answer: C

Slurm job accounting is the source of truth for GPU cluster usage. The database (slurmdbd) stores complete job records. Key queries: `sacct -S 2024-01-01 -E 2024-01-31 -o User,Account,AllocTRES,Elapsed` (January GPU usage by user), `sacct -X -o Account,AllocTRES --format=Elapsed` (total GPU-hours by account, excluding job steps), and `sacct -j JOBID --format=AllocTRES,NodeList,ExitCode` (what GPUs did job X use, on which nodes, did it succeed?). For chargeback: sum GPU-hours × $/GPU-hour rate per department. For troubleshooting: find all jobs running on a node when a GPU failed — notify affected users.

Slurm Job Accounting
  sacct queries: User, Account, GPU-hrs, runtime, exit code, nodelist
  USE: Chargeback ($/GPU-hr), utilization analysis, capacity planning, troubleshooting
AI Ops Q148 of 200
What is Slurm fair-share scheduling, and how does it prevent GPU resource hogging?
A) Fair-share means every user gets exactly equal GPU time regardless of usage — all users have the same priority
B) Fair-share adjusts job priority based on historical usage compared to allocated share. If Team A has 40% share but used 60% of GPU-hours recently, their priority DECREASES. If Team B has 20% share but only used 5%, their priority INCREASES. This ensures long-term fairness: heavy users get deprioritized, allowing light users to get their fair share. Formula: Priority = (Allocated Share / Recent Usage) × Job factors
C) Fair-share means GPUs are literally divided and physically allocated — each team gets their own dedicated GPU servers
D) Fair-share is a manual process — administrators review usage reports weekly and manually adjust user priorities
✅ Correct Answer: B

Fair-share is the mechanism that prevents "the team with the most GPU-hungry researchers from taking all the GPUs." Configuration: each account/team gets a share allocation (e.g., Team A: 40 shares, Team B: 30 shares, Team C: 30 shares). Slurm tracks historical usage over a decay period (typically 2-4 weeks). Priority formula: `Priority = (FairShare factor) × (Job Size factor) × (Queue Wait Time factor)`. FairShare factor = Allocated Shares / Raw Usage (higher for under-utilizers, lower for over-utilizers). Result: Team B (used 5% of their 30% share) gets higher priority than Team A (used 60% of their 40% share). This naturally balances GPU access over time without manual intervention.

Slurm Fair-Share Scheduling
  Priority = FairShare × JobSize × WaitTime
  FairShare = Allocated / Recent Usage → Under-users get boost, over-users get penalty
  RESULT: Heavy users deprioritized → light users get their fair share
AI Ops Q149 of 200
What is Slurm QOS (Quality of Service), and how is it used for GPU job prioritization?
A) QOS is a network configuration — it prioritizes InfiniBand traffic for different Slurm jobs
B) QOS is identical to fair-share — the terms are interchangeable with no functional difference
C) QOS only controls job time limits — it cannot affect job priority or resource limits
D) QOS defines job priority tiers with associated limits. Examples: "high" QOS (priority 1000, max 32 GPUs, max 24hr runtime, preempts lower QOS), "normal" QOS (priority 500, max 8 GPUs, max 7 day runtime), "low" QOS (priority 100, max 4 GPUs, max 48hr runtime, preemptible). Users are granted specific QOS levels. A high-priority job can preempt a low-priority job (checkpoint and requeue)
✅ Correct Answer: D

QOS provides tiered GPU access. Common QOS tiers: "urgent" (production-critical, priority 10000, can preempt anyone — use sparingly), "normal" (standard research, priority 1000, most users), "long" (extended training runs, priority 500, up to 30 days), and "preemptible" (lowest priority, can be interrupted — good for hyperparameter sweeps that can restart). QOS limits per tier: MaxGPUs (prevents one job from taking all GPUs), MaxWallTime (prevents infinite jobs), MaxJobs (prevents queue flooding), and GrpGPUs (limits total GPUs per group). Preemption: high-priority job can signal low-priority job to checkpoint and requeue (SIGTERM with time to save).

Slurm QOS Tiers
  QOS            PRIORITY    MAX GPUs    MAX TIME    PREEMPTIBLE?
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  urgent         10000       128         12 hrs      Preempts others
  normal         1000        32          7 days      No
  preemptible    100         8           48 hrs      YES (checkpoint)
AI Ops Q150 of 200
What is the role of a Slurm job script (`#SBATCH` directives) in GPU cluster operations?
A) `#SBATCH` directives specify job requirements: GPU type and count (`--gres=gpu:H100:8`), number of nodes (`--nodes=2`), tasks per node (`--ntasks-per-node=8`), walltime (`--time=48:00:00`), partition (`--partition=dgx`), QOS (`--qos=normal`), output file (`--output=%j.out`), and email notifications. Slurm reads these directives, queues the job, and runs it when resources match. Proper directives ensure efficient GPU scheduling and prevent resource waste
B) #SBATCH directives are comments — Slurm ignores them; all job configuration must be passed as command-line arguments to sbatch
C) #SBATCH directives only control output formatting — GPU allocation is handled separately by the GPU driver
D) #SBATCH is a Python library — it's a pip-installable package for submitting GPU jobs from Jupyter notebooks
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Slurm job script is the contract between the user and the cluster. Critical directives: `#SBATCH --gres=gpu:H100:8` (the most important — how many GPUs of what type), `#SBATCH --nodes=2 --ntasks-per-node=8` (2 nodes, 8 tasks each), `#SBATCH --time=48:00:00` (hard limit — job is KILLED after this, so overestimate slightly), `#SBATCH --partition=dgx` (which node group), `#SBATCH --output=logs/train_%j.out` (%j = job ID). Bad directives cause problems: requesting too many GPUs = long queue time, requesting too little time = job killed before completion, forgetting --gres = job runs on CPU-only node. Standard template ensures consistency and prevents common mistakes.

Essential #SBATCH Directives
  #SBATCH --gres=gpu:H100:8    # GPU type + count (MOST IMPORTANT)
  #SBATCH --nodes=2             # Number of nodes
  #SBATCH --ntasks-per-node=8   # Tasks per node (1 per GPU for training)
  #SBATCH --time=48:00:00       # Walltime (HARD LIMIT — job killed after)
  #SBATCH --partition=dgx       # Which node pool

AI Ops Q151 of 200
How do you troubleshoot a Slurm job that shows status "PD" (Pending) for hours despite GPUs appearing available?
A) GPUs showing as idle in nvidia-smi always means they're available — Slurm must be restarted to clear its scheduling state
B) Check with `squeue -j JOBID --start` (estimates start time) and `scontrol show job JOBID` (shows reason). Common causes: QOS GPU limit reached (max GPUs per user/QOS), partition node state (nodes drained/down), pending job array limit, priority too low (higher priority jobs ahead), GRES mismatch (requested H100:8 but partition only has A100), or maintenance reservation blocking resources
C) "PD" means the job script has a syntax error — fix the #SBATCH directives and resubmit
D) Slurm jobs always pend for exactly 15 minutes — this is a mandatory cooling period before GPU allocation
✅ Correct Answer: B

Pending Slurm jobs are a daily troubleshooting task. `scontrol show job JOBID` shows the Reason field — the exact cause of pending. Common scenarios: Resources (waiting for 8 H100 GPUs to free up), Priority (higher priority jobs are ahead in the queue), QOSMaxGRESPerUser (user already has 8 GPUs running and their QOS limit is 8), Reservation (a maintenance reservation is blocking the nodes), NodeState (requested nodes are DRAINED or DOWN), and PartitionTimeLimit (job requested 7 days but partition limit is 48 hours). `squeue --start` gives an estimated start time based on currently running jobs' time limits. `scontrol show reservation` shows active reservations that may block resources.

Debugging Pending Slurm Jobs
scontrol show job JOBID → Reason: Resources/Priority/QOSLimit/Reservation
squeue --start → Estimated start time
scontrol show partition → Check partition limits and node states
AI Ops Q152 of 200
A multi-node training job fails with "NCCL WARN NET/IB : Got completion with error" — what should you investigate first?
A) The training script has a bug — NCCL errors are always caused by incorrect PyTorch code
B) Ignore it — NCCL warnings are informational and don't affect training correctness
C) Check InfiniBand fabric health: `ibstat` (link status), `ibstatus` (link speed, check for downgrade from 400Gb/s to lower), `perfquery` (port error counters — symbol errors, link down events, excessive buffer overruns). Check cable seating/power. Run `nccl-tests` (all_reduce_perf) to isolate NCCL from training code. Check `dmesg` for GPU XID errors — a failing GPU can cause NCCL timeouts
D) NCCL errors are caused by incorrect CUDA version — downgrade CUDA to the previous major version
✅ Correct Answer: C

NCCL networking errors are the most common cause of multi-node training failures. The systematic approach: Layer 1 (physical) — cables, transceivers, link LEDs. `ibstat` shows link status (Active? Down? Init?). Layer 2 (link quality) — `ibstatus` shows negotiated speed (NDR 400Gb/s or degraded?), `perfquery -x` shows error counters (SymbolErrorCounter, LinkDownedCounter, PortRcvErrors). Layer 3 (NCCL) — `all_reduce_perf -b 8 -e 128M -f 2 -g 8` benchmarks NCCL performance across all GPUs. Check GPU health (dmesg for XID errors — a GPU generating XID 48/79 will cause NCCL failures in that node). NCCL_DEBUG=INFO environment variable enables verbose NCCL logging for detailed diagnostics.

NCCL Error Troubleshooting
ibstat → Link status? | ibstatus → Speed (400Gb/s or degraded?)
perfquery → Error counters | nccl-tests → Isolate NCCL from training code
dmesg → GPU XID errors | NCCL_DEBUG=INFO → Verbose NCCL logs
AI Ops Q153 of 200
How do you monitor and optimize GPU power efficiency in a large cluster?
A) GPU power efficiency is fixed by hardware design — software cannot influence power consumption
B) Always run GPUs at maximum power limit — performance is the only metric; power cost is negligible
C) Underclock all GPUs to 50% power — this maximizes efficiency regardless of workload requirements
D) Monitor power metrics via DCGM (power draw, power limit, p-state). Use `nvidia-smi -pl` to set power limits per GPU — reducing power by 20% may only reduce performance by 5-10% for memory-bound workloads. Track GPU efficiency (TFLOPS/Watt) to identify inefficient jobs. Use GPU Boost and application clocks for consistent performance. Schedule training during off-peak power pricing when possible
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU power optimization is a cost lever. A 10MW cluster at $0.10/kWh costs $8.76M/year in electricity. Reducing power by 15% saves $1.3M/year. Power capping (`nvidia-smi -pl 600` on 700W H100) reduces max power by 14% — for memory-bandwidth-bound workloads, performance loss may be only 3-5% because the GPU isn't compute-limited anyway. DCGM metrics to track: `DCGM_FI_DEV_POWER_USAGE` (current draw), `DCGM_FI_DEV_POWER_MGMT_LIMIT` (power cap), and compute efficiency (actual TFLOPS / (power × theoretical TFLOPS/W)). Power scheduling: delay non-urgent training to nighttime when electricity is cheaper (some locations have 50% night rate).

GPU Power Optimization
nvidia-smi -pl 600 (H100: 700W→600W, 14% power reduction)
Memory-bound workloads: ~5% perf loss for 14% power savings
10MW cluster at $0.10/kWh: 15% power saving = $1.3M/year
AI Ops Q154 of 200
What is GPU Burn, and when should you run it?
A) GPU Burn is a GPU stress-testing tool that runs maximum compute and memory workloads to validate GPU stability under thermal and power stress. Run it: on new hardware (acceptance testing — find DOA GPUs), after driver updates (verify stability), when diagnosing intermittent GPU failures (XID errors, ECC errors), and periodically for proactive health checks. A GPU that passes GPU Burn for 24+ hours is considered stable
B) GPU Burn is a GPU destruction tool — it intentionally overheats GPUs to test their failure temperature limits
C) GPU Burn is a benchmarking tool — it measures peak TFLOPS for comparing GPU models
D) GPU Burn is thermal paste application software — it guides users through applying thermal compound to GPU dies
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU Burn (gpu-burn) is the "does this GPU work under load?" test. It runs matrix multiplication workloads that maximize GPU utilization, power draw, and temperature. Typical usage: `gpu-burn -d 86400` (run for 24 hours on all GPUs). While running, monitor: temperature (should stabilize, not climb indefinitely), power (should reach TDP and stay), ECC errors (should not increase), and XID errors (should be zero). A new GPU that fails GPU Burn (crashes, ECC/XID errors, thermal throttling at normal ambient) should be RMA'd. For DGX systems, NVIDIA DCGM diagnostics (`dcgmi diag -r 3`) provides a comprehensive GPU validation suite. Acceptance testing: every new GPU/node should pass 24-hour GPU Burn before entering production.

GPU Burn — Acceptance Testing
gpu-burn -d 86400 → 24hr stress test, max power + temp
Monitor: Temperature (stable?), ECC (increasing?), XID (any?)
PASS = 24hrs stable, no errors → GPU ready for production
AI Ops Q155 of 200
How do you handle a "GPU fallen off the bus" error (XID 79)?
A) XID 79 is a software error — reinstall the NVIDIA driver and the GPU will reconnect automatically
B) XID 79 means the GPU has lost PCIe connectivity. Troubleshooting order: check physical seating (reseat GPU in PCIe slot or SXM socket), inspect PCIe slot/riser for damage, check power cables (insufficient power causes GPU disconnect), try a different PCIe slot, check for PCIe errors in dmesg. If hardware checks pass, run `nvidia-smi -r` (GPU reset). If recurring, replace GPU (faulty PCIe interface) or motherboard (faulty slot)
C) XID 79 is a network error — it indicates InfiniBand link failure, not GPU hardware issues
D) XID 79 is a normal event — GPUs regularly disconnect and reconnect as part of power management
✅ Correct Answer: B

XID 79 ("GPU has fallen off the bus") is one of the most disruptive GPU errors — the GPU completely disappears from the system. Impact: all processes using that GPU crash, any NCCL communication involving that GPU hangs, Slurm marks the node as down (or should). Recovery: `nvidia-smi -r` attempts GPU reset. If GPU reappears, monitor closely — one XID 79 may be a fluke; repeated means hardware failure. For SXM GPUs (DGX): GPU is soldered/mezzanine — can't simply reseat. Power cycle the entire system. If recurring, replace the GPU baseboard (all 8 GPUs on one HGX baseboard). For PCIe GPUs: try different slot, different riser, different power cable — isolate the failing component.

XID 79 Troubleshooting
SXM (DGX): Power cycle system → if recurring, replace HGX baseboard
PCIe: Reseat GPU in slot → check riser/cable → try different slot → replace GPU
nvidia-smi -r → GPU reset attempt | dmesg → PCIe error details
AI Ops Q156 of 200
What is the NCCL bandwidth test, and how do you interpret its results?
A) NCCL bandwidth test measures internet speed — it tests the connection between the cluster and cloud storage
B) Results are pass/fail — any output means NCCL is working; bandwidth numbers are irrelevant
C) `nccl-tests` (all_reduce_perf, alltoall_perf) measure GPU-GPU communication bandwidth and latency. Expected results: NVLink intra-node ~450-500 GB/s (out of 900 GB/s theoretical — half due to ring algorithm overhead), InfiniBand inter-node ~45-48 GB/s (out of 50 GB/s line rate — protocol overhead). Degraded results (<70% of expected) indicate: faulty NVLink (check nvidia-smi nvlink), damaged InfiniBand cables, or PCIe bottleneck (Gen3 instead of Gen5)
D) NCCL tests are in-memory GPU benchmarks — they don't test network performance
✅ Correct Answer: C

NCCL tests are the GPU networking equivalent of a speed test. `all_reduce_perf -b 8 -e 2G -f 2 -g 8` runs AllReduce with varying message sizes across 8 GPUs. Key metrics: bus bandwidth (algorithmic bandwidth — the effective data rate accounting for the ring algorithm), and out-of-place vs in-place (in-place is faster, no memory copy). Expected NVLink (intra-node, 8×H100): ~235 GB/s bus bandwidth for large messages (out of 450 GB/s algorithm limit). Expected InfiniBand NDR (inter-node, 8 GPUs/node): ~23 GB/s per GPU pair. Degradation checklist: NVLink errors (`nvidia-smi nvlink -e`), PCIe link speed (`nvidia-smi -q -d PCIE`), InfiniBand link speed (`ibstatus`), and NCCL topology detection (`NCCL_TOPO_DUMP_FILE`).

NCCL Bandwidth Test Results
NVLink intra-node (8×H100): ~235 GB/s bus BW (expected)
InfiniBand NDR inter-node: ~23 GB/s per GPU pair (expected)
Degraded → check: NVLink errors, PCIe speed, IB link speed, topology
AI Ops Q157 of 200
What is GPU thermal throttling, and how do you detect it before it impacts training?
A) Thermal throttling is a GPU protection myth — GPUs can operate indefinitely at any temperature without performance impact
B) Thermal throttling only occurs at 100°C+ — normal GPU operating temperatures (60-85°C) never trigger throttling
C) Thermal throttling is obvious — the GPU shuts down completely, making detection trivial
D) Thermal throttling silently reduces GPU clock speeds when temperature exceeds the throttling threshold (85°C for H100). Detection: `nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK` shows "Clocks Throttle Reasons" — check for "SW Thermal Slowdown" or "HW Thermal Slowdown." Monitor GPU clock speeds alongside temperature — if clock drops while temp is stable, throttling may be active. DCGM `DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS` provides the alert metric
✅ Correct Answer: D

Thermal throttling is the silent performance killer. At 85°C, the H100 begins reducing SM clock speed — from boost (~1.98 GHz) toward base (~1.1 GHz). The throttling is gradual: 85°C = minor reduction, 90°C = significant reduction, 95°C = heavy throttling. Monitoring: `nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK` shows "Clocks Throttle Reasons" with flags for thermal, power, and other throttling causes. DCGM metric `DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS` (bitmask — check for thermal bits). Alerting: set Prometheus alert for GPU temperature >82°C for 5+ minutes (early warning before throttling) AND clock throttling active. Response: increase cooling (higher fan speed, lower coolant temperature), reduce ambient data center temperature, or reduce GPU power limit.

Thermal Throttling Detection
nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK → Clocks Throttle Reasons: SW/HW Thermal Slowdown
DCGM throttle reasons (bitmask) → Alert if thermal bits set
PROMETHEUS: GPU temp >82°C for 5min → WARN | Throttle active → CRITICAL
AI Ops Q158 of 200
What is GPU memory over-subscription, and how does it manifest in training failures?
A) GPU HBM is unlimited — over-subscription is impossible because the GPU driver automatically extends HBM using system RAM
B) GPU memory over-subscription occurs when a workload requests more HBM than available (e.g., 85GB on 80GB H100). Manifestations: CUDA Out of Memory (OOM) error — immediate. For multi-GPU: one GPU OOMs while others are fine. Prevention: `nvidia-smi` shows memory usage per GPU, PyTorch `torch.cuda.memory_summary()`, and batch size tuning (reduce batch size, use gradient accumulation, enable gradient checkpointing)
C) GPU over-subscription is a feature — GPUs automatically swap memory to NVMe SSDs when HBM is full
D) OOM errors are always caused by memory leaks — restarting the training script fixes the issue permanently
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU OOM is the most common training failure. Memory breakdown: model weights (70B × 2 bytes FP16 = 140GB), gradients (140GB), optimizer states (Adam: 2× FP32 copies = 560GB), and activations (batch_size × seq_len × hidden_dim — can be 50-200GB). Total easily exceeds 80GB. Solutions: gradient checkpointing (trade compute for memory — recompute activations instead of storing), mixed precision (FP16 weights, FP32 master copy), optimizer offloading (ZeRO-Offload — move optimizer states to CPU RAM), and reduce batch size (fewer activations). For inference: INT8/FP8 quantization reduces model size 2-4×. MIG: each instance gets DEDICATED HBM — a 1g.20gb instance cannot access more than 20GB even if other instances are idle.

GPU Memory — Where It Goes
TRAINING MEMORY (70B model, FP16): Weights 140GB + Gradients 140GB + Optimizer 560GB + Activations 100GB
TOTAL: ~940GB → Needs 12+ H100s with model parallelism
SOLUTIONS: Gradient checkpoint, mixed precision, ZeRO, reduce batch size
AI Ops Q159 of 200
How do you handle a node that repeatedly produces incorrect training results (silent data corruption)?
A) Isolate immediately: drain the node (Slurm: DRAIN, K8s: cordon), check DCGM for ECC/XID errors on all GPUs, run GPU Burn + memory tests (dcgmi diag -r 3), check CPU (memtest86, mcelog for machine check exceptions), check system memory (EDAC errors). If GPU errors found → replace GPU. If CPU/RAM errors found → replace affected component. If all hardware tests pass → suspect software (driver version mismatch, incorrect CUDA version). Never return a node producing silent errors to production without root cause identification
B) Silent errors are statistical noise — continue using the node; the errors will average out over many training runs
C) Reboot the node and return it to service — a reboot fixes all silent data corruption issues
D) Silent data corruption is always caused by cosmic rays — no hardware action needed; it's an unavoidable environmental phenomenon
✅ Correct Answer: A

Silent data corruption is the most dangerous GPU failure mode — it produces wrong results without crashing. A GPU with failing HBM (increasing ECC single-bit errors, occasional double-bit) may compute slightly incorrect gradients, silently degrading model quality over thousands of iterations. Detection: compare training loss curves across identical runs on different nodes (divergent curves on one node = corruption), periodic validation accuracy checks (unexpected drops), and DCGM health checks (ECC trending). Isolation: DRAIN immediately — every training job on that node may be producing corrupted results. Diagnostics: DCGM level 3/4 diagnostics, GPU Burn, memory test patterns. If no hardware fault found, check software: CUDA version mismatch between nodes, mixed GPU architectures (A100 + H100 in same NCCL communicator).

Silent Data Corruption Response
DRAIN node → DCGM ECC/XID check → GPU Burn + memory tests → CPU/RAM tests
HARDWARE FOUND: Replace faulty component | NO HARDWARE: Check software/drivers
NEVER return node to production without root cause identified

What is the GPU lifecycle management process from procurement to decommissioning?
A) Buy GPUs, install them, use them forever — GPUs have no lifecycle management requirements
B) GPU lifecycle is managed entirely by NVIDIA — they automatically ship replacements when GPUs reach end-of-life
C) Procurement (specify GPU, negotiate lead time 3-6mo) → Acceptance (GPU Burn 24hr, DCGM diag) → Production (monitoring, maintenance) → Degradation monitoring (ECC trending, XID tracking) → Warranty RMA (if within warranty + failing) → Decommissioning (secure erase, e-waste, or resale). Typical GPU lifespan: 3-5 years in production, then repurposed for lower-priority workloads or decommissioned
D) GPUs are disposable — they're replaced annually as new models are released
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU lifecycle management ensures maximum ROI from expensive GPU investments. Phase 1 (Procurement): GPUs have 3-6 month lead times — order BEFORE you need them. Phase 2 (Acceptance): every GPU must pass 24hr GPU Burn + DCGM level 3 diagnostics before production. Phase 3 (Production): continuous monitoring (DCGM/Prometheus), proactive maintenance (driver updates, thermal paste/cleaning), performance tracking (GPU efficiency over time — degradation signals aging). Phase 4 (Degradation): as GPUs age, ECC errors may increase, performance may degrade — move to lower-priority workloads. Phase 5 (End-of-Life): after 3-5 years, newer GPUs offer 3-5× better performance per watt — retire old GPUs. Decommissioning: secure erase (NVMe format, GPU memory clearing), environmental disposal (e-waste regulations), or resale (used H100 market).

GPU Lifecycle
PROCURE(3-6mo) → ACCEPT(24hr test) → PRODUCTION(monitor) → DEGRADE(track) → DECOMMISSION(3-5yr)
Key: Order ahead of need, validate on arrival, monitor continuously, retire strategically
AI Ops Q161 of 200
What is MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) and when should you use it vs. MPS vs. time-slicing?
A) MIG partitions GPU hardware (SM/VRAM) into isolated instances with guaranteed QoS. Use MIG: for inference serving (multiple models on one GPU with strict latency SLAs), multi-tenant environments where one tenant must never impact another, academic clusters sharing GPUs across research groups. Use MPS (Multi-Process Service): for training workloads where processes can share GPU compute dynamically but need CUDA context isolation. Use time-slicing: for batch inference where throughput > latency and you accept context-switch overhead. Key difference: MIG = hardware isolation (best security/QoS), MPS = software sharing (better utilization), time-slicing = oversubscription (maximum density)
B) MIG, MPS, and time-slicing are the same thing — they're interchangeable names for GPU sharing
C) Always use MIG for everything — it's the best GPU sharing technology NVIDIA provides
D) GPU sharing is not possible — each GPU can only run one workload at a time
✅ Correct Answer: A

MIG (A100/A30/H100): partitions a single GPU into up to 7 isolated instances, each with dedicated SM slices + VRAM + cache. Config: `nvidia-smi mig -cgi` to list profiles, `-cci` to create, `-dci` to destroy. MPS: allows multiple processes to share a GPU dynamically, higher utilization than MIG but less isolation — one misbehaving process can impact others. Time-slicing (supported in GPU Operator/run.sh): GPU time is sliced across containers round-robin, maximum density at cost of jitter and contention. Decision matrix: need strict SLAs + hardware security → MIG. Need maximum training throughput with shared GPU → MPS. Inference at scale with bursty load → time-slicing or MIG depending on latency budget.

GPU Sharing Technologies
MIG: [===Instance1===][=I2=][=I3=][=I4=]  Hardware-isolated, dedicated SM/VRAM
MPS: [=====Shared dynamically=====]  Software, processes share cooperatively
TimeSlicing: [P1][P2][P3][P1][P2]  Round-robin, maximum density
AI Ops Q162 of 200
How do you manage GPU firmware and VBIOS updates at scale across a cluster?
A) Firmware updates are handled automatically by NVIDIA drivers — no operator action needed
B) Establish a firmware inventory (nvidia-smi -q -d VBIOS), check NVIDIA advisory bulletins, test on canary nodes first, use BMC/iLO/IPMI for remote flashing or nvflash utility, schedule maintenance windows, track versions in CMDB. Never update firmware without a rollback plan. GPU firmware mismatches across NCCL communicator can cause hangs
C) Flash all GPUs simultaneously during production — firmware updates are low-risk operations
D) GPU firmware never needs updating — the factory firmware is sufficient for the GPU's lifetime
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU firmware management is critical for security patches (CVEs in GPU firmware), performance improvements (NCCL optimizations in newer firmware), and bug fixes. Inventory: `nvidia-smi -q -d VBIOS` shows current version, cross-reference with NVIDIA firmware bulletins. Canary testing: flash 2-3 nodes first, run full validation suite (NCCL allreduce, GPU Burn, training benchmark), promote to rest. Rollback: always have previous firmware binary stored — flashing back requires the same nvflash utility. Critical rule: all GPUs in a training job must have identical firmware — mixed versions in NCCL communicator cause silent hangs. Maintenance coordination: firmware updates require node drain + reboot — integrate with Slurm/K8s maintenance schedules.

Firmware Update Workflow
INVENTORY → ADVISORY CHECK → CANARY(2-3 nodes test) → VALIDATION(gsuite) → ROLLOUT(by rack)
Critical: All GPUs in NCCL communicator MUST have identical firmware
AI Ops Q163 of 200
How do you configure GPU power capping and why is it important for datacenter operations?
A) Power capping is unnecessary — datacenters have unlimited power budget for GPU clusters
B) Set all GPUs to maximum power always — performance is the only consideration for AI workloads
C) `nvidia-smi -pl` sets power limit in watts. Benefits: prevents rack/circuit breaker trips (aggregate power draw of 8× H100 = ~5.6kW per node, 20 nodes per rack = 112kW), enables over-subscription of datacenter power infrastructure (more GPUs in same power envelope), reduces cooling demand proportionally, extends GPU lifespan (lower power = lower temperatures). Trade-off: ~2-5% performance reduction for 10-15% power reduction — often worth it for density
D) Power capping only matters for consumer GPUs — datacenter GPUs have their own power management
✅ Correct Answer: C

Power capping (`nvidia-smi -pl 500` sets max to 500W) is essential for datacenter GPU clusters. H100 SXM5 has 700W TDP; 8 GPUs per node = 5.6kW GPU-only, plus CPU/RAM/network = ~8-10kW per node. A single rack of 20 such nodes draws 160-200kW — exceeding most datacenter per-rack limits (typically 40-60kW). Power capping at 500W drops per-GPU to 4kW for 8 GPUs, ~6.5kW/node, ~130kW/rack — often within limits. The sweet spot: power reduction is super-linear for performance — 10% less power often costs only 2-3% performance due to GPU boost behavior. Persistence: set power limits in GPU Operator CR or startup scripts for consistency across reboots.

Power Capping Math
H100 SXM5: 700W × 8 GPUs = 5.6kW/node → 20 nodes = 112kW (GPU only) → PLUS infra = 160kW+
Capped 500W: 500W × 8 = 4kW/node → 20 nodes = 80kW → 2-3% perf loss, 20%+ power saved
Trade-off: More GPUs in same power budget vs. slight per-GPU performance reduction
AI Ops Q164 of 200
What is GPU Direct Storage (GDS) and how does it accelerate AI data pipelines?
A) GDS enables direct DMA transfer between NVMe storage and GPU memory, bypassing CPU and system memory entirely. Path: NVMe → PCIe → GPU VRAM (no CPU bounce buffer). Benefits for AI: training data loading is 2-5× faster, CPU utilization drops (freed for compute), I/O latency reduced. Requirements: NVIDIA GPU (A100+), supported NVMe drives, nvidia-fs driver, CUDA 11.4+. Use cases: large-scale training (100TB+ datasets), high-resolution image loading, HPC checkpoint/restart
B) GPU Direct Storage is a marketing term for using SSDs — it has no actual performance benefit
C) GDS replaces the need for system memory — GPUs access storage directly without any software changes
D) GPU Direct Storage only works with NVIDIA's proprietary DGX storage — it's not available on commodity hardware
✅ Correct Answer: A

Traditional I/O path: NVMe → CPU (DMA) → system memory → CPU copy → GPU memory (2 copies, CPU involved). GDS path: NVMe → PCIe bus → GPU BAR1 mapping → GPU VRAM (single DMA, zero CPU). This eliminates the CPU bounce buffer — critical when training data is 10-100TB and every millisecond of I/O matters. Enablement: `nvidia-fs.ko` kernel module, CUDA `cuFile` API, and compatible filesystem (ext4, xfs, or parallel filesystems like Lustre/GPFS). Typical observed improvements: 2-3× for large sequential reads, CPU load reduction of 30-50% during data loading. For anihpj-style image processing pipelines with large asset datasets, GDS can halve data loading time.

Traditional vs GDS I/O Path
Traditional: NVMe ──PCIe──▶ CPU RAM ──CPU copy──▶ GPU VRAM  (2 hops, CPU loaded)
GDS:       NVMe ────────PCIe DMA───────────────▶ GPU VRAM  (1 hop, CPU free)
Requires: nvidia-fs.ko, cuFile API, CUDA 11.4+, compatible NVMe

How do you implement fair-share scheduling for GPU resources across multiple teams?
A) First-come-first-served is the only fair scheduling policy for GPU resources
B) Slurm: configure multi-factor priority plugin with fairshare factor, usage decay half-life, and QoS/partition limits. Assign accounts per team with fairshare shares proportional to funding or priority. Monitor `sshare` for fairness drift. K8s: use Kueue for quota-based fair sharing with ResourceFlavors, Cohort for cross-namespace sharing, and fair-sharing weight. Both: implement preemption for high-priority jobs, guarantee minimum allocation per team, prevent hoarding
C) Give each team dedicated GPUs — sharing GPU resources across teams is too complex
D) Use random allocation — over time, randomness provides statistically fair GPU distribution
✅ Correct Answer: B

Fair-share scheduling prevents one team from monopolizing expensive GPU resources. Slurm approach: `PriorityType=priority/multifactor`, set `PriorityWeightFairshare`, define `PriorityDecayHalfLife` (e.g., 7-00:00:00 = 7 days). Each account has `fairshare=` value proportional to resource share. `sshare -a` shows effective usage vs. target — when actual > target, priority drops. K8s approach: Kueue `ResourceFlavor` defines GPU types, `ClusterQueue` sets quotas, `LocalQueue` per team, `Cohort` for cross-team sharing with `fairSharing.weight`. Critical: preemption (`borrowWithinCohort`) lets high-priority jobs reclaim resources from lower-priority ones within fair-share bounds.

Fair-Share Scheduling
Team A: 40% share, current 60% usage → priority LOW
Team B: 30% share, current 10% usage → priority HIGH
Team C: 30% share, current 30% usage → priority MEDIUM
Next job scheduled: Team B (highest deficit from fair share)

AI Ops Q167 of 200
How do you troubleshoot a training job that hangs during NCCL allreduce initialization?
A) Check NCCL_DEBUG=INFO logs for NCCL transport selection (NET/SHM — InfiniBand vs. shared memory). Verify all ranks can reach each other: nccl-tests all_reduce_perf. Check NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME for correct network interface. Verify NCCL_IB_DISABLE not set to 1 if using InfiniBand. Check GDR (GPU Direct RDMA) compatibility: NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL. Verify firewall is open for NCCL communication on the NCCL port range. Check for mixed NCCL versions (all nodes must be identical). For multi-node: verify hostname resolution across all nodes. Common cause: NCCL falling back to socket when InfiniBand expected, causing timeout
B) NCCL hangs are always caused by faulty GPUs — replace the GPU and the problem resolves
C) Restart the training job repeatedly until it works — NCCL hangs are transient and self-resolving
D) NCCL debugging requires NVIDIA support — there's nothing an operator can diagnose independently
✅ Correct Answer: A

NCCL initialization hangs are the #1 distributed training issue. The initialization phase establishes allreduce communicators among all ranks — if any pair can't communicate, all ranks hang indefinitely. Debug: `export NCCL_DEBUG=INFO` shows transport negotiation — look for "NCCL INFO NET" (InfiniBand) vs "NCCL INFO network" (socket). Verify connectivity: `nccl-tests/build/all_reduce_perf -b 8 -e 128M -f 2 -g 8` on all ranks. Network interface: `NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME=ib0` ensures correct NIC. InfiniBand: check `ibstat` for port state, `NCCL_IB_DISABLE=0` (must NOT be 1). GDR: `NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL=5` for full GPU Direct RDMA. Common pitfall: one node has different NCCL version or driver — NCCL version mismatch causes silent incompatibility.

NCCL Hang Debug Flow
NCCL_DEBUG=INFO → Check transport (NET=IB, SHM=shared) → Verify connectivity
IB? → ibstat Active → check NCCL_IB_DISABLE=0 → check GDR level
Socket? → NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME= correct? → firewall open port range?
Multi-node? → hostname resolution across all nodes → identical NCCL version?
AI Ops Q168 of 200
What NCCL environment variables are critical for optimal distributed training performance?
A) NCCL has no tunable parameters — it auto-configures everything optimally
B) Key variables: NCCL_DEBUG (INFO for troubleshooting), NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME (network interface), NCCL_IB_DISABLE (0=enable IB), NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL (0-5, GPU Direct RDMA), NCCL_IB_HCA (specify HCAs), NCCL_IB_GID_INDEX (RoCE GID), NCCL_IB_QPS_PER_CONNECTION (4 or 8), NCCL_IB_TC (traffic class for QoS), NCCL_IB_TIMEOUT (increase for large jobs), NCCL_MIN_NCHANNELS and NCCL_MAX_NCHANNELS (number of ring/channels), NCCL_ALGO (Ring/Tree/CollNetDirect), NCCL_PROTO (Simple/LL/LL128)
C) Only NCCL_DEBUG matters — all other variables are deprecated
D) NCCL environment variables are set automatically by the driver — manual configuration reduces performance
✅ Correct Answer: B

NCCL environment tuning can yield 10-30% allreduce performance improvement. Key variables: `NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME=ib0` — specify correct network interface, wrong interface = socket fallback. `NCCL_IB_DISABLE=0` — must explicitly enable IB/RoCE. `NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL=5` (full GPU Direct RDMA) — H100 with CX7 supports level 5, older NICs may need level 2. `NCCL_IB_HCA=mlx5_0,mlx5_1,...` — specify which HCAs to use when multiple NICs present. `NCCL_IB_QPS_PER_CONNECTION=4` — more queue pairs improve throughput on modern HCAs. `NCCL_IB_TIMEOUT=22` — increase from default 18 for large jobs (>100 GPUs). `NCCL_ALGO=Ring` vs `Tree` vs `CollNetDirect` — Ring is default, Tree better for large messages, CollNetDirect uses in-network reduction (SHARP).

NCCL Performance Tuning Variables
GDR Level: 0(off) → 2(read via BAR1) → 5(write via BAR1, H100+)
QPS: 4 default → 8 for CX7
Timeout: 18(default) → 22-30 for 1000+ GPU jobs
Algo: Ring(most) | Tree(bw-optimal, large msg) | CollNetDirect(SHARP)

How should you design GPU health dashboards for operations teams?

Tier 3 (GPU detail) — single GPU SM/VRAM utilization, clock speeds, ECC trending (last 7d/30d), XID error history. Alerts: GPU temp > 85°C, ECC double-bit error, XID >= 48 (critical), utilization < 10% for > 1hr (stuck job)

D) Use NVIDIA's pre-built dashboard without modification — customization reduces dashboard reliability

Effective GPU dashboards follow the "drill-down" principle — start broad, enable deep investigation. Tier 1 (cluster health at a glance): GPU count (total, available, allocated, error), cluster-wide utilization (target 70-85%), active jobs count. Tier 2 (per-node): GPU temperature (color-coded: green <80°C, yellow 80-85°C, red >85°C), power draw (vs. TDP), utilization (GPU and memory), ECC error rate, NVLink bandwidth, throttling flags (thermal, power). Tier 3 (GPU detail): SM utilization over time, memory bandwidth, clock frequency stability, ECC error trend (increasing errors = impending failure), XID history. Alert thresholds: >85°C (check cooling), double-bit ECC (drain node), XID >= 48 (hardware failure). Source: DCGM → Prometheus → Grafana.

GPU Dashboard Tiers
TIER 1: Cluster ─ GPUs: 512/640 avail | Util: 78% | Errors: 2 | Jobs: 23

AI Ops Q172 of 200
How do you handle NCCL timeout errors during long-running distributed training jobs?
A) NCCL timeouts are hardware failures — drain all nodes and run diagnostics on each one
B) Ignore NCCL timeouts — they're cosmetic warnings that don't affect training correctness
C) Increase NCCL_IB_TIMEOUT from default 18 to 22-30 (larger clusters need higher values). Check for network congestion (IB/RoCE counters — excessive pause frames, CRC errors). Verify allreduce algorithm: Ring is sensitive to stragglers, Tree more robust. Check if timeout correlates with large tensor exchanges — possibly need NCCL_PROTO=LL128 for large messages. For persistent timeouts: run nccl-tests to isolate NCCL from application, check for GPU thermal throttling causing compute jitter
D) NCCL timeouts indicate the job should be restarted on fewer nodes — reduce parallelism
✅ Correct Answer: C

NCCL timeouts during training are often caused by network congestion or insufficient timeout values. Default NCCL_IB_TIMEOUT is 18 (≈68ms) — for jobs with 100+ GPUs, network congestion during allreduce can push latency beyond this, causing false timeout errors. Increase to 22-30 for large clusters. Network health: check IB/RoCE counters — `perfquery -x` shows SymbolErrors, LinkDowned, PortRcvErrors. High CRC errors indicate cable/transceiver issues. Straggler detection: `NCCL_DEBUG=INFO` shows per-rank completion times — if one rank is consistently slow, check that GPU for throttling (thermal, power, or clock issues). Thermal throttling causes variable compute latency that can manifest as NCCL timeout.

NCCL Timeout Troubleshooting
Timeout 18(default) → Increase to 22-30 (large clusters)
Check IB counters: perfquery → CRC errors, SymbolErrors? → Replace cable
Check GPU throttling: nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK → Thermal/Power throttle?
Isolate: nccl-tests all_reduce_perf → NCCL-only, no app overhead

What is the NVIDIA Data Center GPU Manager (DCGM) and what key metrics does it expose?
A) DCGM is just nvidia-smi with a different name — it provides the same information
B) DCGM only tracks GPU temperature and power — it has no diagnostic capabilities
C) DCGM provides comprehensive GPU telemetry and diagnostics. Key metrics: GPU utilization (SM, memory, encoder, decoder), temperature, power draw, clock speeds (SM, memory), PCIe throughput, NVLink throughput, ECC error counts (single/double bit, aggregated), retired pages (memory errors), XID errors, throttling reasons, process-level GPU usage, and FP64/FP32/Tensor Core utilization. Diagnostic levels: 1 (quick), 2 (medium), 3 (extensive), 4 (extended). DCGM exporter feeds Prometheus/Grafana
D) DCGM is a hardware component on the GPU board — it's not software the operator interacts with
✅ Correct Answer: C

DCGM is the foundation of GPU observability. It runs as a daemon (`nv-hostengine`) collecting 100+ GPU metrics at configurable intervals. Key metric categories: Utilization (DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL, DCGM_FI_DEV_MEM_COPY_UTIL), Thermal/Power (DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_TEMP, DCGM_FI_DEV_POWER_USAGE), Memory (DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_USED, DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_FREE), ECC (DCGM_FI_DEV_ECC_SBE_VOL_TOTAL, DCGM_FI_DEV_ECC_DBE_VOL_TOTAL), PCIe/NVLink (DCGM_FI_DEV_PCIE_TX_THROUGHPUT, DCGM_FI_DEV_NVLINK_BANDWIDTH_TOTAL), Errors (DCGM_FI_DEV_XID_ERRORS), Throttling (DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS). Diagnostics: `dcgmi diag -r 1` (level 1, quick), `-r 3` (extensive). DCGM Exporter bridges to Prometheus.

DCGM Architecture
DCGM Daemon(nv-hostengine) → 100+ GPU metrics → DCGM Exporter → Prometheus → Grafana
Diag levels: L1(5min) L2(30min) L3(2hr) L4(extended)
Key: ECC errors, XID errors, throttling, PCIe/NVLink throughput
AI Ops Q176 of 200
How do you monitor GPU throttling reasons and what actions should each trigger?
A) GPU throttling is normal — no action needed for any throttle reason
B) Monitor via `nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK` or DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS (bitmask). Key reasons: Power throttle (bit 0) → increase power limit or check PSU capacity. Thermal throttle (bit 1) → check cooling (fans, ambient temp, airflow blockage). Current throttle (bit 2) → check PSU stability (voltage ripple). Application clock setting (bit 3) → expected if using clock locking. SW power cap (bit 4) → check `nvidia-smi -pl` setting. Slowdown thermal (bit 5) → GPU >95°C critical, immediate cooling check. Sync boost (bit 6) → expected in multi-GPU sync scenarios
C) GPU throttling only matters for gaming GPUs — datacenter GPUs never throttle
D) All throttling is caused by high temperature — just increase fan speed for any throttle condition
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU clock throttling directly impacts training throughput — a throttled H100 can lose 20-30% performance. Throttle reasons are exposed as a bitmask: Bit 0 (0x1 — Power): GPU drawing at power limit, clock reduced. Action: increase power limit (`nvidia-smi -pl`) or redistribute load. Bit 1 (0x2 — Thermal): GPU temp exceeding thermal threshold. Action: check fan speed, ambient temp (should be 18-27°C), airflow clearance, dust buildup. Bit 5 (0x20 — SW Thermal Slowdown): critical temperature >95°C, drastic clock reduction. IMMEDIATE action: drain node, fix cooling. Monitoring: DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS trending — persistent throttling = design issue (under-cooled servers, over-subscribed rack).

Throttle Reason Bitmask
Bit 0(0x1): Power limit    → Increase power cap or redistribute
Bit 1(0x2): Thermal        → Check cooling, air temp, airflow
Bit 2(0x4): Current limit  → Check PSU stability
Bit 5(0x20): SW Thermal(CRITICAL) → IMMEDIATE drain, check cooling >95°C
Monitor: nvidia-smi -q -d CLOCK | DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS

What is the role of InfiniBand subnet manager (SM) in a GPU cluster and what happens if it fails?
A) InfiniBand doesn't need a subnet manager — it's peer-to-peer like Ethernet
B) Subnet manager failure is cosmetic — IB continues to work in degraded mode
C) The subnet manager only manages switches — GPU nodes are unaffected by SM failure
D) The InfiniBand subnet manager (SM) is the brain of the IB fabric. It discovers all nodes/switches, assigns LIDs (Local Identifiers), computes routing tables (LFT — Linear Forwarding Table), and monitors fabric health via periodic sweeps. Without an active SM, the IB fabric collapses — no new LIDs are assigned, existing LIDs expire, NCCL communication fails across all nodes, and all distributed training jobs crash. Always deploy redundant SMs (opensm in master/slave or active/standby)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The InfiniBand subnet manager is a single point of failure for the entire GPU fabric. Every IB fabric requires exactly ONE active SM. It performs: topology discovery (sweeps all ports), LID assignment (each port gets a 16-bit address), path computation (calculates optimal routes between all pairs), and fabric monitoring (periodic sweeps for topology changes). SM failure: routes are NOT static — LIDs have a lease time; when the SM disappears, LIDs expire, fabric state becomes stale, new connections fail, and NCCL cannot form communicators. Protection: deploy redundant SMs (`opensm --daemon` on 2-3 nodes, one becomes master, others standby). Monitoring: `ibstat` shows SM LID, `sminfo` shows SM status. If SM LID = 0, fabric is dead.

InfiniBand Subnet Manager
SM Master: topology discovery → LID assignment → route computation → fabric monitoring
SM Standby(2-3): ready to take over if master fails
SM failure = ALL NCCL communication stops = ALL distributed training jobs crash
Check: sminfo → SM LID=0 means fabric is dead
AI Ops Q179 of 200
What are GPU retired pages and what do they indicate about GPU health?
A) Retired pages are a normal part of GPU operation — every GPU accumulates them over time
B) Retired pages are HBM memory pages that have been marked as faulty and removed from service by the GPU driver. Single-bit ECC errors are corrected transparently, but when a page accumulates too many errors, it's "retired" (remapped to spare pages). Monitor: `nvidia-smi -q -d PAGE_RETIREMENT` shows single/double bit retired, pending retirement. A few retired pages are normal for large HBM (80GB). Red flag: sudden increase in retired pages (dying HBM), double-bit retired pages (uncorrectable errors = silent corruption risk), or pending blacklist pages. GPU with >10% retired pages should be RMA'd
C) Retired pages are performance optimization — they're disabled to improve GPU speed
D) GPU memory doesn't have page retirement — this is a CPU-only concept
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU HBM uses ECC (Error Correction Code) — every memory access is checked. Single-bit errors (SBE): corrected transparently — the GPU continues with the corrected data. However, each page tracks SBE count; when it exceeds a threshold, the page is "retired" and remapped to spare HBM pages. Double-bit errors (DBE): uncorrectable — indicates data corruption. A DBE on a page triggers immediate retirement. Monitoring: `nvidia-smi -q -d PAGE_RETIREMENT` shows "Retired Pages" (single and double bit), "Pending Page Blacklist" (pages being monitored for potential retirement). Healthy GPU: 0-10 retired pages total across 80GB. Warning: sudden increase in retired pages = HBM degradation. Critical: double-bit retired pages = GPU has experienced uncorrectable errors = replace.

GPU Page Retirement
HBM page → SBE(too many) → RETIRED(single-bit) → remapped to spare
HBM page → DBE(uncorrectable) → RETIRED(double-bit) → DATA CORRUPTION
Healthy: 0-10 retired | Warning: rapid increase | Critical: DBE retired → RMA
Check: nvidia-smi -q -d PAGE_RETIREMENT
AI Ops Q180 of 200
What is NVLink and NVSwitch, and how do they impact multi-GPU training performance?
A) NVLink is a high-bandwidth GPU-to-GPU interconnect (H100: 900 GB/s bidirectional per link, 18 links = 7.2 TB/s per GPU). NVSwitch connects all GPUs in a node (up to 8 GPUs) in a non-blocking crossbar, enabling any-to-any communication at full NVLink speed. Impact on training: NVLink enables efficient model parallelism (tensor/pipeline parallelism) within a node — GPUs can share model layers at near-local-memory speeds. Without NVLink: GPU-to-GPU communication goes through PCIe (64 GB/s) — 100× slower. NVSwitch ensures all-to-all communication without bandwidth contention
B) NVLink and NVSwitch are just marketing names for PCIe — they offer no real performance benefit
C) NVLink only matters for gaming — datacenter GPUs use standard PCIe for inter-GPU communication
D) NVSwitch replaces the need for NCCL — GPUs connected via NVSwitch don't need communication libraries
✅ Correct Answer: A

NVLink is the foundation of multi-GPU scaling. H100 has 18 NVLink 4.0 connections at 50 GB/s each direction (100 GB/s bidirectional per link), total 900 GB/s per GPU × 18 links = 7.2 TB/s aggregate per GPU. NVSwitch: a non-blocking crossbar connecting all 8 GPUs in a DGX/HGX — any GPU can talk to any GPU at full NVLink speed simultaneously. Impact: model parallelism (Megatron-LM tensor parallelism) requires frequent allreduce across GPUs — NVLink makes this nearly transparent. Without NVLink (PCIe-only): 64 GB/s PCIe Gen5 × 16 = severely limits inter-GPU bandwidth. NVSwitch health: `nvidia-smi nvlink -s` shows link status, `nvidia-smi nvlink -e` shows error counts.

NVLink vs PCIe Bandwidth
NVLink 4.0 (H100): 900 GB/s per GPU, 18 links → NVSwitch crossbar → any-to-any
PCIe Gen5 x16:    64 GB/s per GPU → limited inter-GPU bandwidth
NVSwitch: 8 GPUs full mesh, non-blocking, 7.2 TB/s aggregate per GPU
Use: Model parallelism, tensor parallelism, pipeline parallelism within node

What are GPU XID errors and how should each severity level be handled?
A) XID errors are informational only — no action is needed for any XID
B) All XID errors require immediate GPU replacement regardless of severity
C) XID errors are NVIDIA GPU hardware/software error codes logged by the driver. Severity levels: Informational (XID < 32) — log, no action. Warning (XID 32-47) — monitor trends, may indicate developing hardware issues. Critical (XID 48-63) — hardware failure, GPU requires drain and RMA (e.g., XID 48 = Double Bit ECC Error, XID 61 = Internal micro-controller error, XID 62 = GPU memory error). Application (XID 64-79) — usually software/driver bugs, try driver update. XID 79 (User App Error) — user's application issue. Monitor via `nvidia-smi -q -d XID` or DCGM XID counters
D) XID errors are caused by user applications — they don't indicate GPU hardware problems
✅ Correct Answer: C

XID (eXternal ID) errors are NVIDIA's diagnostic heartbeat. Critical XIDs that demand immediate action: XID 48 (Double Bit ECC Error — uncorrectable memory error, GPU must be drained and RMA'd), XID 61 (Internal micro-controller error — GPU control processor failure), XID 62 (GPU memory error — HBM failure detected), XID 74 (GPU NVLink error — NVLink hardware fault). Warning XIDs: XID 32 (Unknown error — track frequency), XID 45 (PCIe link error — check PCIe retimers/risers). Application XIDs: XID 13 (Graphics Engine Exception — usually driver bug), XID 31 (GPU memory page fault — application accessing invalid memory), XID 79 (User App Error). Monitoring: DCGM_FI_DEV_XID_ERRORS counter, alert on critical XIDs (>=48) — drain node, run DCGM diag, RMA if confirmed.

XID Severity Classification
XID < 32:  Informational — log, no action
XID 32-47:  Warning — monitor trends, potential developing issue
XID 48-63:  CRITICAL — hardware failure → DRAIN → RMA (48=ECC, 61=MCU, 62=HBM)
XID 64-79:  Application/Driver — check driver version, user code, update if needed
Alert on any XID >= 48 → immediate node drain

How do you optimize GPU cluster cooling for maximum GPU density?
A) GPU cooling is the datacenter team's problem — AI operators don't need to understand it
B) Key strategies: Hot aisle/cold aisle containment (separate hot exhaust from cold intake), rack power density planning (H100 nodes: 8-10kW each, plan rack cooling capacity accordingly), liquid cooling (direct-to-chip for >30kW/rack — H100 SXM with liquid cooling reduces datacenter cooling overhead by 30-40%), ambient temp management (ASHAE recommends 18-27°C, NVIDIA spec max 35°C but performance degrades above 30°C), airflow management (blanking panels for empty RU, cable management for unobstructed airflow), GPU temperature monitoring (DCGM, alert at 85°C sustained)
C) Just run GPUs at maximum performance — cooling will adapt automatically
D) Underclock all GPUs to reduce heat — performance doesn't matter for AI workloads
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU cooling directly impacts performance (thermal throttling at >85°C) and density (more GPUs per rack = more cooling needed). H100 SXM5 at 700W × 8 GPUs = 5.6kW GPU heat per node, plus CPU/RAM/network = 8-10kW node total. A 42U rack with 4 nodes (4U each) = 32-40kW — exceeds traditional air cooling (20-30kW/rack). Liquid cooling: Direct-to-chip cold plates remove 80-90% of GPU heat at the source, reducing datacenter CRAC/CRAH load by 30-40%, enabling 50-80kW/rack density. Hot/cold aisle containment: prevents hot exhaust recirculating to cold intake (recirculation = GPU inlet temp rises = thermal throttling). Monitor GPU temps via DCGM: sustained >85°C = cooling inadequate.

GPU Cooling Density
Air cooling:  20-30kW/rack (4-5 H100 nodes max)
Liquid cooling: 50-80kW/rack (8-12 H100 nodes)
Hot/cold aisle containment: prevent recirculation → GPU inlet < 25°C
Monitor: GPU temp > 85°C sustained = cooling failure → thermal throttle
AI Ops Q183 of 200
How do you handle a GPU that shows "ERR!" in nvidia-smi or has fallen off the PCIe bus?
A) GPU showing "ERR!" or missing from nvidia-smi = PCIe link failure or GPU hardware failure. Steps: 1) Check dmesg for PCIe errors (AER — Advanced Error Reporting), AER corrected/replay timeouts. 2) `lspci -vvv -s ` — does GPU appear? If not, physical reseat required. 3) Check NVIDIA kernel module: `lsmod | grep nvidia` — reload if needed (`rmmod nvidia_uvm nvidia_drm nvidia_modeset nvidia; modprobe nvidia`). 4) Check PCIe retimers/risers (common failure point in dense GPU servers). 5) If GPU returns after reboot but repeatedly falls off → hardware failure → RMA. Falling off the bus during training = GPU hardware issue (power delivery, thermal, or GPU itself)
B) GPUs showing "ERR!" just need a driver reload — `nvidia-smi -r` fixes all GPU errors
C) GPU falling off the bus is a software issue — update CUDA and it resolves
D) Ignore "ERR!" state — the GPU will self-recover when workload changes
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU "ERR!" state or PCIe bus disappearance is a critical hardware event. PCIe Advanced Error Reporting (AER): `dmesg | grep -i aer` shows corrected/replay timeout/fatal errors. Corrected errors increasing = PCIe link degrading. Uncorrectable/fatal errors = GPU or PCIe infrastructure failure. Check if GPU appears on PCIe bus: `lspci | grep -i nvidia` — if missing, physical issue. PCIe retimers (used on risers for dense GPU servers) are common failure points — if one retimer fails, all GPUs on that riser may disappear. Kernel module reload: `rmmod nvidia_uvm nvidia_drm nvidia_modeset nvidia` then `modprobe nvidia` can recover from driver state corruption. Persistent bus-off issues = RMA the GPU or server motherboard.

GPU PCIe Bus-Off Troubleshooting
nvidia-smi shows ERR!/missing → dmesg | grep AER → PCIe errors?
lspci | grep nvidia → GPU on bus? No → physical reseat/retimer check
Kernel module reload → still missing? → Reboot → still missing? → RMA
Repeated bus-off = hardware issue (GPU, retimer, riser, or motherboard)

Level 4) Application-level: MLPerf benchmarks (BERT, ResNet, GPT-3 style), or representative training mini-run. GPU benchmarking establishes known-good performance baselines — critical for detecting degradation and validating hardware. AI Ops Q185 of 200
What is GPU Direct RDMA (GDR) and how does it benefit distributed training?
A) GDR is the same as GPU Direct Storage — they're interchangeable terms
B) GPU Direct RDMA allows NICs to DMA data directly to/from GPU memory, bypassing CPU and system memory. NCCL uses GDR for allreduce: GPU data → NIC (via PCIe P2P or BAR1 mapping) → network → remote NIC → remote GPU (reverse path). Without GDR: GPU → CPU RAM → NIC → network → remote CPU RAM → remote GPU (2 extra copies, CPU loaded). Benefit: 2-3× reduction in allreduce latency, CPU freed for compute, higher effective network bandwidth. Requires: GDR-compatible NIC (ConnectX-6+), NVIDIA GPU (A100+), nvidia_peermem kernel module
C) GDR only works on DGX systems — it's not available on commodity GPU servers
D) GPU Direct RDMA is deprecated — NCCL now handles all data movement without GDR
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU Direct RDMA (GDR) is the most impactful performance feature for distributed training. Traditional path: GPU buffer → CPU pinned memory (cudaMemcpy) → NIC DMA → network → remote NIC → CPU memory → remote GPU (4 total copy operations, 2 through CPU). GDR path: GPU buffer → NIC (PCIe BAR1 direct access) → network → remote NIC → remote GPU (2 copies, zero CPU). NCCL uses GDR via `NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL` — level 2 (read via BAR1), level 5 (write via BAR1, requires H100+). Enable GDR: load `nvidia-peermem` kernel module, ensure GPU BAR1 size is sufficient (`nvidia-smi -q -d BAR1` — need >32GB for large tensors). Typical improvement: 2-3× faster allreduce, 30-40% reduction in end-to-end training time for large-scale jobs.

GDR Data Path
No GDR: GPU → CPU RAM → NIC → Network → CPU RAM → GPU (4 copies)
GDR:    GPU ──PCIe BAR1──▶ NIC → Network → NIC ──BAR1──▶ GPU (2 copies)
Requires: nvidia-peermem.ko, GDR-capable NIC (CX6+), NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL=2/5
AI Ops Q186 of 200
What is RoCE (RDMA over Converged Ethernet) and how does it compare to InfiniBand for AI clusters?
A) RoCE and InfiniBand are identical — choose based on vendor preference only
B) RoCE is always faster than InfiniBand — it should always be used for AI clusters
C) RoCEv2 encapsulates RDMA in UDP/IP over Ethernet, enabling RDMA on standard Ethernet switches without specialized IB hardware. IB advantages: lower latency (~1μs vs ~2-3μs for RoCE), lossless by design (credit-based flow control), mature ecosystem. RoCE advantages: lower cost (standard Ethernet switches), easier integration with existing DC infrastructure, skilled Ethernet engineers more available. For AI: IB (NDR 400Gb/s) still preferred for large-scale (>256 GPU) training due to in-network computing (SHARP). RoCE (400GbE) viable for smaller clusters, especially with ECN/PFC for lossless operation
D) RoCE requires specialized InfiniBand hardware — it's not compatible with standard Ethernet
✅ Correct Answer: C

RoCEv2 brings RDMA to Ethernet, making it an attractive alternative to InfiniBand. IB: native RDMA, credit-based flow control (lossless by design), very low latency (~1μs), supports SHARP (in-network allreduce). RoCEv2: RDMA over UDP/IP, requires PFC (Priority Flow Control) + ECN (Explicit Congestion Notification) for lossless behavior, slightly higher latency (~2-3μs), no SHARP equivalent (yet). For AI training: IB is preferred at scale (>256 GPUs) because NCCL allreduce is extremely sensitive to tail latency — IB's deterministic latency wins. RoCE is viable for <256 GPU clusters and is often chosen by cloud providers (Azure, AWS) for cost/ecosystem reasons. NCCL configuration: `NCCL_IB_DISABLE=0` (IB), `NCCL_NET=Socket` then RoCE via `NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME`.

IB vs RoCE Comparison
IB NDR400:   400Gb/s, ~1μs latency, credit-based lossless, SHARP, $$
RoCEv2 400GbE: 400Gb/s, ~2-3μs latency, PFC+ECN, no SHARP, $
IB wins: >256 GPU training (latency-sensitive, SHARP)
RoCE wins: <256 GPU, cloud, mixed workload DC
AI Ops Q187 of 200
How do you manage training job checkpointing and what should be checkpointed?
A) Checkpoint includes: model weights (optimizer state, model parameters), training metadata (step/epoch, learning rate schedule), random number generator states (for reproducibility), and data loader position. Strategy: save every N steps (typically every 1000-5000 steps or every N hours), keep last K checkpoints + best validation checkpoints, use async checkpointing (non-blocking I/O) to avoid pausing training, verify checkpoint integrity after save. Storage: high-performance shared filesystem (Lustre, GPFS, NFS-over-RDMA). For large models (>100B params), FSDP/LoRA checkpoint sharding is required
B) Only model weights need to be saved — everything else can be reconstructed
C) Checkpoint at the end of training only — intermediate checkpoints waste storage space
D) Use local NVMe for checkpoints — shared storage is too slow for large model checkpoints
✅ Correct Answer: A

Checkpointing is insurance against GPU failures — without it, a GPU crash after 50 days of training means starting from zero. What to save: model state_dict, optimizer state_dict (Adam moments — often larger than model), LR scheduler state, RNG states (torch, numpy, random — critical for exact reproducibility), data loader position (to resume from correct sample). Frequency: every 1000-5000 steps or every 2-4 hours, whichever comes first. Retention: keep last 5-10 checkpoints + checkpoints at best validation metrics. Async checkpointing: use `torch.save` in a separate thread/process to avoid blocking training — for large models (175B GPT-3 = ~350GB per checkpoint), sync I/O can pause training for minutes. Storage: shared parallel filesystem (Lustre with RDMA) — local NVMe is risky (node loss = checkpoint loss).

Checkpoint Contents
Model weights(state_dict) + Optimizer state(Adam moments) + LR scheduler + RNG states + Data position
Frequency: every 1000-5000 steps or 2-4 hours
Retention: last 5-10 + best validation checkpoints
Async: non-blocking I/O — don't pause training for checkpoints
AI Ops Q188 of 200
What is SHARP (Scalable Hierarchical Aggregation and Reduction Protocol) and when does it help?
A) SHARP is a training optimization technique — it has nothing to do with networking
B) SHARP performs in-network allreduce: data reduction happens inside the InfiniBand switch ASIC, not on the GPU. As data flows through the switch, the switch's SHARP engine aggregates (sums) the data, sending only the final result to each GPU. Benefits: reduces network data volume by 2× (no all-to-all exchange needed), lowers allreduce latency by 30-50%, frees GPU compute (no reduction kernel needed). Requirements: Mellanox IB switch with SHARP capability (Quantum/HDR/NDR), NCCL configured with CollNet or SHARP plugin. Best for: large-scale training (>64 GPUs) with large tensor allreduces
C) SHARP only works on Ethernet networks — it's not available on InfiniBand
D) SHARP is automatically enabled — no configuration is needed to benefit from it
✅ Correct Answer: B

SHARP moves the allreduce computation from GPUs into the network fabric. Traditional allreduce (Ring): GPU1 sends to GPU2, GPU2 reduces, sends to GPU3... N-1 steps. SHARP: all GPUs send to switch, switch ASIC aggregates in-flight, switch sends result back — 1 step. This reduces data movement by 2× (GPUs send once, receive once vs. send+receive multiple times) and frees GPU SMs from running reduction kernels. Configuration: NCCL must use CollNet algorithm (`NCCL_ALGO=CollNet`) or the NCCL SHARP plugin. Check: `nccld-sharp` tool verifies SHARP capability. Limitations: works on InfiniBand only (not RoCE), requires Mellanox Quantum/HDR/NDR switches, all GPUs must connect through SHARP-capable switches.

SHARP In-Network Allreduce
Traditional Ring: GPU1→GPU2→GPU3→...→GPU_N→GPU1 (N steps, Nx data)
SHARP: GPU1↘ GPU2→[SWITCH AGGREGATES]→ GPU1↙ GPU2↙ (1 step, 1x data)
Benefits: 2x less network data, 30-50% lower latency, GPU compute freed
Requires: IB Quantum/HDR/NDR switch, NCCL CollNet/SHARP plugin
AI Ops Q189 of 200
How should you set up monitoring and alerting for GPU cluster network fabric health?
A) Network monitoring isn't needed — if training jobs complete, the network is healthy
B) Just monitor switch uptime — if switches are up, the fabric is healthy
C) Use ping between nodes — if ping works, the GPU fabric is fine
D) IB fabric: monitor port counters (SymbolErrors, LinkDowned, PortRcvErrors, PortXmitDiscard, ExcessiveBufferOverrunErrors) via `perfquery` or UFM (Unified Fabric Manager). Alert on: SymbolErrors > 0 (cable/transceiver issue), LinkDowned transitions (flapping link), non-zero PortRcvErrors (CRC/physical errors), PortXmitDiscard (congestion/packet loss). RoCE/ethernet: monitor PFC pause frames (excessive = congestion), ECN marks, CRC errors. Topology: monitor SM changes, link speed degradation (e.g., HDR downgraded to FDR). Bandwidth: NCCL allreduce benchmark throughput trending
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU fabric health directly determines distributed training performance. IB fabric monitoring: `perfquery -x 1` for port counters — SymbolErrors indicate physical layer issues (dirty fiber, bad transceiver, bent cable), LinkDowned counts indicate flapping (intermittent connection), PortRcvErrors indicate CRC errors (corrupted data on wire), ExcessiveBufferOverrunErrors indicate congestion (switch buffer overflow). UFM (Unified Fabric Manager) provides centralized fabric monitoring and alerting. RoCE monitoring: PFC pause frame counters — rising = congestion triggering flow control, ECN marks — early congestion warning before PFC kicks in. Link speed: verify all links at expected speed (NDR=400Gb/s, HDR=200Gb/s) — a single degraded link creates a bottleneck for all NCCL traffic traversing it. Bandwidth trending: run nightly NCCL allreduce benchmark, alert on >10% throughput drop.

Fabric Health Monitoring
IB: SymbolErrors(physical) LinkDowned(flap) PortRcvErrors(CRC) ExcessBufOverrun(congestion)
RoCE: PFC pause frames(rising=congestion) ECN marks(early warning)
Topology: SM changes, link speed degradation (HDR→FDR = problem)
Nightly: NCCL allreduce benchmark — >10% drop = investigate
AI Ops Q190 of 200
What is the correct strategy for GPU cluster maintenance windows and rolling updates?
A) Establish regular maintenance windows (weekly/monthly), communicate schedule to users. Rolling updates: drain one node → apply updates (driver, firmware, OS patches) → run validation pipeline (DCGM diag L2 + NCCL test) → return to production → proceed to next node. Never update all nodes simultaneously (entire cluster down). Maintain N-1 node availability during updates. For critical security patches: expedite window, notify users of urgent maintenance. Track maintenance history in CMDB. GPU-specific: coordinate firmware updates so all GPUs in training jobs have identical versions
B) Apply updates whenever convenient — no scheduling or user communication needed
C) Reboot all GPU nodes simultaneously to minimize maintenance time — drain the entire cluster at once
D) GPU clusters don't need maintenance — they're designed to run continuously without updates
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU cluster maintenance must balance stability with security/performance updates. Regular cadence: weekly or bi-weekly maintenance windows, e.g., "Tuesday 2-6 AM" — users can plan around it. Rolling update procedure: `scontrol update NodeName=gpu01 State=DRAIN Reason="Maintenance"` (Slurm) or `kubectl cordon gpu01` (K8s). Wait for running jobs to complete or preempt gracefully. Apply updates, reboot if needed, run validation (DCGM diag L2 minimum), uncordon. Proceed node by node. Critical: GPU firmware updates require all GPUs in a training communicator to match — stagger updates so all GPUs in a node are updated together, and consider updating the entire cluster of interconnected nodes in sequence. Validation gate: if a node fails validation, keep it drained and investigate — never return untested nodes.

Rolling Maintenance Procedure
Drain node → Wait for jobs → Apply updates → Reboot → Validate(DCGM+NCCL) → Uncordon
Stagger: node-by-node, never entire cluster simultaneously
Validation gate: DCGM L2 + NCCL test must pass before returning to production
AI Ops Q191 of 200
What is the correct approach to GPU job preemption and graceful shutdown?
A) Kill the job immediately — GPU jobs don't need graceful shutdown since training can restart from any checkpoint
B) Implement preemption signal handling: Slurm sends SIGTERM (with GraceTime), application catches signal → saves checkpoint (model + optimizer + data position) → exits cleanly. K8s: preStop hook with `sleep X; save_checkpoint.sh`. Critical: checkpoint window (Slurm GraceTime or K8s terminationGracePeriodSeconds) must be long enough for checkpoint I/O (large models: 5-15 min). For urgent preemption (node failure), rely on periodic automatic checkpoints (every N steps). Never kill without checkpoint opportunity — training progress loss can be days/weeks
C) GPU jobs should never be preempted — they're too important to interrupt
D) Send SIGKILL directly — this forces the fastest possible checkpoint save before termination
✅ Correct Answer: B

Graceful preemption saves weeks of training progress. Slurm preemption: `GraceTime` parameter specifies seconds between SIGTERM and SIGKILL — set to checkpoint save time + buffer (e.g., 10 minutes for large models). Application must trap SIGTERM: Python `signal.signal(signal.SIGTERM, handler)` → save checkpoint, close files, exit. K8s: `terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 600` in pod spec, `preStop` hook runs `/scripts/save_checkpoint.sh`. Checkpoint I/O sizing: 175B parameter model (GPT-3 scale) ≈ 350GB in FP16, saving to NFS/RDMA at 10 GB/s = 35 seconds. Always combine with periodic automatic checkpointing (every N steps) — if preemption is hard (SIGKILL due to node failure), only lose progress since last checkpoint.

Graceful Preemption
Slurm: SIGTERM(graceTime) → checkpoint save → SIGTERM_ACK → job exit
K8s:  preStop hook → save_checkpoint.sh → SIGTERM → terminationGracePeriod → SIGKILL
Critical: GraceTime/terminationGracePeriod > checkpoint save time (large model: 5-15 min)
Safety net: automatic periodic checkpoints every N steps
AI Ops Q192 of 200
How do you validate GPU cluster readiness after initial deployment?
A) Run a comprehensive acceptance test suite: 1) All GPUs visible (`nvidia-smi` on every node), 2) Driver/CUDA version consistency across all nodes, 3) DCGM diagnostic level 3 on ALL GPUs (extensive test, ~2hr per GPU, parallel per node), 4) GPU Burn (24hr thermal/power stress test), 5) NCCL allreduce performance test across all nodes (validate expected bandwidth: H100 ~450 GB/s intra-node, ~400 GB/s inter-node with GDR), 6) NVLink/NVSwitch health (`nvidia-smi nvlink -s`, verify all links active), 7) IB fabric validation (all links at expected speed, no errors), 8) End-to-end training benchmark (MLPerf or representative workload). Document all results as baseline
B) Run nvidia-smi once — if it shows GPUs, the cluster is ready
C) Cluster validation is the vendor's responsibility — operators just accept the delivered state
D) Run a single training job — if it completes, everything is working correctly
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU cluster acceptance testing catches hardware issues BEFORE users find them. GPU inventory: `pdsh -w gpu[01-20] nvidia-smi --query-gpu=name,uuid,driver_version --format=csv` — verify all expected GPUs present, identical driver versions. DCGM Level 3 per GPU: tests HBM (extensive memory pattern), PCIe stress, NVLink stress, GPU compute — catches marginal hardware that passes Level 1. GPU Burn 24hr: thermal/power stress catches cooling issues, power delivery problems, infant mortality. NCCL performance: `mpirun -np 160 nccl-tests/build/all_reduce_perf -b 8 -e 2G -f 2 -g 8` — compare against expected bandwidth. IB validation: `ibdiagnet` for fabric health, verify all links at NDR400. End-to-end: MLPerf benchmark or 1B-parameter model training for 1000 steps.

Cluster Acceptance Test Suite
GPU inventory(nvidia-smi all nodes) → DCGM L3(all GPUs) → GPU Burn(24hr) → NCCL perf(all nodes) → NVLink check → IB fabric(ibdiagnet) → Training benchmark → Baseline documented
ALL tests must pass before cluster is declared production-ready
AI Ops Q193 of 200
What is the proper way to handle GPU RMAs (Return Merchandise Authorization)?
A) Just ship the GPU back — no documentation or diagnostics needed for RMA
B) RMAs are handled by the vendor automatically — operators don't need to do anything
C) Before RMA: collect evidence — DCGM diagnostic logs (Level 3/4 output), XID error history (`nvidia-smi -q -d XID`), ECC error counts and page retirement status, `nvidia-bug-report.sh` log, GPU serial number and UUID, description of failure symptoms and impact. Drain node, remove GPU (document slot position), initiate RMA with vendor (attach evidence). Track RMA in CMDB (serial, date, reason, replacement serial). After replacement: run full validation (DCGM L3 + GPU Burn) before returning node to production. Keep RMA history for fleet reliability analysis
D) Buy a new GPU — RMA process is too slow for production GPU clusters
✅ Correct Answer: C

Proper GPU RMA process ensures quick replacement and prevents returned GPUs from being rejected for insufficient evidence. Evidence collection: `dcgmi diag -r 3 -g ` output (showing which test failed), XID error log (`nvidia-smi -q -d XID` — critical XIDs like 48, 61, 62 are automatic RMA approvals), ECC error trend (DCGM_FI_DEV_ECC_SBE_VOL_TOTAL showing rapid increase), retired pages (`nvidia-smi -q -d PAGE_RETIREMENT` — double-bit retired pages = confirmed hardware fault), and `nvidia-bug-report.sh` (comprehensive system snapshot). Track: serial number, node slot position, removal date, RMA #, replacement serial, re-installation date. Fleet reliability: track RMA rate per GPU model — high rates may indicate systemic issue (cooling, power, batch defect).

GPU RMA Process
Collect evidence(DCGM L3, XID, ECC, nvidia-bug-report) → Drain node → Remove GPU → Initiate RMA(vendor) → Track in CMDB → Install replacement → Validate(DCGM L3 + GPU Burn) → Return to production
Track fleet RMA rate for reliability analysis
