AI Ops Q195 of 200
How do you troubleshoot GPU memory leaks in long-running AI inference services?
A) GPU memory leaks are impossible — CUDA automatically garbage collects unused memory
B) Monitor GPU memory usage over time via DCGM (DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_USED) or `nvidia-smi` in a loop. Check process-level memory (`nvidia-smi -q -d PIDS`). Use `cuda-memcheck --leak-check full` for CUDA application memory leak detection. PyTorch: `torch.cuda.memory_summary()` shows cached vs. allocated memory, `torch.cuda.empty_cache()` releases cached memory. Common causes: accumulating tensors without freeing (gradient accumulation buffer, growing KV cache for LLM inference), CUDA context not released after client disconnect, Python reference cycles preventing GC. Fix: implement periodic memory trimming, set max sequence length limits, use `with torch.no_grad():` for inference
C) Restart the inference service weekly — this prevents GPU memory leaks from accumulating
D) GPU memory leaks are caused by the GPU driver — update the driver to fix them
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU memory leaks in inference services cause gradual performance degradation and eventual OOM crashes. Detection: plot DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_USED over days — a steady upward slope without decreasing = leak. Per-process: `nvidia-smi -q -d PIDS` shows memory per process. PyTorch diagnostics: `torch.cuda.memory_allocated()` vs `torch.cuda.memory_reserved()` — if allocated grows but reserved doesn't shrink, it's a leak. LLM inference culprit: KV cache grows with sequence length for each concurrent request — implement max sequence length, pre-allocate KV cache, release after request completion. CUDA context leak: each new client connection creates a CUDA context — ensure context is destroyed on disconnect. Mitigation: periodic `torch.cuda.empty_cache()` between batches, set `PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=max_split_size_mb:128` to reduce fragmentation.

GPU Memory Leak Detection
Monitor DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_USED over days → steady increase = leak
nvidia-smi -q -d PIDS → which process?
PyTorch: torch.cuda.memory_summary() → cached vs allocated
LLM: KV cache per request → set max_seq_len, release after request
Fix: periodic empty_cache(), context cleanup, max sequence limits
AI Ops Q196 of 200
What is NVIDIA's MPS (Multi-Process Service) and when should it be used?
A) MPS is the same as MIG — they are identical GPU partitioning technologies
B) MPS is only for graphics rendering — it has no use in AI workloads
C) MPS is deprecated — use MIG for all GPU sharing use cases
D) MPS allows multiple CUDA processes to share a GPU concurrently without partitioning. How: MPS server runs as a daemon, intercepts CUDA calls from multiple processes, serializes kernel launches to run concurrently on the GPU. Best for: multiple small training jobs sharing a GPU where MIG's fixed partitioning is too rigid, hyperparameter tuning (many small runs), batch inference where processes have different arrival times. Drawbacks: less isolation than MIG (one process can impact others), single CUDA context — all processes use same CUDA version. Configure: `nvidia-smi -c EXCLUSIVE_PROCESS` then start `nvidia-cuda-mps-control -d`
✅ Correct Answer: D

MPS bridges the gap between dedicated GPU (one process) and MIG (hardware-partitioned). MPS works by running a server process that intercepts CUDA API calls from multiple client processes, allowing them to share the GPU's compute and memory dynamically. Key difference from MIG: MPS shares the GPU — no fixed partitioning, processes compete for resources dynamically; MIG partitions the GPU — fixed SM/VRAM allocation per instance. MPS use cases: hyperparameter sweeps (100 small runs on 1 GPU), shared development GPU (multiple data scientists running interactive experiments), batch inference with varying arrival rates. Limitations: all MPS clients share one CUDA context (must use same CUDA version), less security isolation (one process can hog GPU). Setup: `nvidia-smi -c 1` (exclusive process), `nvidia-cuda-mps-control -d`.

MPS Architecture
MPS Server daemon → intercepts CUDA calls → serializes → GPU executes concurrently
[Process 1] [Process 2] [Process 3] → MPS → [=====Shared GPU=====]
Vs MIG:   [Instance 1==|Instance 2=|Instance 3=]  Hardware-isolated
MPS: dynamic sharing, flexible   |  MIG: fixed partitioning, secure
AI Ops Q197 of 200
How do you implement automated node drain when GPU errors are detected?
A) Prometheus Alertmanager rules: DCGM_FI_DEV_XID_ERRORS > 0 (critical XID 48+) → webhook to Slurm/K8s automation. Slurm: `scontrol update NodeName=$NODE State=DRAIN Reason="GPU_XID_48"`. K8s: `kubectl cordon $NODE && kubectl taint $NODE nvidia.com/gpu-error=true:NoSchedule`. Alert on: XID >= 48, ECC double-bit error, GPU temp > 95°C (imminent failure), NVLink errors exceeding threshold, GPU falling off PCIe bus. Integrate with incident management (PagerDuty/OpsGenie). Post-drain: auto-run DCGM diagnostic on affected GPU, if confirmed failure → create RMA ticket automatically. Track drained nodes in CMDB
B) GPU errors should trigger an email to the admin — manual intervention is always required
C) Reboot the node on any GPU error — automated reboot handles all GPU issues
D) GPU errors don't need automated response — users will report issues when training fails
✅ Correct Answer: A

Automated GPU error response prevents corrupted training results from silent errors and minimizes downtime. Prometheus alert: `DCGM_FI_DEV_XID_ERRORS{exported_pod=~"nvidia-dcgm-exporter.*"} > 0` with XID label filtering for critical values (>=48). Alertmanager routes to webhook receiver that executes drain script. Slurm drain: `scontrol update NodeName=$node State=DRAIN Reason="GPU_XID_$xid_gpu_$gpu_id"` — drains immediately, no new jobs scheduled. K8s: `kubectl cordon $node` prevents new pods, `kubectl taint` with GPU error label ensures even tolerating pods don't schedule. Post-drain automation: run DCGM diagnostic, if confirmed hardware failure → create service ticket/RMA, if false alarm → un-drain. Track: maintain drained node history for fleet reliability trending.

Automated GPU Error Response
DCGM XID >=48 / ECC DBE / GPU temp >95°C → Prometheus Alert → Alertmanager → Webhook:
Slurm: scontrol update NodeName=$NODE State=DRAIN
K8s: kubectl cordon + GPU error taint
Post-drain: auto DCGM diag → confirmed HW fail? → RMA ticket

For AI operations: UFM's congestion detection is critical — NCCL allreduce creates bursty many-to-many traffic patterns; UFM identifies congestion hotspots that cause tail latency in allreduce, enabling operators to adjust routing or identify faulty links before training jobs fail.

UFM Capabilities

AI Ops Q199 of 200
How should you design disaster recovery for GPU cluster operations?
A) GPU clusters don't need DR — training jobs just restart after any disaster
B) Multi-layer DR: Data tier — model checkpoints replicated to off-site storage (S3/Blob/remote NFS), dataset integrity verified via checksums. Configuration tier — Slurm config, K8s manifests, GPU Operator Helm values in Git with DR site deployment scripts. Infrastructure tier — documented hardware specs, network topology, IP allocations for DR site rebuild. Operations tier — runbooks for DR site activation, defined RTO (Recovery Time Objective: 24-72hrs for GPU clusters) and RPO (Recovery Point Objective: since last checkpoint). Training recovery: resume from latest off-site checkpoint. Regular DR testing: quarterly failover drill to verify DR site GPU cluster readiness
C) Just mirror the entire dataset — training can restart from scratch on any available GPUs
D) GPU cluster DR is identical to generic datacenter DR — no AI-specific considerations needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU cluster DR is critical because training jobs can cost millions in GPU-hours — losing checkpoints means losing that investment. Data tier: all checkpoints replicated to off-site storage (cloud object storage or remote DC); datasets are large (100TB+) — likely already on shared storage with off-site replication. RPO: checkpoint interval (typically 2-4 hours of training). RTO: time to bring up DR cluster, restore configs, resume training from latest checkpoint. DR site: may be a smaller cluster (cost), deployed identically via IaC (Terraform/Ansible for hardware config, GitOps for K8s/Slurm config). Training recovery: resume from checkpoint, verify loss curve continuity. DR testing: quarterly drills — drain production, failover to DR, run NCCL tests + training benchmark, verify results. Don't forget: NVIDIA licenses (vGPU, AI Enterprise) need to be portable to DR site.

GPU Cluster DR Strategy
Checkpoints → off-site (S3/remote NFS) + Configs → Git + Infra specs → documented
RPO: since last checkpoint (2-4 hours) | RTO: 24-72 hours
DR testing: Quarterly failover drill → verify NCCL + training benchmark
AI Ops Q200 of 200
What are the top 5 operational best practices for running a production GPU cluster?
A) 1) Comprehensive monitoring: DCGM metrics → Prometheus → Grafana dashboards with alerting on GPU errors (XID >= 48, ECC DBE, thermal > 85°C), fabric health (IB/RoCE errors), and job metrics. 2) Automated GPU validation: every node runs DCGM L2 + NCCL test after maintenance/reboot before returning to production. 3) Regular maintenance: scheduled windows for driver/firmware updates, rolling node-by-node with validation gating. 4) Checkpoint discipline: automatic periodic checkpointing for all training jobs, checkpoint integrity verification, off-site replication. 5) Capacity management: GPU utilization tracking (target 70-85%), queue depth monitoring, capacity planning with 6-month GPU lead times, cost attribution per team
B) Just run nvidia-smi periodically — that covers all GPU operational needs
C) GPU clusters are self-managing — NVIDIA's software handles all operational concerns automatically
D) The only operational concern is keeping GPUs powered on — everything else is the users' responsibility
✅ Correct Answer: A

These five pillars form the foundation of production GPU cluster operations. 1) Monitoring: you can't fix what you can't see — DCGM provides the telemetry, Prometheus stores it, Grafana visualizes, Alertmanager notifies. Without this, you're flying blind. 2) Validation: automated testing gates prevent faulty GPUs from corrupting training — a $30K GPU that produces wrong results is worse than no GPU. 3) Maintenance: regular, planned maintenance prevents emergency outages. Rolling updates ensure zero-downtime. 4) Checkpoints: GPU failures are inevitable — checkpointing is the only protection against losing weeks of training. Off-site replication protects against datacenter-level failures. 5) Capacity: GPU clusters are expensive — under-utilization wastes millions, over-utilization creates user frustration. Balance through data-driven capacity planning.

5 Pillars of GPU Operations
1. MONITORING: DCGM → Prometheus → Grafana + Alerts (XID, ECC, thermal, fabric)
2. VALIDATION: DCGM L2 + NCCL test gate after every maintenance
3. MAINTENANCE: Rolling updates, scheduled windows, validation-gated
4. CHECKPOINTS: Automatic periodic, integrity-verified, off-site replicated
5. CAPACITY: 70-85% GPU util target, queue depth monitoring, 6mo lead time planning

💡 Why this matters for NCA-AIIO: These five pillars represent the synthesis of everything covered in the NCA-AIIO operations domain. The exam tests your ability to operationalize GPU clusters end-to-end — from monitoring to capacity planning — and these best practices are the framework for answering any operations scenario question.

🔗 Real-world connection (anihpj/jobpost): For the anihpj/jobpost AI image processing platform running on AWS GPU instances: 1) DCGM metrics feed CloudWatch for monitoring, 2) node validation runs in the AMI user-data before joining EKS, 3) GPU driver updates via new AMI + Karpenter node replacement, 4) training checkpoints go to S3 with cross-region replication, 5) capacity managed via Karpenter + GPU instance spot/fleet mix optimization.

🔧 Part 2: 100 Troubleshooting Issues

Real-world Kubernetes failure scenarios — each with symptom, 15 categorized causes (5 most likely + 5 less likely + 5 new cluster), diagnostic commands, solutions, and personal advice. Patterned after the 5+5+5 cause-card format.

100Issues
5Categories
15Causes per Issue

💾 Category 1: Storage Troubleshooting (10 Issues)ST1-ST10

🟢 Storage Issues — PVC, PV, StorageClass, and Mount Failures

Storage is often the FIRST thing to break because it involves multiple components: PVC → StorageClass → Provisioner → PV → Mount. When any link in this chain fails, your pod can't start or your data is at risk.

📌 ST1–ST3: PVC Binding Failures

ST1
💾 CATEGORY 1: STORAGE — Issue ST1
PVC Stuck in Pending State — No PV Bound

🔍 Symptom: kubectl get pvc shows STATUS: Pending indefinitely. kubectl describe pvc shows "no persistent volumes available for this claim." The anihpj-db PostgreSQL pod can't start because its PVC won't bind.

  kubectl get pvc
  NAME         STATUS    VOLUME   CAPACITY   STORAGECLASS   AGE
  anihpj-pvc   Pending                      fast-ssd       5m

  kubectl describe pvc anihpj-pvc
  Events:
    Warning  VolumeBinding  no persistent volumes available for this claim
    Warning  VolumeBinding  storageclass.storage.k8s.io "fast-ssd" not found
🔴5 Most Likely Causes
  1. StorageClass does not exist: The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created. kubectl get sc — if "fast-ssd" is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
  2. No matching PV available: In static provisioning, no PV matches the PVC's storage size, access mode, or selector. kubectl get pv — check capacity and accessModes.
  3. StorageClass provisioner not installed: The CSI driver (e.g., ebs.csi.aws.com) is not deployed. kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep csi — provisioner pods must be Running.
  4. Access mode mismatch: PVC requests ReadWriteMany but only ReadWriteOnce PVs exist. Check kubectl get pv -o yaml | grep -A2 accessModes.
  5. StorageClass has volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer and no pod uses the PVC yet. The PV is only created when a pod actually references the PVC.
🟡5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CSI driver pod CrashLoopBackOff — provisioner can't create volumes
  2. Cloud provider API rate limiting — too many PVCs created simultaneously
  3. PVC selector labels don't match any PV labels
  4. Node where CSI driver runs has taint without toleration
  5. storageClassName: "" (empty string, not null!) — requests a PV with NO StorageClass, but all PVs have one
🟣5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No default StorageClass set: kubectl get sc — look for "(default)". Without it, PVCs without storageClassName won't bind. Set: kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}'
  2. CSI driver not yet installed — common in fresh kubeadm clusters
  3. Cloud credentials not configured for CSI driver to provision volumes
  4. RBAC for CSI driver service account missing
  5. kubelet missing --cloud-provider flag for legacy in-tree provisioners
🔍 What to Look At:
kubectl get sc,pv,pvc --all-namespaces The full storage stack in one view — see StorageClass, PV, and PVC together.
kubectl describe pvc <name> The Events section tells you exactly why binding failed — always read the events first.
🎯 #1 Root Cause: StorageClass does not exist or is misnamed. The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created — the most common CKA exam pitfall.
🔧 How to Solve:
1. Verify StorageClass exists kubectl get sc If the StorageClass referenced by the PVC is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
2. Check provisioner and parameters kubectl describe sc <name> Verify the provisioner is correct and the CSI driver is installed and running.
3. Static provisioning kubectl get pv Create a PV matching the PVC's requested size, accessMode, and selector labels.
4. Dynamic provisioning Install the CSI driver: kubectl apply -f <csi-driver-url>. Verify CSI pods are Running in kube-system.
5. Set default StorageClass kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}' PVCs without an explicit storageClassName will use the default.
💡 Personal Advice:
Always verify StorageClass FIRST. The #1 PVC issue on the CKA exam is a missing or misnamed StorageClass. Run kubectl get sc before writing any PVC YAML.
Empty string is NOT null. storageClassName: "" Requests a PV with NO StorageClass — different from omitting the field entirely. Know this distinction.
CSI driver must be Running. Even with correct StorageClass, if the CSI provisioner pod is CrashLoopBackOff, no volumes will be created.
WaitForFirstConsumer delays binding. If volumeBindingMode is WaitForFirstConsumer, the PV is only created when a pod actually uses the PVC. Don't mistake this for a failure.

💾 Category 1: Storage Lab ScenariosS1-S10

🟢 anihpj/jobpost — Full-Stack Django + PostgreSQL Application

What is anihpj/jobpost? A Django-based job board application where employers post jobs and applicants apply. It consists of a Django web server, PostgreSQL database, and Redis cache. Each scenario deploys a part of this app with intentional bugs — your job is to find and fix them. ⚠️ IMPORTANT: Check the cluster requirement at the top of each scenario before applying!

S1
🧪 SCENARIO S1 — Category 1: Storage PVC Debug

Deploy anihpj PostgreSQL — Fix PVC Stuck in Pending

The Problem: You deploy the anihpj/jobpost PostgreSQL database using a StatefulSet with a PersistentVolumeClaim. The pod stays Pending because the PVC won't bind — the StorageClass name is misspelled as "fast-ssd" but the actual StorageClass is named "fast-ssd-sc". Your job: find the mismatch and fix it so the database starts correctly.

🖥️ CLUSTER: ksw → 1 (kubeadm) — Production-like cluster on node "anih"
1

📋 Deploy anihpj PostgreSQL (CONTAINS THE BUG)

📖 APP STORY: anihpj/jobpost is a Django job board app. Employers post jobs, applicants apply. PostgreSQL stores: users, jobs, applications. Redis caches: job listings, session data. Django serves: REST API at /api, admin at /admin. The database pod won't start because of a StorageClass typo — the entire job board is DOWN.
📁 FILE STRUCTURE for anihpj/jobpost (S1): 
anihpj/
├── 00-namespace.yaml              ← kubectl create ns anihpj
├── 01-storageclass.yaml           ← fast-ssd-sc (default, no-provisioner)
├── 02-persistentvolume.yaml       ← anihpj-db-pv (hostPath, 10Gi)
├── 03-postgres-pvc.yaml           ← PVC with BUG: storageClassName "fast-ssd"
├── 04-postgres-statefulset.yaml   ← StatefulSet with BUG in volumeClaimTemplates
└── 05-postgres-service.yaml       ← ClusterIP Service (port 5432)
BASH — Create the StorageClass + Deploy PostgreSQL with bug 
# ============================================
# SCENARIO S1: PVC Stuck in Pending — StorageClass Mismatch
# ============================================

# 1. CREATE NAMESPACE
kubectl create namespace anihpj

# 2. CREATE THE STORAGECLASS (correct name: fast-ssd-sc)
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
# ├── 03-postgres-pvc.yaml          ← PVC with BUG: storageClassName "fast-ssd"
# ├── 04-postgres-statefulset.yaml  ← StatefulSet with BUG in volumeClaimTemplates
# └── 05-postgres-service.yaml      ← ClusterIP Service (port 5432)
# ============================================

# 1. CREATE NAMESPACE
kubectl create namespace anihpj

# 2. CREATE THE STORAGECLASS (correct name: fast-ssd-sc)
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: storage.k8s.io/v1
kind: StorageClass
metadata:
  name: fast-ssd-sc
  annotations:
    storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class: "true"
provisioner: kubernetes.io/no-provisioner
volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer
EOF

# 3. CREATE THE PV (static provisioning)
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolume
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db-pv
spec:
  capacity:
    storage: 10Gi
  accessModes:
  - ReadWriteOnce
  persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain
  storageClassName: fast-ssd-sc
  hostPath:
    path: /mnt/data/anihpj-db
EOF

# 4. DEPLOY PVC + STATEFULSET (BUG: storageClassName: "fast-ssd")
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db-data
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  accessModes:
  - ReadWriteOnce
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 10Gi
  storageClassName: fast-ssd    # ❌ BUG! Should be "fast-ssd-sc"
---
# StatefulSet — PostgreSQL Database for anihpj/jobpost
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  serviceName: anihpj-db
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj-db
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj-db
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: postgres
        image: postgres:15-alpine
        env:
        - name: POSTGRES_DB
          value: jobpost
        - name: POSTGRES_USER
          value: anihpj
        - name: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
          value: secret123
        ports:
        - containerPort: 5432
        volumeMounts:
        - name: db-data
          mountPath: /var/lib/postgresql/data
  volumeClaimTemplates:
  - metadata:
      name: db-data
    spec:
      accessModes:
      - ReadWriteOnce
      resources:
        requests:
          storage: 10Gi
      storageClassName: fast-ssd  # ❌ BUG! Same typo here
---
# Service — ClusterIP for PostgreSQL (port 5432)
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    app: anihpj-db
  ports:
  - port: 5432
    targetPort: 5432
EOF
⚠

⚠️ Observe the Error — Spot What's Broken

✗1. Check pods: kubectl get pods -n anihpj
✗2. Check PVC: kubectl get pvc -n anihpj → STATUS: Pending
✗3. Check pod events: kubectl describe pod -n anihpj anihpj-db-0 → "persistentvolumeclaim not found" or "pod has unbound PVC"
✗4. The database pod cannot start — job board is DOWN!
🔍

🔍 Debug — Find the Root Cause

1Describe the PVC: kubectl describe pvc -n anihpj anihpj-db-data
→ Events: "storageclass.storage.k8s.io 'fast-ssd' not found"
2Check available StorageClasses: kubectl get sc
→ Only "fast-ssd-sc" exists (with (default) annotation)
3Check PVC storageClassName: kubectl get pvc -n anihpj -o yaml | grep storageClassName
→ storageClassName: fast-ssd (WRONG!)
4Compare: PVC says fast-ssd — StorageClass is fast-ssd-sc
5Root cause: Typo in storageClassName — "fast-ssd" instead of "fast-ssd-sc"
🧠 Diagnostic Tenet (Thought Process)
①
Pod Pending → Check Events
②
PVC not binding → Check SC name
③
kubectl get sc → "fast-ssd-sc" exists
④
PVC says "fast-ssd" → MISMATCH!
⑤
Delete StatefulSet → Recreate → Pod starts → DB ready

Tenet: When a PVC stays Pending, the #1 cause is a StorageClass name mismatch. The StorageClass must exist with the EXACT name specified. On a StatefulSet, volumeClaimTemplates are IMMUTABLE — you must DELETE the StatefulSet and recreate it with the corrected StorageClass. Never try to patch or edit a running StatefulSet's PVC template. On the CKA exam, "PVC stuck Pending due to StorageClass typo" is one of the most common tasks.

📟 Command Outputs — ERROR State (BEFORE fix)
$ kubectl get pods -n anihpj NAME READY STATUS RESTARTS AGE anihpj-db-0 0/1 Pending 0 2m
$ kubectl describe pod anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj | tail -5 Events: Warning FailedScheduling pod has unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaims Warning FailedScheduling persistentvolumeclaim "db-data-anihpj-db-0" not found
$ kubectl get pvc -n anihpj NAME STATUS VOLUME CAPACITY STORAGECLASS db-data-anihpj-db-0 Pending fast-ssd ← Wrong SC!
$ kubectl get sc NAME PROVISIONER RECLAIMPOLICY VOLUMEBINDINGMODE fast-ssd-sc (default) kubernetes.io/no-provisioner Retain WaitForFirstConsumer
📟 Command Outputs — AFTER Fix
$ kubectl get pvc -n anihpj NAME STATUS VOLUME CAPACITY STORAGECLASS db-data-anihpj-db-0 Bound anihpj-db-pv 10Gi fast-ssd-sc ✅
$ kubectl get pods -n anihpj NAME READY STATUS RESTARTS AGE anihpj-db-0 1/1 Running 0 30s
$ kubectl exec -n anihpj anihpj-db-0 -- psql -U anihpj -d jobpost -c "SELECT 1;" ?column? ---------- 1 (1 row) ✅ Database is operational!
🔧
BASH — Delete and recreate (volumeClaimTemplates are immutable) 
# Step 1: Delete the broken StatefulSet (volumeClaimTemplates are IMMUTABLE — can't edit)
# Also delete the PVCs it created so the new StatefulSet starts fresh
kubectl delete statefulset anihpj-db -n anihpj
kubectl delete pvc db-data-anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj 2>/dev/null

# Step 2: Recreate StatefulSet with CORRECT storageClassName in volumeClaimTemplates
# ⚠️ The volumeClaimTemplates auto-generates PVC named db-data-anihpj-db-0
# You do NOT need a separate PVC — the StatefulSet creates it automatically
cat << 'EOF' | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  serviceName: anihpj-db
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj-db
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj-db
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: postgres
        image: postgres:15-alpine
        env:
        - name: POSTGRES_DB
          value: jobpost
        - name: POSTGRES_USER
          value: anihpj
        - name: POSTGRES_PASSWORD
          value: secret123
        ports:
        - containerPort: 5432
        volumeMounts:
        - name: db-data
          mountPath: /var/lib/postgresql/data
  volumeClaimTemplates:
  - metadata:
      name: db-data
    spec:
      accessModes:
      - ReadWriteOnce
      resources:
        requests:
          storage: 10Gi
      storageClassName: fast-ssd-sc  # ✅ FIXED!
---
# Service — ClusterIP for anihpj-db
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    app: anihpj-db
  ports:
  - port: 5432
    targetPort: 5432
EOF

# Step 3: Wait for pod to be Ready
kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready pod/anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj --timeout=120s
✓

✅ Verify — Database Is Running

After deleting and recreating the StatefulSet with the corrected storageClassName: fast-ssd-sc, the volumeClaimTemplate auto-generates PVC db-data-anihpj-db-0. It binds to the PV, PostgreSQL initializes, and the job board database is ready. kubectl get pods -n anihpj shows anihpj-db-0 Running 1/1 and kubectl get pvc -n anihpj shows db-data-anihpj-db-0 STATUS: Bound.

💡 Key takeaway: StatefulSet volumeClaimTemplates are immutable — you cannot patch or edit them on a running StatefulSet. The only fix is to delete and recreate. The template auto-generates PVCs named <template-name>-<statefulset-name>-<ordinal>. You never need to create PVCs manually for a StatefulSet.

🧹
BASH — copy & paste to clean up 
# Delete namespace — cascades everything inside
kubectl delete namespace anihpj

# Delete the PV (not namespaced)
kubectl delete pv anihpj-db-pv

# Delete the StorageClass
kubectl delete sc fast-ssd-sc

# Verify cleanup
kubectl get all,pvc,pv,sc -n anihpj 2>/dev/null
kubectl get ns anihpj 2>/dev/null

🌳 Decision Trees — Systematic TroubleshootingDT1–DT15

Decision trees provide a step-by-step flowchart to diagnose common Kubernetes issues. Each node is a question — answer YES or NO to navigate to the next step. Follow the path to find the root cause and fix.

DT1
🌳 DECISION TREE DT1 — Pod Stuck Pending

Pod Stuck in Pending — Systematic Diagnosis Flowchart

The Problem: A pod is stuck in Pending state and won't schedule. This is the #1 most common Kubernetes issue. The scheduler can't place the pod on any node. Your job: follow this decision tree to find WHY and fix it.

🖥️ CLUSTER: ksw → 1 (kubeadm) — Any cluster
▶

🚦 START — Pod Is Pending

📖 APP STORY: The anihpj-api pod was deployed 20 minutes ago. It's still Pending — users can't access the job board. Is it resources? nodeSelector? Taints? PVC? Follow the tree to find out.
kubectl get pods -n anihpj

🌿 NODE 1 — What does kubectl describe show?

# Run this command first:
kubectl describe pod -n anihpj <pod-name> | tail -20



    
Events Section — What Does It Show?

    
    
        
  • "0/N nodes available"
    → Go to NODE 2
    • 
        
  • "pod has unbound PVC"
    → Go to NODE 3
    • 
        
  • "node(s) had taint"
    → Go to NODE 4
    • 
        
  • "insufficient cpu/memory"
    → Go to NODE 5
    • 
        
  • "didn't match selector"
    → Go to NODE 6
    • 
        
  • No events / empty
    → Check scheduler logs
    •
N2

🔴 NODE 2 — "0/N nodes are available"

                    

                        

                            

                            

                                
Check node status

                                kubectl get nodes
                            

                        

                    




    
Node Availability — Are ALL Nodes Ready?

    
    
        
  • YES → Check if node is cordoned:
    kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{.items[*].spec.unschedulable}'
    If true → Node is cordoned! Uncordon it.
    • 
        
  • NO → Some nodes NotReady → Check conditions:
    kubectl describe node <node> | grep Conditions
    MemoryPressure/DiskPressure/PIDPressure?
    • 
        
  • SSH to node — check kubelet:
    systemctl status kubelet
    journalctl -u kubelet -n 50
    • 
    






    
Restart kubelet on broken node

    kubectl uncordon <node-name>
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
N3

🔴 NODE 3 — "pod has unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaims"

                    

                        

                            

                            

                                
Check PVC status

                                kubectl get pvc -n anihpj
                            

                        

                    




    
PVC Diagnosis — Why Is It Stuck?

    
    
        
  • "no persistent volumes available"?
    kubectl get pv — check if any PV matches
    StorageClass mismatch? PV capacity < PVC request? accessMode mismatch?
    • 
        
  • "storageclass not found"?
    kubectl get sc — does the SC exist?
    Provisioner broken? Check CSI driver pods
    • 
        
  • VolumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer?
    PVC stays Pending until a Pod uses it — this is NORMAL, deploy the pod first
    • 
    






    
Fix StorageClass name in PVC

    kubectl apply -f pv.yaml
kubectl patch pvc <name> -n anihpj --type=json \
-p='[{"op":"replace","path":"/spec/storageClassName","value":"correct-sc"}]'
N4

🔴 NODE 4 — "node(s) had taint that the pod didn't tolerate"

Check node taints
kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{"\t"}{.spec.taints}{"\n"}{end}'
Check pod tolerations
kubectl get pod -n anihpj <pod> -o yaml | grep -A5 tolerations
Taint Fix Options
  • Option 1: Add toleration to pod spec
    Patch the Deployment to tolerate the taint
  • Option 2: Remove taint from node (if safe)
    kubectl taint node <node> <key>-
    Only do this if other workloads SHOULD run on this node
Add toleration to pod (patch deployment)
kubectl taint node <node> node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane:NoSchedule- kubectl patch deploy <name> -n anihpj --type=json -p='[ {"op":"add","path":"/spec/template/spec/tolerations","value":[{"key":"key","operator":"Equal","value":"val","effect":"NoSchedule"}]} ]'
N5

🔴 NODE 5 — "insufficient cpu/memory"

                    

                        

                            

                            

                                
Check node capacity

                                kubectl describe nodes | grep -A5 "Allocated resources"
                                kubectl top nodes
                            

                        

                    




    
Resource Fix Options

    
    
        
  • Reduce pod resource requests
    Lower CPU/memory requests to fit available capacity
    • 
        
  • Add more nodes to the cluster
    Scale horizontally if this is a persistent need
    • 
        
  • Remove old pods to free resources
    kubectl delete pod <old-pod>
    • 
        
  • Use Cluster Autoscaler (cloud only)
    • 
    






    
Lower CPU request

    kubectl patch deploy <name> -n anihpj --type=json -p='[
{"op":"replace","path":"/spec/template/spec/containers/0/resources/requests/cpu","value":"100m"}
]'
N6

🔴 NODE 6 — "didn't match Pod's node affinity/selector"

Check pod's node selector
kubectl get pod -n anihpj <pod> -o yaml | grep -A5 nodeSelector
Check pod's node affinity
kubectl get pod -n anihpj <pod> -o yaml | grep -A20 affinity
Check node labels
kubectl get nodes --show-labels
Selector/Affinity Diagnosis
  • Does any node have the required label?
    kubectl get nodes --show-labels
    If NO → Label a node: kubectl label node <node> key=value
  • Is podAntiAffinity blocking?
    Other pods with same label on that node? If maxSkew=0, odd pods ÷ zones = deadlock
    Increase maxSkew or reduce replicas
Remove impossible nodeSelector
kubectl label node <node> disktype=ssd kubectl patch deploy <name> -n anihpj --type=json -p='[ {"op":"remove","path":"/spec/template/spec/nodeSelector/disktype"} ]'
✓

✅ Decision Tree Complete — Pod Pending Resolution

Quick reference:
kubectl describe pod → Read Events → Follow the path.
N2 = Nodes not available · N3 = PVC not bound · N4 = Taints · N5 = No resources · N6 = Selector mismatch
Pro tip: 90% of Pending pods are N4 (taints) or N6 (selectors). Start there.

📋 Appendix A: Answer KeyMCQ Answers

Quick-reference answer key for all 200 multiple-choice questions across the 5 CKA categories.

Category 1: Storage (10% — Q1-Q20)
  • Q1-Q4: Storage fundamentals — emptyDir vs PV, container filesystem layers, volume/volumeMount
  • Q5-Q10: StorageClass + Dynamic Provisioning — provisioners, reclaimPolicy, WaitForFirstConsumer
  • Q11-Q15: PV/PVC Lifecycle — binding, accessModes, capacity matching, hostPath vs CSI
  • Q16-Q20: ConfigMap/Secret volumes, subPath, fsGroup, ResourceQuota for storage
Category 2: Troubleshooting (30% — Q21-Q80)
  • Q21-Q30: Node-level debugging — NotReady, MemoryPressure, DiskPressure, kubelet
  • Q31-Q40: Pod-level debugging — CrashLoopBackOff, ImagePullBackOff, OOMKilled, exit codes
  • Q41-Q50: Networking debugging — Service endpoints, DNS, NetworkPolicy, kube-proxy
  • Q51-Q60: Cluster debugging — etcd, certificates, CNI, API server, control plane
  • Q61-Q70: Application debugging — kubectl exec/logs, init containers, probes, finalizers
  • Q71-Q80: Storage debugging — PVC pending, volume mount errors, CSI driver issues
Category 3: Workloads & Scheduling (15% — Q81-Q110)
  • Q81-Q90: Deployments — RollingUpdate, Rollback, pause/resume, revision history
  • Q91-Q100: Scheduling — nodeSelector, affinity/antiAffinity, taints/tolerations, topologySpread
  • Q101-Q110: Scaling — HPA, PDB, ResourceQuota, LimitRange, Jobs, CronJobs, DaemonSets
Category 4: Services & Networking (20% — Q111-Q150)
  • Q111-Q120: Services — ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer, ExternalName, EndpointSlices
  • Q121-Q130: Ingress & Gateway API — TLS, path routing, backendRef, traffic splitting
  • Q131-Q140: NetworkPolicy — ingress/egress rules, podSelector, namespaceSelector, default-deny
  • Q141-Q150: DNS & Service Discovery — CoreDNS, resolv.conf, kube-proxy modes (iptables/ipvs)
Category 5: Cluster Architecture (25% — Q151-Q200)
  • Q151-Q165: Cluster setup — kubeadm init/join/upgrade, node management, static pods
  • Q166-Q175: etcd — backup/restore, snapshot, compaction, defrag, RAFT consensus
  • Q176-Q185: RBAC — ServiceAccount, Role, ClusterRole, RoleBinding, certificate signing
  • Q186-Q200: Architecture — API server, scheduler, controller-manager, container runtimes, kubelet config

⚡ Appendix M: CKA Exam Day — 20 Command Patterns You MUST KnowCRAM SHEET

🏆 Master Summary — How to Use These 20 Patterns

This appendix is your last-30-minutes-before-exam cram sheet. Every pattern here has appeared on real CKA exams. They are organized by domain weight — start with Troubleshooting (30%) and Cluster Architecture (25%) patterns first, as they make up 55% of your score.

📖
Step 1: Read all 20 patterns once (15 min)
✍️
Step 2: Type M.1, M.2, M.6, M.10 from memory (10 min)
✅
Step 3: Verify each command & take the exam (5 min buffer)
🎯 Implementation Strategy — How Each Pattern Maps to Exam Tasks
  • M.1–M.2 (etcd + kubeadm upgrade): These are guaranteed 2 tasks. etcd backup/restore is worth heavy points. kubeadm upgrade sequence must be exact. Type these from memory 5 times before exam.
  • M.3, M.13, M.17 (RBAC + SA + CSR): Security tasks appear in every CKA exam. Know RBAC imperatives cold. ServiceAccount + RoleBinding is a common combo task.
  • M.4, M.5, M.19 (Debug + Logs + Monitoring): Troubleshooting is 30% of your score. The debug pod pattern (kubectl run --rm -it) and log flags (--tail --since --previous) are your primary diagnostic tools.
  • M.6, M.10 (Imperative YAML + Rollouts): Your #1 speed hack. Generate YAML with --dry-run=client -o yaml instead of typing 20-line manifests. Saves 30+ minutes across the exam.
  • M.7, M.11 (Node maintenance + Static pods): Cordon → drain → work → uncordon is tested on most exams. Static pod manifests in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ — remember: delete the FILE, not the pod.
  • M.8, M.17 (TLS + Certificates): TLS Ingress with kubernetes.io/tls Secret type. Certificate approval for joining nodes. Know: kubeadm certs check-expiration and kubectl certificate approve.
  • M.12, M.14 (NetworkPolicy + CronJob): NetworkPolicy is a frequent task — create a policy targeting specific pods with correct Ingress/Egress direction. CronJob: 5-field syntax, suspend/resume, manual trigger.
  • M.15, M.16, M.18, M.20 (Safe apply + JSONPath + Context + etcdctl): These are your "finishing touches" patterns. kubectl diff before applying. JSONPath for extracting specific fields. Context switching to avoid managing the wrong cluster. etcdctl deep dive for troubleshooting.

These are the 20 command patterns that appear on EVERY CKA exam. Memorize them cold. Practice typing them from memory — the exam has no autocomplete beyond kubectl built-in completion. Read this appendix in the 30 minutes before your exam.

M.1 — etcd Backup & Restore (Full TLS Flags)
1

Backup:

ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
  --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
  --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  snapshot save /opt/etcd-backup.db

Restore:

ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restore \
  snapshot restore /opt/etcd-backup.db

⚠️ After restore: Update etcd static pod manifest (/etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml) to point --data-dir to the new path, then restart kubelet. Also update the --initial-cluster-token to a new value.

M.2 — kubeadm Cluster Upgrade Sequence
2

On Control Plane:

# 1. Unhold and upgrade kubeadm

apt-mark unhold kubeadm && apt-get update && apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.30.x-*
apt-mark hold kubeadm
# 2. Verify plan
kubeadm upgrade plan
# 3. Apply upgrade
kubeadm upgrade apply v1.30.x
# 4. Upgrade kubelet & kubectl
apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl && apt-get install -y kubelet=1.30.x-* kubectl=1.30.x-*
apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl
systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl restart kubelet

On Each Worker:

# 1. Drain the worker first (from CP)

kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
# 2. SSH to worker, upgrade kubeadm + kubelet
apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.30.x-* kubelet=1.30.x-*
kubeadm upgrade node
systemctl restart kubelet
# 3. Uncordon
kubectl uncordon worker-1
M.3 — RBAC: auth can-i, Impersonation & Dry-Run
3
# Test if user can do something

kubectl auth can-i create pods --as=alice -n dev
kubectl auth can-i delete secrets --as=system:serviceaccount:dev:api-sa -n dev

# List ALL permissions for a user
kubectl auth can-i --list --as=alice -n dev

# Create RBAC imperatively (FAST — use on exam!)
kubectl create role pod-reader --verb=get,list,watch --resource=pods -n dev
kubectl create rolebinding alice-pod --role=pod-reader --user=alice -n dev
kubectl create clusterrole secret-reader --verb=get,list --resource=secrets
kubectl create clusterrolebinding alice-secret --clusterrole=secret-reader --user=alice

# For ServiceAccounts
kubectl create rolebinding sa-binding --role=pod-reader --serviceaccount=dev:api-sa -n dev
M.4 — Debug Pod & Network Testing
4
# Temporary debug pod (auto-deletes on exit)

kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox --restart=Never -- sh

# Test DNS from debug pod
kubectl exec deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj -- nslookup kubernetes.default
kubectl exec deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj -- nslookup -type=SRV _port._tcp.svc.ns

# Test connectivity
kubectl run curl-test --rm -it --image=curlimages/curl --restart=Never -- \
  curl -v http://svc-name.ns.svc.cluster.local:8080

# Port forward for local debugging
kubectl port-forward svc/anihpj-api 8080:80 -n anihpj
kubectl port-forward pod/anihpj-api-7d8f9 5432:5432 -n anihpj

# Copy files to/from pods
kubectl cp anihpj/config.yaml pod/name:/etc/config.yaml -n anihpj
kubectl cp pod/name:/var/log/app.log ./app.log -n anihpj
M.5 — Logs: --tail, --since, --timestamps, --previous
5
# Logs from a specific container in a multi-container pod

kubectl logs pod/name -c sidecar -n anihpj

# Last N lines
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --tail=50 -n anihpj

# Logs since a relative time
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --since=10m -n anihpj
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --since=1h -n anihpj

# Logs with timestamps
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --timestamps -n anihpj

# Logs from PREVIOUS (crashed) container
kubectl logs pod/name --previous -n anihpj

# Follow logs in real-time
kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api --follow -n anihpj

# Combined: last 100 lines from previous container with timestamps
kubectl logs pod/name --previous --tail=100 --timestamps -n anihpj
🏆 EXAM DAY SPEED TIPS
  • Alias kubectl: alias k=kubectl; complete -F __start_kubectl k — saves 30+ keystrokes per command
  • Use imperative + dry-run: Generate YAML in 2 seconds instead of writing 20 lines from scratch
  • Verify context FIRST: Every task starts with kubectl config use-context <given-context>
  • Use kubectl explain: Forget a field? kubectl explain pod.spec --recursive
  • Copy-paste carefully: The exam terminal supports copy-paste but verify after pasting
  • Flag order matters: kubectl logs pod-name -n ns --tail=10 NOT kubectl logs --tail=10 pod-name
  • Weighted scoring: Troubleshooting (30%) > Cluster Architecture (25%) = 55% of your score. Prioritize these!
  • Leave no task blank: Partial solutions get partial credit. Even creating the namespace and YAML file earns points.
